[Narrator] A Last Chronicle of Carlingford. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER I. THE PASTOR'S PROGRESS. [/]
[Narrator] Miss Phoebe Tozer, the only daughter of the chief deacon and leading member of the Dissenting connection in Carlingford, married, shortly after his appointment to the charge of Salem Chapel, in that town, the Reverend Mr. Beecham, one of the most rising young men in the denomination. [/]
[Narrator] The marriage was in many ways satisfactory to the young lady's family, for Mr. Beecham was himself the son of respectable people in a good way of business, and not destitute of means; and the position was one which they had always felt most suitable for their daughter, and to which she had been almost, it may be said, brought up. [/]
[Narrator] It is, however, scarcely necessary to add that it was not quite so agreeable to the other leading members of the congregation. [/]
[Narrator] I should be very sorry to say that each family wished that preferment for its own favourite daughter; for indeed there can be no doubt, as Mrs. Pigeon asserted vigorously, that a substantial grocer, whose father before him had established an excellent business, and who had paid for his pew in Salem as long as any one could recollect, and supported every charity, and paid up on all occasions when extra expense was necessary, was in every way a more desirable son - in - law than a poor minister who was always dependent on pleasing the chapel folks, and might have to turn out any day. [/]
[Narrator] Notwithstanding, however, the evident superiority of the establishment thus attained by Maria Pigeon, there is a certain something attached to the position of a clerical caste, even among such an independent body as the congregation at Salem Chapel, which has its own especial charms, and neither the young people who had been her companions nor the old people who had patronized and snubbed her, felt any satisfaction in seeing Phoebe thus advanced over them to the honours and glories inalienable from the position of minister's wife. [/]
[Narrator] All her little airs of bridal vanity were considered as so many offensive manifestations of delight and exultation in her rise in life. [/]
[Narrator] Her _ trousseau _, though pronounced by all competent judges not half so abundant or fine as Maria Pigeon's, still called forth comments which nobody ventured to indulge in, in respect to the grocer's blooming bride. [/]
[Narrator] A grocer's lady has a right to anything her parents can afford; but to see a minister's wife swelling herself up, and trying to ape the quality, filled the town with virtuous indignation. [/]
[MrsRider] The sight of young Mrs. Beecham walking about with her card - case in her hand, calling on the Miss Hemmingses, shaking hands with Mrs. Rider the doctor's wife, caused unmitigated disgust throughout all the back streets of Carlingford; and"_ that _ [/]
[MrsRider] Phoebe a - sweeping in as if the chapel belonged to her,"was almost more than the oldest sitter could bear. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe, it must be added, felt her elevation to the full, and did not spare her congregation. [/]
[Narrator] Sometimes she would have the audacity to walk from the vestry to the pew, as if she were an office - bearer, instead of coming in humbly by the door as became a woman. [/]
[Narrator] She would sit still ostentatiously until every one had gone, waiting for her husband. [/]
[Narrator] She quite led the singing, everybody remarked, paying no more attention to the choir than if it did not exist; and once she had even paused on her way to her seat, and turned down the gas, which was blazing too high, with an air of proprietorship that nobody could endure. [/]
[MrsBrown] "Does Salem belong to them Tozers, I should like to know?"said Mrs. Brown. [/]
[MrsBrown] "Brown would never be outdone by him in subscriptions you may be sure, nor Mr. Pigeon neither, if the truth was known. [/]
[MrsBrown] I never gave my money to build a castle for the Tozers." [/]
[Everybody] Thus the whole congregation expressed itself with more or less eloquence, and though the attendance never diminished, everybody being too anxious to see"what they would do next,"the feeling could not be ignored. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe herself, with a courage which developed from the moment of her marriage, took the initiative. [/]
[Narrator] "It never answers,"she said, solemnly,"to marry one of the flock; I knew it, Henery, and I told you so; and if you would be so infatuated, and marry me when I told you not, for your own interests--""They're all jealous of you, my pet, that's what it is,"said Mr. Beecham, and laughed. [/]
[Narrator] He could bear the annoyance in consideration of that sweet consciousness of its cause which stole over all his being. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe laughed, too, but not with so delicious a gratification. [/]
[Narrator] She felt that there were people, even in Salem, who might be jealous of him. [/]
[Phoebe] "The end of it all is, we must not stay here,"she said. [/]
[Phoebe] "You must find another sphere for your talents, Henery, and I'm sure it will not be difficult. [/]
[Phoebe] If you get put on that deputation that is going down to the North, suppose you take a few of your best sermons, dear. [/]
[Phoebe] That can never do any harm -- indeed it's sure to do good, to some poor benighted soul at least, that perhaps never heard the truth before. [/]
[Phoebe] And likewise, perhaps, to some vacant congregation. [/]
[Phoebe] I have always heard that chapels in the North were very superior to here. [/]
[Phoebe] A different class of society, and better altogether. [/]
[Phoebe] These Pigeons and Browns, and people are not the sort of society for you. [/]
[Narrator] ""Well, there's truth in that,"said Mr. Beecham, pulling up his shirt - collar. [/]
[MrBeecham] "Certainly it is n't the sort of thing one was accustomed to." [/]
[Narrator] And he lent a serious ear to the suggestion about the sermons. [/]
[Narrator] The consequence was that an invitation followed from a chapel in the North, where indeed Mrs. Phoebe found herself in much finer society, and grew rapidly in importance and in ideas. [/]
[Narrator] After this favourable start, the process went on for many years by which a young man from Homerton was then developed into the influential and highly esteemed pastor of an important flock. [/]
[Narrator] Things may be, and probably are, differently managed now - a - days. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Beecham had unbounded fluency and an unctuous manner of treating his subjects. [/]
[Narrator] It was eloquence of a kind, though not of an elevated kind. [/]
[Narrator] Never to be at a loss for what you have to say is a prodigious advantage to all men in all positions, but doubly so to a popular minister. [/]
[Narrator] He had an unbounded wealth of phraseology. [/]
[Narrator] Sentences seemed to melt out of his mouth without any apparent effort, all set in a certain cadence. [/]
[Narrator] He had not, perhaps, much power of thought, but it is easy to make up for such a secondary want when the gift of expression is so strong. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Beecham rose, like an actor, from a long and successful career in the provinces, to what might be called the Surrey side of congregational eminence in London; and from thence attained his final apotheosis in a handsome chapel near Regent's Park, built of the whitest stone, and cushioned with the reddest damask, where a very large congregation sat in great comfort and listened to his sermons with a satisfaction no doubt increased by the fact that the cushions were soft as their own easy - chairs, and that carpets and hot - water pipes kept everything snug under foot. [/]
[Narrator] It was the most comfortable chapel in the whole connection. [/]
[Narrator] The seats were arranged like those of an amphitheatre, each line on a slightly higher level than the one in front of it, so that everybody saw everything that was going on. [/]
[Narrator] No dimness or mystery was wanted there; everything was bright daylight, bright paint, red cushions, comfort and respectability. [/]
[Narrator] It might not be a place very well adapted for saying your prayers in, but then you could say your prayers at home -- and it was a place admirably adapted for hearing sermons in, which you could not do at home; and all the arrangements were such that you could hear in the greatest comfort, not to say luxury. [/]
[Narrator] I wonder, for my own part, that the poor folk about did not seize upon the Crescent Chapel on the cold Sunday mornings, and make themselves happy in those warm and ruddy pews. [/]
[Narrator] It would be a little amusing to speculate what all the well - dressed pew - holders would have done had this unexpected answer to the appeal which Mr. Beecham believed himself to make every Sunday to the world in general, been literally given. [/]
[Narrator] It would have been extremely embarrassing to the Managing Committee and all the office - bearers, and would have, I fear, deeply exasperated and offended the occupants of those family pews; but fortunately this difficulty never did occur. [/]
[Narrator] The proletariat of Marylebone had not the sense or the courage, or the profanity, which you will, to hit upon this mode of warming themselves. [/]
[Narrator] The real congregation embraced none of the unwashed multitude. [/]
[Narrator] Its value in mere velvet, silk, lace, trinkets, and furs was something amazing, and the amount these comfortable people represented in the way of income would have amounted to a most princely revenue. [/]
[Narrator] The little Salems and Bethesdas, with their humble flocks, could not be supposed to belong to the same species; and the difference was almost equally marked between such a place of worship as the Crescent Chapel and the parish churches, which are like the nets in the Gospel, and take in all kinds of fish, bad and good. [/]
[Narrator] The pew - holders in the Crescent Chapel were universally well off; they subscribed liberally to missionary societies, far more liberally than the people in St. Paul's close by did to the S. P. G. They had everything of the best in the chapel, as they had in their houses. [/]
[Narrator] They no more economized on their minister than they did on their pew - cushions, and they spent an amount of money on their choir which made the singing - people at St. Paul's gnash their teeth. [/]
[Narrator] From all this it will be seen that the atmosphere of the Crescent Chapel was of a very distinct and individual kind. [/]
[Narrator] It was a warm, luxurious air, perfumy, breathing of that refinement which is possible to mere wealth. [/]
[Narrator] I do not say there might not be true refinement besides, but the surface kind, that which you buy from upholsterers and tailors and dressmakers, which you procure ready made at schools, and which can only be kept up at a very high cost, abounded and pervaded the place. [/]
[Narrator] Badly dressed people felt themselves out of place in that brilliant sanctuary; a muddy footprint upon the thick matting in the passages was looked at as a crime. [/]
[Narrator] Clean dry feet issuing out of carriage or cab kept the aisles unstained, even on the wettest day. [/]
[Narrator] We say cab, because many of the people who went to the Crescent Chapel objected to take out their own carriages or work their own horses on Sunday; and there were many more who, though they did not possess carriages, used cabs with a freedom incompatible with poverty. [/]
[Narrator] As a general rule, they were much better off than the people at St. Paul's, more universally prosperous and well - to - do. [/]
[Narrator] And they were at the same time what you might safely call well - informed people -- people who read the newspapers, and sometimes the magazines, and knew what was going on. [/]
[WellInformedPeople] The men were almost all liberal in politics, and believed in Mr. Gladstone with enthusiasm; the women often"took an interest"in public movements, especially of a charitable character. [/]
[Narrator] There was less mental stagnation among them probably than among many of their neighbours. [/]
[Narrator] Their life was not profound nor high, but still it was life after a sort. [/]
[Narrator] Such was the flock which had invited Mr. Beecham to become their pastor when he reached the climax of his career. [/]
[Narrator] They gave him a very good salary, enough to enable him to have a handsome house in one of the terraces overlooking Regent's Park. [/]
[Narrator] It is not a fashionable quarter, but it is not to be despised in any way. [/]
[Narrator] The rooms were good - sized and lofty, and sometimes have been known to suffice for very fine people indeed, a fact which the Beechams were well aware of; and they were not above the amiable weakness of making it known that their house was in a line with that of Lady Cecilia Burleigh. [/]
[Narrator] This single fact of itself might suffice to mark the incalculable distance between the Reverend Mr. Beecham of the Crescent Chapel, and the young man who began life as minister of Salem in Carlingford. [/]
[Narrator] And the development outside was not less remarkable than the development within. [/]
[Narrator] It is astonishing how our prejudices change from youth to middle age, even without any remarkable interposition of fortune; I do not say dissipate, or even dispel, which is much more doubtful -- but they change. [/]
[Narrator] When Mr. and Mrs. Beecham commenced life, they had both the warmest feeling of opposition to the Church and everything churchy. [/]
[Narrator] All the circumstances of their lives had encouraged this feeling. [/]
[Narrator] The dislike of the little for the great, the instinctive opposition of a lower class towards the higher, intensified that natural essence of separatism, that determination to be wiser than one's neighbour, which in the common mind lies at the bottom of all dissent. [/]
[Narrator] In saying this we no more accuse Dissenters in religion than Dissenters in politics, or in art, or in criticism. [/]
[Narrator] The first dissenter in most cases is an original thinker, to whom his enforced departure from the ways of his fathers is misery and pain. [/]
[Narrator] Generally he has a hard struggle with himself before he can give up, for the superlative truth which has taken possession of him, all the habits, the pious traditions of his life. [/]
[Narrator] He is the real Nonconformist -- half martyr, half victim, of his convictions. [/]
[Narrator] But that Nonconformity which has come to be the faith in which a large number of people are trained is a totally different business, and affects a very different kind of sentiments. [/]
[Narrator] Personal and independent conviction has no more to do with it than it has to do with the ardour of a Breton peasant trained in deepest zeal of Romanism, or the unbounded certainty of any other traditionary believer. [/]
[Narrator] For this reason we may be allowed to discuss the changes of feeling which manifested themselves in Mr. and Mrs. Beecham without anything disrespectful to Nonconformity. [/]
[Narrator] Not being persons of original mind, they were what their training and circumstances, and a flood of natural influences, made them. [/]
[Narrator] They began life, feeling themselves to be of a hopelessly low social caste, and believing themselves to be superior to their superiors in that enlightenment which they had been brought up to believe distinguished the connection. [/]
[Narrator] The first thing which opened their minds to a dawning doubt whether their enlightenment was, in reality, so much greater than that of their neighbours, was the social change worked in their position by their removal from Carlingford. [/]
[Narrator] In the great towns of the North, Dissent attains its highest social elevation, and Chapel people are no longer to be distinguished from Church people except by the fact that they go to Chapel instead of Church, a definition so simple as to be quite overwhelming to the unprepared dissenting intelligence, brought up in a little Tory borough, still holding for Church and Queen. [/]
[Narrator] The amazing difference which this made in the sentiments of Mrs. Phoebe Beecham, _ née _ Tozer, it is quite impossible to describe. [/]
[Phoebe] Her sudden introduction to"circles"which Mrs. Pigeon had never entered, and to houses at the area - door of which Mr. Brown, the dairyman, would have humbly waited, would have turned the young woman's head, had she not felt the overpowering necessity of keeping that organ as steady as possible, to help her to hold her position in the new world. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe was a girl of spirit, and though her head went round and round, and everything felt confused about her, she did manage desperately to hold her own and to avoid committing herself; but I can not attempt to tell how much her social elevation modified her sectarian zeal. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe was only a woman, so that I am free to assign such motives as having a serious power over her. [/]
[Narrator] Let us hope Mr. Beecham, being a man and a pastor, was moved in a more lofty, intellectual, and spiritual way. [/]
[Narrator] But however that may be, the pair went conjugally together in this modification of sentiment, and by the time they reached the lofty eminence of the Crescent Chapel, were as liberal - minded Nonconformists as heart could desire. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Beecham indeed had many friends in the Low, and even some in the Broad Church. [/]
[Narrator] He appeared on platforms, to promote various public movements, along with clergymen of the Church. [/]
[MrBeecham] He spoke of"our brethren within the pale of the Establishment"always with respect, sometimes even with enthusiasm. [/]
[MrBeecham] "Depend upon it, my dear Sir,"he would even say sometimes to a liberal brother,"the Establishment is not such an unmitigated evil as some people consider it. [/]
[MrBeecham] What should we do in country parishes where the people are not awakened? [/]
[MrBeecham] They do the dirty work for us, my dear brother -- the dirty work." [/]
[Narrator] These sentiments were shared, but perhaps not warmly, by Mr. Beecham's congregation, some of whom were hot Voluntaries, and gave their ministers a little trouble. [/]
[Narrator] But the most part took their Nonconformity very quietly, and were satisfied to know that their chapel was the first in the connection, and their minister justly esteemed as one of the most eloquent. [/]
[Narrator] The Liberation Society held one meeting at the Crescent Chapel, but it was not considered a great success. [/]
[Narrator] At the best, they were no more than lukewarm Crescent - Chapelites, not political dissenters. [/]
[Narrator] Both minister and people were Liberal, that was the creed they professed. [/]
[Narrator] Some of the congregations Citywards, and the smaller chapels about Hampstead and Islington, used the word Latitudinarian instead; but that, as the Crescent Chapel people said, was a word always applied by the bigoted and ignorant to those who held in high regard the doctrines of Christian charity. [/]
[Narrator] They were indeed somewhat proud of their tolerance, their impartiality, their freedom from old prejudices. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "That sort of thing will not do now - a - days,"said Mr. Copperhead, who was a great railway contractor and one of the deacons, and who had himself a son at Oxford. [/]
[Narrator] If there had been any bigotry in the Crescent, Mr. Copperhead would have had little difficulty in transferring himself over the way to St. Paul's Church, and it is astonishing what an effect that fact had upon the mind of Mr. Beecham's flock. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Beecham's house was situated in Regent's Park, and was constructed on the ordinary model of such houses. [/]
[Narrator] On the ground - floor was a handsome dining - room, a room which both Mr. and Mrs. Beecham twenty years before would have considered splendid, but which now they condescended to, as not so large as they could wish, but still comfortable. [/]
[MrBeecham] The drawing - room above was larger, a bright and pleasant room, furnished with considerable"taste." [/]
[Narrator] Behind the dining - room, a smaller room was Mr. Beecham's study, or the library, as it was sometimes called. [/]
[Narrator] It was lined with book - cases containing a very fair collection of books, and ornamented with portraits (chiefly engravings) of celebrated ministers and laymen in the connection, with a bust of Mr. Copperhead over the mantelpiece. [/]
[Narrator] This bust had been done by a young sculptor whom he patronized, for the great man's own house. [/]
[Narrator] When it was nearly completed, however, a flaw was found in the marble, which somewhat detracted from its perfection. [/]
[Narrator] The flaw was in the shoulder of the image, and by no means serious; but Mr. Copperhead was not the man to pass over any such defect. [/]
[Narrator] After a long and serious consultation over it, which made the young artist shake in his shoes, a solution was found for the difficulty. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "Tell you what, Sir,"said Mr. Copperhead;"I'll give it to the minister. [/]
[MrCopperhead] It'll look famous in his little study. [/]
[MrCopperhead] Works of art do n't often come his way; and you'll get a block of the best, Mr. Chipstone -- the very best, Sir, no expense spared -- and begin another for me." [/]
[Narrator] This arrangement was perfectly satisfactory to all parties, though I will not say that it was not instrumental in bringing about certain other combinations which will be fully discussed in this history. [/]
[Narrator] The Beechams were mightily surprised when the huge marble head, almost as large as a Jupiter, though perhaps not otherwise so imposing, arrived at the Terrace; but they were also gratified. [/]
[Phoebe] "It is quite like receiving us into his own family circle,"Mrs. Beecham said with a glance at her daughter, Phoebe, junior, who, with all her pink fingers outspread, was standing in adoration before that image of wealth and fabulous luxury. [/]
[TheYoungEnthusiast] "What a grand head it is!"cried the young enthusiast, gazing rapt upon the complacent marble whisker so delightfully curled and bristling with realistic force. [/]
[MrBeecham] "It looks well, I must say, it looks well,"said Mr. Beecham himself, rubbing his hands,"to receive such a token of respect from the leading member of the flock." [/]
[Narrator] And certainly no more perfect representation of a bell - wether ever adorned any shepherd's sanctuary. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER II. [/]
[Narrator] THE LEADING MEMBER. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Copperhead, to whom so much allusion has been made, was a well - known man in other regions besides that of the Crescent Chapel. [/]
[Narrator] His name, indeed, may be said to have gone to the ends of the earth, from whence he had conducted lines of railway, and where he had left docks, bridges, and light - houses to make him illustrious. [/]
[Narrator] He was one of the greatest contractors for railways and other public works in England, and, by consequence, in the world. [/]
[Narrator] He had no more than a very ordinary education, and no manners to speak of; but at the same time he had that kind of faculty which is in practical work what genius is in literature, and, indeed, in its kind is genius too, though it neither refines nor even (oddly enough) enlarges the mind to which it belongs. [/]
[Narrator] He saw the right track for a road through a country with a glance of his eye; he mastered all the points of nature which were opposed to him in the rapidest survey, though scientifically he was great in no branch of knowledge. [/]
[Narrator] He could rule his men as easily as if they were so many children; and, indeed, they were children in his hands. [/]
[Narrator] All these gifts made it apparent that he must have been a remarkable and able man; but no stranger would have guessed as much from his appearance or his talk. [/]
[Narrator] There were people, indeed, who knew him well, and who remained incredulous and bewildered, trying to persuade themselves that his success must be owing to pure luck, for that he had nothing else to secure it. [/]
[Narrator] The cause of this, perhaps, was that he knew nothing about books, and was one of those jeering cynics who are so common under one guise or another. [/]
[Narrator] Fine cynics are endurable, and give a certain zest often to society, which might become too civil without them; but your coarse cynic is not pleasant. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Copperhead's eye was as effectual in quenching emotion of any but the coarsest kind as water is against fire. [/]
[Narrator] People might be angry in his presence -- it was the only passion he comprehended; but tenderness, sympathy, sorrow, all the more generous sentiments, fled and concealed themselves when this large, rich, costly man came by. [/]
[Narrator] People who were brought much in contact with him became ashamed of having any feelings at all; his eye upon them seemed to convict them of humbug. [/]
[Narrator] Those eyes were very light grey, prominent, with a jeer in them which was a very powerful moral instrument. [/]
[MrCopperheadToWhomSoMuchAllusionHasBeenMade] His own belief was that he could"spot"humbug wherever he saw it, and that nothing could escape him; and, I suppose, so much humbug is there in this world that his belief was justified. [/]
[Narrator] But there are few more awful people than those ignoble spectators whose jeer arrests the moisture in the eye, and strangles the outcry on their neighbour's lip. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Copperhead had risen from the ranks; yet not altogether from the ranks. [/]
[Narrator] His father before him had been a contractor, dealing chiefly with canals and roads, and the old kind of public works; a very rough personage indeed, but one to whose fingers gold had stuck, perhaps because of the clay with which they were always more or less smeared. [/]
[Narrator] This ancestor had made a beginning to the family, and given his son a name to start with. [/]
[Narrator] _ [/]
[Narrator] Our _ [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Copperhead had married young, and had several sons, who were all in business, and all doing well; less vigorous, but still moderately successful copies of their father. [/]
[Narrator] When, however, he had thus done his duty to the State, the first Mrs. Copperhead having died, he did the only incomprehensible action of his life -- he married a second time, a feeble, pretty, pink - and - white little woman, who had been his daughter's governess; married her without rhyme or reason, as all his friends and connections said. [/]
[Narrator] The only feasible motive for this second union seemed to be a desire on Mr. Copperhead's part to have something belonging to him which he could always jeer at, and in this way the match was highly successful. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Copperhead the second was gushing and susceptible, and as good a butt as could be imagined. [/]
[Narrator] She kept him in practice when nobody else was at hand. [/]
[Narrator] She was one of those naturally refined but less than half - educated, timid creatures who are to be found now and then painfully earning the bread which is very bitter to them in richer people's houses, and preserving in their little silent souls some fetish in the shape of a scrap of gentility, which is their sole comfort, or almost their sole comfort. [/]
[MrsCopperhead] Mrs. Copperhead's fetish was the dear recollection that she was"an officer's daughter;"or rather this had been her fetish in the days when she had nothing, and was free to plume herself on the reflected glory. [/]
[Narrator] Whether in the depths of her luxurious abode, at the height of her good fortune, she still found comfort in the thought, it would be hard to tell. [/]
[Narrator] Everybody who had known her in her youth thought her the most fortunate of women. [/]
[Narrator] Her old school companions told her story for the encouragement of their daughters, as they might have told a fairy tale. [/]
[Narrator] To see her rolling in her gorgeous carriage, or bowed out of a shop where all the daintiest devices of fashion had been placed at her feet, filled passers - by with awe and envy. [/]
[Narrator] She could buy whatever she liked, festoon herself with finery, surround herself with the costliest knick - knacks; the more there were of them, and the costlier they were, the better was Mr. Copperhead pleased. [/]
[Narrator] She had everything that heart could desire. [/]
[Narrator] Poor little woman! [/]
[Narrator] What a change from the governess - chrysalis who was snubbed by her pupil and neglected by everybody! [/]
[Narrator] and yet I am not sure that she did not -- so inconsistent is human nature -- look back to those melancholy days with a sigh. [/]
[Narrator] This lady was the mother of Clarence Copperhead, the young man who was at Oxford, her only child, upon whom (of course) [/]
[Narrator] she doted with the fondest folly; and whom his father jeered at more than at any one else in the world, more even than at his mother, yet was prouder of than of all his other sons and all his possessions put together. [/]
[Narrator] Clarence, whom I will not describe, as he will, I trust, show himself more effectually by his actions, was like his mother in disposition, or so, at least, she made herself happy by thinking; but by some freak of nature he was like his father in person, and carried his mouse's heart in a huge frame, somewhat hulking and heavy - shouldered, with the same roll which distinguished Mr. Copperhead, and which betrayed something of the original navvy who was the root of the race. [/]
[Narrator] He had his father's large face too, and a tendency towards those demonstrative and offensive whiskers which are the special inheritance of the British Philistine. [/]
[Narrator] But instead of the large goggle eyes, always jeering and impudent, which lighted up the paternal countenance, Clarence had a pair of mild brown orbs, repeated from his mother's faded face, which introduced the oddest discord into his physiognomy generally. [/]
[Narrator] In the family, that is to say among the step - brothers and step - sisters who formed Mr. Copperhead's first family, the young fellow bore no other name than that of the curled darling, though, indeed, he was as far from being curled as any one could be. [/]
[Narrator] He was not clever; he had none of the energy of his race, and promised to be as useless in an office as he would have been in a cutting or a yard full of men. [/]
[Narrator] I am not sure that this fact did not increase secretly his father's exultation and pride in him. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Copperhead was fond of costly and useless things; he liked them for their cost, with an additional zest in his sense of the huge vulgar use and profit of most things in his own life. [/]
[Narrator] This tendency, more than any appreciation of the beautiful, made him what is called a patron of art. [/]
[Narrator] It swelled his personal importance to think that he was able to hang up thousands of pounds, so to speak, on his walls, knowing all the time that he could make thousands more by the money had he invested it in more useful ways. [/]
[Narrator] The very fact that he could afford to refrain from investing it, that he could let it lie there useless, hanging by so many cords and ribbons, was sweet to him. [/]
[Narrator] And so also it was sweet to him to possess a perfectly useless specimen of humanity, which had cost him a great deal, and promised to cost him still more. [/]
[Narrator] He had plenty of useful sons as he had of useful money. [/]
[Narrator] The one who was of no use was the apex and glory of the whole. [/]
[Narrator] But these three made up a strange enough family party, as may be supposed. [/]
[Narrator] The original Copperheads, the first family, who were all of the same class and nature, would have made a much noisier, less peaceable household; but they would have been a much jollier and really more harmonious one. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Copperhead himself somewhat despised his elder sons, who were like himself, only less rich, less vigorous, and less self - assertive. [/]
[Narrator] He saw, oddly enough, the coarseness of their manners, and even of their ways of thinking; but yet he was a great deal more comfortable, more at his ease among them, than he was when seated opposite his trembling, deprecating, frightened little wife, or that huge youth who cost him so much and returned him so little. [/]
[Narrator] Now and then, at regular periodical intervals, the head of the family would go down to Blackheath to dine and spend the night with his son Joe, the second and the favourite, where there were romping children and a portly, rosy young matron, and loud talk about City dinners, contracts, and estimates. [/]
[Narrator] This refreshed him, and he came home with many chuckles over the imperfections of the family. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "My sons buy their wives by the hundred - weight,"he would say jocularly at breakfast the day after;"thirteen stone if she is a pound, is Mrs. Joe. [/]
[MrCopperhead] Expensive to keep up in velvet and satin, not to speak of mutton and beef. [/]
[MrCopperhead] Your mother comes cheap,"he would add aside to Clarence, with a rolling laugh. [/]
[Narrator] Thus he did not in the least exempt his descendants from the universal ridicule which he poured on all the world; but when he sat down opposite his timid little delicate wife, and by his University man, who had very little on the whole to say for himself, Mr. Copperhead felt the increase in gentility as well as the failure in jollity. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "You are a couple of ghosts after Joe and his belongings, you two. [/]
[MrCopperhead] Speak louder, I say, young fellow. [/]
[MrCopperhead] You do n't expect me to hear that penny - whistle of yours,"he would say, chuckling at them, with a mixture of pride and disdain. [/]
[Narrator] They amused him by their dulness and silence, and personal awe of him. [/]
[Narrator] He was quite out of his element between these two, and yet the very fact pleasantly excited his pride. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "I speak as gentlemen generally speak,"said Clarence, who was sometimes sullen when attacked, and who knew by experience that his father was rarely offended by such an argument. [/]
[MrsCopperhead] "And I am sure, dear, your papa would never wish you to do otherwise,"said anxious Mrs. Copperhead, casting a furtive frightened glance at her husband. [/]
[Narrator] He rolled out a mighty laugh from the head of the table where he was sitting. [/]
[Narrator] He contemplated them with a leer that would have been insulting, had he not been the husband of one and the father of the other. [/]
[Narrator] The laugh and the look called forth some colour on Mrs. Copperhead's cheek, well as she was used to them; but her son was less susceptible, and ate his breakfast steadily, and did not care. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "A pretty pair you are,"said Mr. Copperhead. [/]
[I] "I like your gentility. [/]
[I] How much _ foie gras _ would you eat for breakfast, I wonder, my lad, if you had to work for it? [/]
[I] Luckily for you, I was n't brought up to talk, as you say, like a gentleman. [/]
[I] I'd like to see you managing a field of navvies with that nice little voice of yours -- ay, or a mob before the hustings, my boy. [/]
[I] You're good for nothing, you are; a nice delicate piece of china for a cupboard, like your mother before you. [/]
[I] However, thank Heaven, we've got the cupboard,"he said with a laugh, looking round him;"a nice big'un, too, well painted and gilded; and the time has come, through not talking like a gentleman, that I can afford you. [/]
[I] You should hear Joe. [/]
[I] When that fellow talks, his house shakes. [/]
[I] Confounded bad style of house, walls like gingerbread. [/]
[I] How the boards do n't break like pie - crust under Mrs. Joe's fairy foot, I ca n't make out. [/]
[I] By Jove, ma'am, one would think I starved you, to see you beside your daughter - in - law. [/]
[I] Always had a fine healthy appetite had Mrs. Joe." [/]
[Narrator] There was nothing to answer to this speech, and therefore a dead silence ensued. [/]
[Narrator] When the master of the house is so distinctly the master, silence is apt to ensue after his remarks. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Copperhead sipped her tea, and Clarence worked steadily through his breakfast, and the head of the family crumpled the Times, which he read at intervals. [/]
[Narrator] All sorts of jokes had gone on at Joe's table the morning before, and there had been peals of laughter, and Mrs. Joe had even administered a slap upon her husband's ruddy cheek for some pleasantry or other. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Copperhead, as he looked at his son and his wife, chuckled behind the Times. [/]
[Narrator] When they thought he was occupied they made a few gentle remarks to each other. [/]
[Narrator] They had soft voices, with that indescribable resemblance in tone which so often exists between mother and son. [/]
[Narrator] Dresden china; yes, that was the word; and to see his own resemblance made in that delicate _ pâte _, and elevated into that region of superlative costliness, tickled Mr. Copperhead, and in the most delightful way. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "How about your ball?"was his next question,"or Clarence's ball, as you do n't seem to take much interest in it, ma'am? [/]
[MrCopperhead] You are afraid of being brought in contact with the iron pots, eh? [/]
[MrCopperhead] You might crack or go to pieces, who knows, and what would become of me, a wretched widower. [/]
[Narrator] "Mr. Copperhead himself laughed loudly at this joke, which did not excite any mirth from the others, and then he repeated his question,"How about the ball?""The invitations are all sent out, Mr. Copperhead; ninety - five -- I -- I mean a hundred and thirty - five. [/]
[ThePoorWoman] I -- I beg your pardon, they were in two lots,"answered the poor woman nervously. [/]
[Narrator] "A hundred and thirty - eight -- and there is -- a few more--""Take your time, ma'am, take your time, we'll get at the truth at last,"said her husband; and he laid down his paper and looked at her. [/]
[Narrator] He was not angry nor impatient. [/]
[Narrator] The twinkle in his eye was purely humorous. [/]
[Narrator] Her stumblings amused him, and her nervousness. [/]
[Narrator] But oddly enough, the most furious impatience could not have more deeply disconcerted her. [/]
[ThePoorWoman] "There are a few more -- some old friends of mine,"she went on, confused. [/]
[Narrator] "They were once rather -- kind -- took an interest; that is--""Oh, the baronet and his daughters,"said Mr. Copperhead,"by all means let's have the baronet and his daughters. [/]
[Narrator] Though as for their taking an interest -- if you had not been a rich man's wife, ma'am, living in a grand house in Portland Place--""It was not now,"she said, hurriedly. [/]
[ThePoorWoman] "I do not suppose that any one takes an interest -- in me now--"Mr. Copperhead laughed, and nodded his head. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "Not many, ma'am, I should think -- not many. [/]
[MrCopperhead] You women must make up your minds to that. [/]
[MrCopperhead] It's all very well to take an interest in a pretty girl; but when you come to a certain age -- [/]
[MrCopperhead] Well, let's proceed, the baronet--""And his two girls--""Ah, there's two girls! [/]
[MrCopperhead] that's for you, Clarence, my boy. [/]
[MrCopperhead] I thought there must be a motive. [/]
[MrCopperhead] Think that fellow a good _ parti _, eh? [/]
[MrCopperhead] And I would not say they were far wrong if he behaves himself. [/]
[MrCopperhead] Make a note of the baronet's daughter, young man. [/]
[MrCopperhead] Lord, what a world it is!"said Mr. Copperhead, reflectively. [/]
[Narrator] "I should not wonder if you had been scheming, too.""I would not for the world!"cried the poor little woman, roused for once. [/]
[ThePoorLittleWoman] "I would not for anything interfere with a marriage. [/]
[ThePoorLittleWoman] That is the last thing you need fear from me. [/]
[ThePoorLittleWoman] Whether it was a girl I was fond of, or a girl I disliked -- so long as she was Clarence's choice. [/]
[ThePoorLittleWoman] Oh, I know the harm that is done by other people's meddling -- nothing, nothing, would induce me to interfere. [/]
[ThePoorLittleWoman] "Mr. Copperhead laid down his paper, and looked at her. [/]
[Narrator] I suppose, however little a man may care for his wife, he does not relish the idea that she married him for anything but love. [/]
[Narrator] He contemplated her still with amused ridicule, but with something fiercer in his eyes. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "Oh -- h!"he said,"you do n't like other people to interfere? [/]
[MrCopperhead] not so much as to say, it's a capital match, eh? [/]
[MrCopperhead] You'll get so and so, and so and so, that you could n't have otherwise -- carriages perhaps, and plenty of money in your pocket (which it may be you never had in your life before), and consideration, and one of the finest houses in London, let us say in Portland Place. [/]
[MrCopperhead] You do n't like that amount of good advice, eh? [/]
[MrCopperhead] Well, I do -- I mean to interfere with my son, to that extent at least -- you can do what you like. [/]
[MrCopperhead] But as you're a person of prodigious influence, and strong will, and a great deal of character, and all that,"Mr. Copperhead broke out with a rude laugh,"I'm afraid of you, I am -- quite afraid. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "Fortunately, just at this moment his brougham came round, and the great man finished his coffee at a gulp, and got up. [/]
[TheGreatMan] "You look out for the baronet's daughters, then--"he said,"and see all's ready for this ball of yours; while I go and work to pay the bills, that's my share. [/]
[TheGreatMan] You do the ornamental, and I do the useful, ha, ha! [/]
[TheGreatMan] I'll keep up my share. [/]
[TheGreatMan] "It was astonishing what a difference came upon the room the moment he disappeared. [/]
[Narrator] Somehow it had been out of harmony. [/]
[Narrator] His voice, his look, his heavy person, even his whiskers had been out of character. [/]
[Narrator] Now the air seemed to flutter after the closing of the door like water into which something offensive has sunk, and when the ripples of movement were over the large handsome room had toned down into perfect accord with its remaining inhabitants. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Copperhead's eyes were rather red -- not with tears, but with the inclination to shed tears, which she carefully restrained in her son's presence. [/]
[Narrator] He still continued to eat steadily -- he had an admirable appetite. [/]
[TheGreatMan] But when he had finished everything on his plate, he looked up and said,"I hope you do n't mind, mamma; I do n't suppose you do; but I do n't like the way my father speaks to you." [/]

[TheMother] I ought to know by this time that it's only your papa's way. [/]
[Narrator] ""I suppose so -- but I do n't like it,"said the young man, decisively. [/]
[Narrator] He did not notice, however, as after second thoughts he returned to the game - pie, that his mother's eyes were redder than ever. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER III. [/]
[Narrator] MR. COPPERHEAD'S BALL. [/]
[Narrator] This ball was an event, not only in Mr. Copperhead's household, but even in the connection itself, to which the idea of balls, as given by leading members of the flock, was somewhat novel. [/]
[Narrator] Not that the young people were debarred from that amusement, but it was generally attained in a more or less accidental manner, and few professing Christians connected with the management of the chapel had gone the length of giving such an entertainment openly and with design. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Copperhead, however, was in a position to triumph over all such prejudices. [/]
[Narrator] He was so rich that any community would have felt it ought to extend a certain measure of indulgence to such a man. [/]
[Narrator] Very wealthy persons are like spoilt children, their caprices are allowed to be natural, and even when we are angry with them we excuse the vagaries to which money has a right. [/]
[Narrator] This feeling of indulgence goes a very great way, especially among the classes engaged in money - making, who generally recognize a man's right to spend, and feel the sweetness of spending more acutely than the hereditary possessors of wealth. [/]
[Narrator] I do not believe that his superior knowledge of the best ways of using money profitably ever hinders a money - making man from lavish expenditure; but it gives him a double zest in spending, and it makes him, generally, charitable towards the extravagances of persons still richer than himself. [/]
[Narrator] A ball, there was no doubt, was a worldly - minded entertainment, but still, the chapel reflected, it is almost impossible not to be a little worldly - minded when you possess such a great share of the world's goods, and that, of course, it could not be for himself that Mr. Copperhead was doing this, but for his son. [/]
[Narrator] His son, these amiable casuists proceeded, was being brought up to fill a great position, and no doubt society did exact something, and as Mr. Copperhead had asked all the chief chapel people, his ball was looked upon with very indulgent eyes. [/]
[Narrator] The fact that the minister and his family were going staggered some of the more particular members a little, but Mr. Beecham took high ground on the subject and silenced the flock. [/]
[ThePastor] "The fact that a minister of religion is one of the first persons invited, is sufficient proof of the way our friend means to manage everything,"said the pastor. [/]
[ThePastor] "Depend upon it, it would be good for the social relations of the country if your pastors and teachers were always present. [/]
[ThePastor] It gives at once a character to all the proceedings." [/]
[Narrator] This, like every other lofty assertion, stilled the multitude. [/]
[Narrator] Some of the elder ladies, indeed, groaned to hear, even at the prayer - meetings, a whisper between the girls about this ball and what they were going to wear; but still it was Christmas, and all the newspapers, and a good deal of the light literature which is especially current at that season, persistently represented all the world as in a state of imbecile joviality, and thus, for the moment, every objection was put down. [/]
[Narrator] To nobody, however, was the question, what to wear, more interesting than to Phoebe, junior, who was a very well - instructed young woman, and even on the point of dress had theories of her own. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe had, as her parents were happy to think, had every advantage in her education. [/]
[Narrator] She had possessed a German governess all to herself, by which means, even Mr. Beecham himself supposed, a certain amount of that philosophy which Germans communicate by their very touch must have got into her, besides her music and the language which was her primary study. [/]
[Narrator] And she had attended lectures at the ladies'college close by, and heard a great many eminent men on a great many different subjects. [/]
[Narrator] She had read, too, a great deal. [/]
[Narrator] She was very well got up in the subject of education for women, and lamented often and pathetically the difficulty they lay under of acquiring the highest instruction; but at the same time she patronized Mr. Ruskin's theory that dancing, drawing, and cooking were three of the higher arts which ought to be studied by girls. [/]
[Narrator] It is not necessary for me to account for the discrepancies between those two systems, in the first place because I can not, and in the second place, because there is in the mind of the age some ineffable way of harmonizing them which makes their conjunction common. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe was restrained from carrying out either to its full extent. [/]
[Narrator] She was not allowed to go in for the Cambridge examinations because Mr. Beecham felt the connection might think it strange to see his daughter's name in the papers, and, probably, would imagine he meant to make a schoolmistress of her, which he thanked Providence he had no need to do. [/]
[Narrator] And she was not allowed to educate herself in the department of cooking, to which Mrs. Beecham objected, saying likewise, thank Heaven, they had no need of such messings; that she did not wish her daughter to make a slave of herself, and that Cook would not put up with it. [/]
[Virgil] Between these two limits Phoebe's noble ambition was confined, which was a"trial"to her. [/]
[Narrator] But she did what she could, bating neither heart nor hope. [/]
[Narrator] She read Virgil at least, if not Sophocles, and she danced and dressed though she was not allowed to cook. [/]
[Narrator] As she took the matter in this serious way, it will be understood that the question of dress was not a mere frivolity with her. [/]
[Narrator] A week before the ball she stood in front of the large glass in her mother's room, contemplating herself, not with that satisfaction which it is generally supposed a pretty young woman has in contemplating her own image. [/]
[Narrator] She was decidedly a pretty young woman. [/]
[Narrator] She had a great deal of the hair of the period, nature in her case, as (curiously, yet very truly) in so many others, having lent herself to the prevailing fashion. [/]
[Narrator] How it comes about I can not tell, but it is certain that there does exist at this present moment, a proportion of golden - haired girls which very much exceeds the number we used to see when golden hair had not become fashionable -- a freak of nature which is altogether independent of dyes and auriferous fluid, and which probably has influenced fashion unawares. [/]
[Narrator] To be sure the pomades of twenty years ago are, Heaven be praised! [/]
[Narrator] unknown to this generation, and washing also has become the fashion, which accounts for something. [/]
[Narrator] Anyhow, Phoebe, junior, possessed in perfection the hair of the period. [/]
[Narrator] She had, too, the complexion which goes naturally with those sunny locks -- a warm pink and white, which, had the boundaries between the pink and the white been a little more distinct, would have approached perfection too. [/]
[Narrator] This was what she was thinking when she looked at herself in her mother's great glass. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Beecham stood behind her, more full - blown and more highly - coloured than she, but very evidently the rose to which this bud would come in time. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe looked at her own reflection, and then at her mother's, and sighed such a profound sigh as only lungs in the most excellent condition could produce. [/]
[Phoebe] "Mamma,"she said, with an accent of despair,"I am too pink, a great deal too pink! [/]
[Phoebe] What am I to do?""Nonsense, my pet,"said Mrs. Beecham;"you have a lovely complexion;"and she threw a quantity of green ribbons which lay by over her child's hair and shoulders. [/]
[Narrator] A cloud crossed the blooming countenance of Phoebe, junior. [/]
[Narrator] She disembarrassed herself of the ribbons with another sigh. [/]
[Phoebe] "Dear mamma,"she said,"I wish you would let me read with you now and then, about the theory of colours, for instance. [/]
[Phoebe] Green is the complementary of red. [/]
[Phoebe] If you want to bring out my pink and make it more conspicuous than ever, of course you will put me in a green dress. [/]
[Phoebe] No, mamma, dear, not that -- I should look a fright; and though I dare say it does not matter much, I object to looking a fright. [/]
[Phoebe] Women are, I suppose, more ornamental than men, or, at least, everybody says so; and in that case it is our duty to keep it up." [/]
[Phoebe] "You are a funny girl, with your theories of colour,"said Mrs. Beecham. [/]
[Phoebe] "In my time, fair girls wore greens and blues, and dark girls wore reds and yellows. [/]
[Phoebe] It was quite simple. [/]
[Phoebe] Have a white tarlatan, then; every girl looks well in that. [/]
[Phoebe] ""You do n't see, mamma,"said Phoebe, softly, suppressing in the most admirable manner the delicate trouble of not being understood,"that a thing every girl looks well in, is just the sort of thing that no one looks _ very _ well in. [/]
[Phoebe] White shows no invention. [/]
[Phoebe] It is as if one took no trouble about one's dress. [/]
[Narrator] ""And neither one ought, Phoebe,"said her mother. [/]
[HerMother] "That is very true. [/]
[HerMother] It is sinful to waste time thinking of colours and ribbons, when we might be occupied about much more important matters. [/]
[Narrator] ""That is not my opinion at all,"said Phoebe. [/]
[Phoebe] "I should like people to think I had taken a great deal of trouble. [/]
[Phoebe] Think of all the trouble that has to be taken to get up this ball!""I fear so, indeed; and a great deal of expense,"said Mrs. Beecham, shaking her head. [/]
[Phoebe] "Yes, when one comes to think of that. [/]
[Phoebe] But then, you see, wealth has its duties. [/]
[Phoebe] I do n't defend Mr. Copperhead--""I do n't think he wants to be defended, mamma. [/]
[Phoebe] I think it is all nonsense about wasting time. [/]
[Phoebe] What I incline to, if you wo n't be shocked, is black. [/]
[Phoebe] ""Black!" [/]
[Narrator] The suggestion took away Mrs. Beecham's breath. [/]
[Phoebe] "As if you were fifty! [/]
[Phoebe] Why, I do n't consider myself old enough for black. [/]
[Phoebe] ""It is a pity,"said Phoebe, with a glance at her mother's full colours; but that was really of so much less importance. [/]
[Phoebe] "Black would throw me up,"she added seriously, turning to the glass. [/]
[Phoebe] "It would take off this pink look. [/]
[Phoebe] I do n't mind it in the cheeks, but I am pink all over; my white is pink. [/]
[Phoebe] Black would be a great deal the best for both of us. [/]
[Phoebe] It would tone us down,"said Phoebe, decisively,"and it would throw us up." [/]
[Phoebe] "But for you, a girl under twenty, my dear--""Mamma, what does it matter? [/]
[Phoebe] The question is, am I to look my best? which I think is my duty to you and to Providence; or am I just,"said Phoebe, with indignation,"to look a little insipidity -- a creature with no character -- a little girl like everybody else? [/]
[Phoebe] "The consequence of this solemn appeal was that both the Phoebes went to Mr. Copperhead's ball in black; the elder in velvet, with Honiton lace (point, which Phoebe, with her artistic instincts, would have much preferred, being unattainable); the younger in tulle, flounced to distraction, and largely relieved with blue. [/]
[Narrator] And the consequence of this toilette, and of the fact that Phoebe did her duty by her parents and by Providence, and looked her very best, was that Clarence Copperhead fell a hopeless victim to her fascinations, and scarcely could be induced to leave her side all night. [/]
[Narrator] The ball was about as remarkable a ball as could have been seen in London. [/]
[Narrator] The son of the house had contemplated with absolute despair the list of invitations. [/]
[Narrator] He had deprecated the entertainment altogether. [/]
[TheSonOfTheHouse] He had said,"We know nobody,"with a despairing impertinence which called forth one of his father's roars of laughter. [/]
[Narrator] And though Mr. Copperhead had done all he could to assume the position of that typical Paterfamilias who is condemned to pay for those pleasures of his family which are no pleasure to him, yet common - sense was too much for him, and everybody felt that he was in reality the giver and enjoyer of the entertainment. [/]
[Narrator] It was Mr. , not Mrs. Copperhead's ball. [/]
[Narrator] It was the first of the kind which had ever taken place in his house; the beginning of a new chapter in his social existence. [/]
[MrsCopperhead] Up to this moment he had not shown any signs of being smitten with that craze for"Society,"which so often and so sorely affects the millionnaire. [/]
[Narrator] He had contented himself hitherto with heavy and showy dinners, costing Heaven knows how much a head (Mr. Copperhead knew, and swelled visibly in pride and pleasure as the cost increased), which he consumed in company with twenty people or so of kindred tastes to himself, who appreciated the cost and understood his feelings. [/]
[Narrator] On such people, however, his Dresden china was thrown away. [/]
[SuchPeople] Joe and Mrs. Joe were much more in their way than the elegant University man and the well - bred mother, who was"a poor little dowdy,"they all said. [/]
[Narrator] Therefore the fact had been forced upon Mr. Copperhead that his circle must be widened and advanced, if his crowning glories were to be appreciated as they deserved. [/]
[Narrator] The hunger of wealth for that something above wealth which the bewildered rich man only discovers the existence of when he has struggled to the highest pinnacle of advancement in his own way, began to seize this wealthy neophyte. [/]
[Narrator] To be sure, in this first essay, the company which he assembled in his fine rooms in Portland Place, to see all his fine things and celebrate his glory, was not a fine company, but they afforded more gratification to Mr. Copperhead than if they had been ever so fine. [/]
[Narrator] They were people of his own class, his old friends, invited to be dazzled, though standing out to the utmost of their power, and refusing, so far as in them lay, to admit how much dazzled they were. [/]
[Narrator] It was a more reasonable sort of vanity than the commoner kind, which aims at displaying its riches to great personages, people who are not dazzled by any extent of grandeur, and in whose bosoms no jealousy is excited towards the giver of the feast. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Copperhead's friends had much more lively feelings; they walked about through the great rooms, with their wives on their arms, in a state of semi - defiance, expressing no admiration, saying to each other,"This must have cost Copperhead a pretty penny,"as they met in doorways; while the ladies put their flowery and jewelled heads together and whispered,"Did you ever see such extravagance? [/]
[TheLadies] And what a dowdy _ she _ is with it all!" [/]
[Narrator] This was the under - current of sentiment which flowed strong in all the passages, and down the rapids of the great staircase; a stream of vigorous human feeling, the existence of which was as deeply gratifying to the entertainer as the sweetest flattery. [/]
[Narrator] The lord and the ladies who might have been tempted to his great house would not have had a thought to spare for Mr. Copperhead; but the unwilling applause of his own class afforded him a true triumph. [/]
[Narrator] Amid this throng of people, however, there could be little doubt that the one young lady who attracted his son was the least eligible person there, being no other than Phoebe Beecham, the pastor's daughter. [/]
[Narrator] Almost the only other utterly ineligible girl was a pale little maiden who accompanied Sir Robert Dorset and his daughters, and who was supposed to be either their governess or their humble companion. [/]
[TheOnlyPeopleWhoHadAnyPretensionsToBelongToSocietyInAllThoseCrowdedRooms] The Dorsets were the only people who had any pretensions to belong to"society,"in all those crowded rooms. [/]
[TheBaronet] They were distantly related to Mrs. Copperhead, and had been, she gratefully thought, kind to her in her youth, and they had no particular objection to be kind to her now that she was rich, though the Baronet, as Mr. Copperhead always called him, winced at so rampant a specimen of wealth, and"the girls"did not see what good it was to keep up relations with a distant cousin, who though so prodigiously rich was of no possible use, and could neither make parties for them, nor chaperon them to the houses of the great. [/]
[Narrator] When they had received her present invitation, they had accepted it with surprise and hesitation. [/]
[Narrator] Chance only had brought them to London at that time of the year, the most curious time surely to choose for a ball, but convenient enough as affording a little amusement at a season when little amusement was ordinarily to be had. [/]
[Narrator] Sir Robert had consented to go, as a man with no occupation elsewhere might consent to go to the Cannibal Islands, to see how the savages comported themselves. [/]
[Narrator] And little Ursula May, another poor relative on the other side of the house, whom they had charitably brought up to town with them, might go too, they decided, to such a gathering. [/]
[TheGirls] There was no Lady Dorset, and the girls were"girls"only by courtesy, having passed the age to which that title refers. [/]
[Narrator] Such good looks as they had were faded, and they were indifferently dressed. [/]
[Narrator] This last circumstance arose partly from the fact that they never dressed very well, and partly because they did not think it necessary to put themselves to much trouble for poor Mrs. Copperhead's ball. [/]
[Narrator] Their little companion, Ursula, was in a white frock, the sort of dress which Phoebe had rebelled against. [/]
[Narrator] She was all white and had never been to a ball before. [/]
[Narrator] This little party, which represented the aristocracy at the Copperhead's ball, went to the entertainment with a little expectation in their minds: What sort of people would be there? [/]
[TheGirls] Would they be"frights?" [/]
[Narrator] They were not likely to be interesting in any other way, the Miss Dorsets knew; but to little Ursula a ball was a ball, and meant delight and glory she was aware, though she did not quite know how. [/]
[Narrator] The expectations of the party, however, were strangely disappointed. [/]
[MrsCopperhead] Instead of being"a set of frights,"Mrs. Copperhead's guests were found to be resplendent in toilette. [/]
[Narrator] Never, even under a ducal roof, had these ladies found themselves in such a gorgeous assembly, and never before, perhaps, even at the Duchess's grandest receptions, had they been unable to discover a single face they knew. [/]
[Narrator] Sir Robert was even more appalled by this discovery than his daughters were. [/]
[Narrator] He put up his glass and peered more and more wistfully into the crowd. [/]
[SirRobert] "Do n't know a soul,"he repeated at intervals. [/]
[Narrator] Poor Sir Robert! [/]
[Narrator] he had not thought it possible that such an event could happen to him within the four seas. [/]
[Narrator] Accordingly the Dorsets clung, somewhat scared, to Mrs. Copperhead's side, and Ursula along with them, who looked at the crowd still more wistfully than Sir Robert did, and thought how nice it would be to know somebody. [/]
[Narrator] Unfortunately the Miss Dorsets were not attractive in personal appearance. [/]
[Narrator] Clarence Copperhead, though he was not indifferent to a baronet, was yet not sufficiently devoted to the aristocracy to do more than dance once, as was his bounden duty, with each of the sisters. [/]
[TheseLadies] "It seems so strange not to know any one,"these ladies said. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "Is n't it?"said Clarence. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "_ I _ do n't know a soul." [/]
[Narrator] But then he went off and danced with Phoebe Beecham, and the Miss Dorsets stood by Mrs. Copperhead, almost concealing behind them the slight little snow - white figure of little Ursula May. [/]
[Narrator] Clarence was a very well - behaved young man on the whole. [/]
[Narrator] He knew his duty, and did it with a steady industry, working off his dances in the spirit of his navvy forefather. [/]
[Narrator] But he returned between each duty dance to the young lady in black, who was always distinguishable among so many young ladies in white, and pink, and green, and blue. [/]
[Narrator] The Miss Dorsets and Ursula looked with interest and something like envy at that young lady in black. [/]
[Narrator] She had so many partners that she scarcely knew how to manage them all, and the son of the house returned to her side with a pertinacity that could not pass unremarked. [/]
[Ursula] "Why should one girl have so much and another girl so little?" [/]
[Narrator] Ursula said to herself; but, to be sure, she knew nobody, and the young lady in black knew everybody. [/]
[Narrator] On the whole, however, it became evident to Ursula that a ball was not always a scene of unmixed delight. [/]
[Phoebe] "It is very kind of you to remember what old friends we are,"said Phoebe. [/]
[Phoebe] "But, Mr. Clarence, do n't be more good to me than you ought to be. [/]
[Phoebe] I see your mother looking for you, and Mr. Copperhead might not like it. [/]
[Phoebe] Another time, perhaps, we shall be able to talk of old days. [/]
[Narrator] ""There is no time like the present,"said the young man, who liked his own way. [/]
[Narrator] I do not mean to say that it was right of Phoebe to dance with him, especially dances she had promised to other people. [/]
[Narrator] But he was the personage of the evening, and that is a great temptation. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Copperhead himself came up to them more than once, with meaning in his eyes. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "Do n't be too entertaining, Miss Phoebe,"he said; for he saw no reason why he should not speak plainly in his own house, especially to the minister's daughter. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "Do n't be too entertaining. [/]
[MrCopperhead] This is Clarence's ball, and he ought to be civil to other people too." [/]
[Phoebe] "Oh, please go away!" [/]
[Narrator] cried Phoebe, after this admonition. [/]
[Narrator] But Clarence was sullen, and stood his ground. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "We are going to have our waltz out,"he said. [/]
[TheYoungMan] "It is not my ball a bit -- let him entertain his people himself. [/]
[TheYoungMan] How should I know such a set of guys? [/]
[TheYoungMan] I know nobody [/]
[TheYoungMan] but you and the Dorset girls, who are in society. [/]

[TheYoungMan] Perhaps you do n't feel that, but I should think girls must see it sometimes as well as men. [/]
[Narrator] ""Girls do n't use such strong expressions,"said Phoebe, smiling, as they flew off in the uncompleted waltz. [/]
[Narrator] She danced very well, better than most of the ladies present, and that was the reason Clarence assigned to his mother for his preference of her. [/]
[Narrator] But when Mr. Copperhead saw that his remonstrance was unheeded by the young people, he went up to Mrs. Beecham, with a rich man's noble frankness and courage. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "I am delighted to see you here, ma'am, and I hope you have remarked how well Miss Phoebe is entertaining my boy. [/]
[MrCopperhead] Do you see them dancing? [/]
[MrCopperhead] She's been away from you a long time, Mrs. Beecham, as girls will when they get hold of somebody that pleases them. [/]
[MrCopperhead] Should n't you like me to go and fetch her back?" [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Beecham, with cheeks that were very full blown indeed, and required a great deal of fanning, called back her child to her side at the end of that dance. [/]
[Narrator] She scolded Phoebe behind her fan, and recalled her to a sense of duty. [/]
[Phoebe] "A pastor's daughter has to be doubly particular,"she said;"what if your poor papa was to get into trouble through your thoughtlessness?""I was not thoughtless, mamma; forgive me for answering back,"said Phoebe, very meekly; and she showed no signs of sulkiness, though Clarence was carried off and kept from approaching her again. [/]
[Narrator] Unfortunately, however, when Clarence was removed from Phoebe, he fell into still greater peril. [/]
[Narrator] The eldest Miss Dorset and her mother, both of them with equally benevolent intentions, introduced him simultaneously to Ursula May. [/]
[MrsCopperhead] "The poor little girl has not danced once,"Mrs. Copperhead, who had recollections of standing by herself for a whole evening, unnoticed, whispered in his ear, and Miss Dorset spoke to him still more plainly. [/]

[Narrator] And what could Clarence do but offer himself? [/]
[Narrator] And Ursula, too, was a good dancer, and very pretty -- far prettier than Phoebe. [/]
[HisFather] "Confound him! [/]
[HisFather] there he is now for ever with that girl in white,"said his father to himself, with great rage. [/]
[Narrator] Dozens of good partners in pink and blue were going about the room. [/]
[Narrator] What did the boy mean by bestowing himself upon the two poor ones, the black and the white. [/]
[Narrator] This disturbed Mr. Copperhead's enjoyment, as he stood in the doorway of the ball - room, looking round upon all the splendour that was his, and feeling disposed, like Nebuchadnezzar, to call upon everybody to come and worship him. [/]
[Narrator] He expanded and swelled out with pride and complacency, as he looked round upon his own greatness, and perceived the effect made upon the beholders. [/]
[Narrator] When that effect did not seem sufficiently deep, he called here and there upon a lingerer for applause. [/]
[TheBoy] "That's considered a very fine Turner,"he said, taking one of them into a smaller room. [/]
[TheBoy] "Come along here, you know about that sort of thing -- I do n't. [/]
[TheBoy] I should be ashamed to tell you how much I gave for it; all that money hanging there useless, bringing in nothing! [/]
[Narrator] But when I do buy anything I like it to be the very best that is to be had.""I'd as soon have a good chromo,"said the person addressed,"which costs a matter of a five - pound note, and enough too, to hang up against a wall. [/]
[ThePerson] But you can afford it, Copperhead. [/]
[ThePerson] You've the best right of any man I know to be a fool if you like." [/]
[Narrator] The great man laughed, but he scarcely liked the compliment. [/]
[TheGreatMan] "I am a fool if you like,"he said,"the biggest fool going. [/]
[TheGreatMan] I like a thing that costs a deal, and is of no use. [/]
[TheGreatMan] That's what I call luxury. [/]
[TheGreatMan] My boy, Clarence, and my big picture, they're dear; but I can afford'em, if they were double the price." [/]
[HisFriend] "If I were you,"said his friend,"I would n't hang my picture in this little bit of a hole, nor let my boy waste his time with all the riff - raff in the room. [/]
[HisFriend] There's Smith's girl and Robinson's niece, both of them worth a cool hundred thousand; and you leave him to flourish about all over the place with a chit in a white frock, and another in a black one. [/]
[HisFriend] I call that waste, not luxury, for my part. [/]
[Narrator] ""I do n't want to sell either the boy or the picture,"said the rich man, with a laugh. [/]
[Narrator] But nevertheless he was annoyed that his son should be such an ass. [/]
[Narrator] Miss Smith and Miss Robinson were as fine as their milliners could make them. [/]
[Narrator] The first of these ladies had an emerald locket almost as big as a warming - pan, and Miss Robinson's pearls were a little fortune in themselves; but the chosen objects of that young idiot's attentions wore nothing but trumpery twopenny - halfpenny trinkets, and gowns which had been made at home for all Mr. Copperhead knew. [/]
[Narrator] Confound him! [/]
[Narrator] the father breathed hotly to himself. [/]
[Narrator] Thus it will be seen that unmixed pleasure is not to be had in this world, even in the midst of envious friends and the most splendid entertainment which money could supply. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER IV. [/]
[Narrator] A COUNTRY PARTY. [/]
[SirRobert] "Very funny, now,"said Sir Robert. [/]
[SirRobert] "I do n't know that such a thing ever happened to me before. [/]
[SirRobert] Give you my word for it, I did n't know a single soul, not one; and there must have been a couple of hundred or so there. [/]
[SirRobert] Jove! [/]
[SirRobert] I never thought there were as many people in England that I did n't know." [/]
[Sophy] "How could you know Mr. Copperhead's friends?"said Sophy Dorset. [/]
[Sophy] "What I wonder is, that she should have asked us. [/]
[Sophy] Not but that it was amusing enough, once in a way, just to see how such people look. [/]
[Narrator] ""They looked very much like other people, my dear. [/]
[SirRobert] Finer, though. [/]
[SirRobert] I have n't seen so many jewels at an evening party for ages. [/]
[SirRobert] Very much like other people. [/]
[SirRobert] Fatter, perhaps, the men, but not the women. [/]
[SirRobert] I notice,"said Sir Robert, who himself was spare,"that City men generally have a tendency to fat. [/]
[Narrator] ""They are so rich,"said Miss Dorset, with gentle disgust. [/]
[Narrator] She was the quiet one, never saying much. [/]
[Narrator] Sophy, who was lively, conducted the conversation. [/]
[Narrator] They were all seated at breakfast, later than usual, on the morning after the Copperheads'ball. [/]
[Sophy] It was a hazy morning, and the party were seated in a large sitting - room in the"very central"locality of Suffolk Street, looking down that straight little street upon the stream of carriages and omnibuses in the foggy distance. [/]
[Narrator] It was not for pleasure that this country party had come to London. [/]
[Narrator] Sir Robert's second son, who was in India, had sent his eldest children home to the care of his father and sisters. [/]
[Narrator] They were expected at Portsmouth daily, and the aunts, somewhat excited by the prospect of their charge, had insisted upon coming to town to receive them. [/]
[Narrator] As for Ursula May, who was a poor relation on the late Lady Dorset's side, as Mrs. Copperhead had been a poor relation on Sir Robert's, London at any season was a wonder and excitement to her, and she could not sufficiently thank the kind relations who had given her this holiday in her humdrum life. [/]
[Narrator] She was the daughter of a poor clergyman in the little town of Carlingford, a widower with a large family. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula was the eldest daughter, with the duties of a mother on her much burdened hands; and she had no special inclination towards these duties, so that a week's escape from them was a relief to her at any time. [/]
[Narrator] And a ball! [/]
[Narrator] But the ball had not been so beatific as Ursula hoped. [/]
[Narrator] In her dark blue serge dress, close up to the throat and down to the wrists, she did not look so pale as she had done in her snow - white garments on the previous night; but she was at the best of times a shadowy little person, with soft, dark brown hair, dark brown eyes, and no more colour than the faintest of wild rose tints; but the youthfulness, and softness, and roundness of the girl showed to full advantage beside the more angular development of the Miss Dorsets, who were tall, and had lost the first smooth curves of youth. [/]
[Narrator] To Ursula, not yet twenty, these ladies looked very mature, almost aged, being one of them ten, and the other eight years older than herself. [/]
[Narrator] She looked up to them with great respect; but she felt, all the same -- how could she help it? --that in some things, though the Miss Dorsets were her superiors, it was best to be Ursula May. [/]
[SirRobert] "Poor Clara!"said Sir Robert. [/]
[SirRobert] "She was always a frightened creature. [/]
[Narrator] When I recollect her, a poor little governess, keeping behind backs at the nursery parties -- and to see her in all her splendour now!""She would keep behind backs still, if she could,"said Miss Dorset. [/]
[Sophy] "Think of that, Ursula,"cried Sophy;"there is an example for you. [/]
[Sophy] She was a great deal worse off than you are; and to see her now, as papa says! [/]
[Sophy] You may have a house in Portland Place too, and ask us to balls, and wear diamonds. [/]
[Sophy] Think of that! [/]
[Narrator] Though last night you looked as frightened as she.""Do n't put such demoralizing ideas into the child's head. [/]
[MissDorset] How it is that girls are not ruined,"said Miss Dorset, shaking her head,"ruined! [/]
[MissDorset] by such examples, I can not tell. [/]
[MissDorset] They must have stronger heads than we think. [/]
[MissDorset] As poor as Cinderella one day, and the next as rich as the Queen -- without any merit of theirs, all because some chance man happens to take a fancy to them. [/]
[Narrator] ""Quite right,"said Sir Robert;"quite right, my dear. [/]
[SirRobert] It is the natural course of affairs. [/]
[SirRobert] "Miss Dorset shook her head. [/]
[Narrator] She went on shaking her head as she poured out the tea. [/]
[Narrator] She was not given to eloquence, but the subject inspired her. [/]
[TheElderSister] "Do n't think of it, Ursula; it is not the sort of thing that good girls ought to think of,"and the elder sister made signs to Sophy, who was reckless, and did not mind the moral effect of the suggestion. [/]
[Sophy] "Poor Mrs. Copperhead! [/]
[Sophy] I shall never have a house in Portland Place, nor any diamonds, except Aunt Mary's old brooch. [/]
[Sophy] I shall live and die an old maid, and nobody will waste a thought upon me,"said Sophy, who made this prophecy at her ease, not expecting it to come true;"but I do n't envy poor Clara, and if you marry such a man as Mr. Copperhead, though I shall admire you very much, Ursula, I sha n't envy you. [/]
[Narrator] ""Is young Mr. Copperhead as bad as his father?"said Ursula, simply. [/]
[Narrator] She was so far from thinking what meaning could be attached to her words, that she stopped and looked, wondering, from one to another when they laughed. [/]
[SirRobert] "Ha! ha! ha!"said Sir Robert;"not so bad, either! [/]
[SirRobert] "Poor Ursula was extremely serious. [/]
[Narrator] She turned with relief to Miss Dorset, who was serious too. [/]
[Anne] "My dear, we do n't know much about Clarence; he is a heavy young man. [/]
[Anne] I do n't think he is attractive. [/]

[Narrator] The girl did not understand why the simple little epistle should be so interesting, nor did she perceive yet what the laughter was about. [/]
[Narrator] To tell the truth, Ursula, who was not clever, had thought young Mr. Copperhead very _ nice _. [/]
[Narrator] He had asked her to dance when nobody else did; he had talked to her as much as he could have talked to Sophy Dorset herself. [/]
[They] He had rehabilitated her in her own eyes after the first disappointment and failure of the evening, and she was prepared to think, whatever might be said about the father, that the son was"very kind"and very agreeable. [/]
[Narrator] Why should they laugh? [/]
[Narrator] Ursula concluded that there must be some private joke of their own about Clarence (what a pretty, interesting, superior name Clarence was!) [/]
[Narrator] which she could not be permitted to know. [/]

[Sophy] "If you had put the idea into my head, I should not be Sophy Dorset now. [/]
