[Sophy] Can she live there for ever in that dreadful Parsonage, among all those children whom she does not know how to manage? [/]
[Sophy] Do n't be absurd, Anne; except an elder daughter like you here and there, you know, girls must marry if they are to be of any consequence in the world. [/]
[Narrator] Let them get it into their heads; we ca n't change what is the course of nature, as papa says.""Oh, Sophy! [/]
[Anne] it is so unwomanly. [/]
[Narrator] ""Never mind; when a man chuckles and jeers at me because I am unmarried, I think it is unmanly; but they all do it, and no one finds any fault.""Not all surely; not near _ all _. [/]
[Narrator] ""Do n't they? [/]
[Sophy] Not to our faces, perhaps; but whenever they write, whenever they speak in public. [/]
[Sophy] When men are so mean, why should we train girls up to unnatural high - mindedness? [/]
[Sophy] Why, that is the sort of girl who ought to make a good marriage; to'catch'somebody, or have somebody'hooked'for her. [/]
[Sophy] She is pretty, and soft, and not very wise. [/]
[Sophy] I am doing the very best thing in the world for her, when I laugh at love and all that nonsense, and put a good match into her mind. [/]
[Sophy] "Miss Dorset turned away with a sigh, and shook her head. [/]
[Narrator] It was all she could do. [/]
[Narrator] To encounter Sophy in argument was beyond her power, and if it had not been beyond her power, what would have been the good of it? [/]
[Narrator] Sophy had a story which, unfortunately, most people knew. [/]
[Narrator] She had been romantic, and she had been disappointed. [/]
[Narrator] Five or six years before, she had been engaged to a clergyman, who, finding that the good living he was waiting for in order to marry was not likely to come through Sir Robert's influence, intimated to his betrothed his serious doubt whether they were likely to be happy together, and broke off the engagement. [/]
[Narrator] He married somebody else in six months, and Sophy was left to bear the shame as she might. [/]
[Narrator] To be sure, a great many people were highly indignant with him at the moment; his sin, however, was forgotten long ago, so far as he was concerned; but nobody forgot that Sophy had been jilted, and she did not forget it herself, which was worse. [/]
[Narrator] Therefore Miss Dorset attempted no argument with her sister. [/]
[Narrator] She shook her gentle head, and said nothing. [/]
[Narrator] Anne was the elder sister born, the maiden - mother, who is a clearly defined type of humanity, though rare, perhaps, like all the finer sorts. [/]
[Narrator] She resolved in her own mind to take private means for the fortification and preservation of Ursula, whose position, as elder sister of a motherless family, interested her especially as being like her own; but Anne owned within herself that she had never been so young as little Ursula May. Ursula, for her part, thought very little about the question which had thus moved her cousins. [/]
[Narrator] She thought Mr. Clarence Copperhead was very nice, and that if she had but known as many people, and had as many partners as that young lady in black, she would have enjoyed the ball very much. [/]
[Narrator] After all, now that it was over, she felt that she had enjoyed it. [/]
[Narrator] Three dances were a great deal better than none at all, and to have that pretty white frock given to her by Sir Robert was no small matter. [/]
[Narrator] Besides, for in this as in other things the uses of adversity are sometimes sweet, the pretty dress, which no doubt would have been torn and crumpled had she danced much, was almost quite fresh now, and would do very well at Carlingford if there should be any balls there -- events which happened occasionally, though Ursula had never been lucky enough to go to any of them. [/]
[Narrator] And Cousin Sophy had given her a set of Venetian beads and Cousin Anne a bracelet. [/]
[Narrator] This good fortune was quite enough to fill her mind with satisfaction, and prevent any undue meditation upon good matches or the attentions of Clarence Copperhead. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula was as different as possible from Phoebe Beecham. [/]
[Narrator] She had no pretensions to be intellectual. [/]
[Narrator] She preferred the company even of her very smallest brothers and sisters to the conversation of her papa, though he was known to be one of the most superior men in the diocese. [/]
[Narrator] Even when her elder brother Reginald, of whom she was very fond, came home from college, Ursula was more than indifferent to the privileged position of elder sister, by which she was permitted to sit up and assist at the talks which were carried on between him and his father. [/]
[Narrator] Reginald was very clever too; he was making his own way at the university by means of scholarships, the only way in which a son of Mr. May's was likely to get to the university at all, and to hear him talk with his father about Greek poetry and philosophy was a very fine thing indeed; how Phoebe Beecham, if the chance had been hers, would have prized it; but Ursula did not enjoy the privilege. [/]
[Narrator] She preferred a pantomime, or the poorest performance in a theatre, or even Madame Tussaud's exhibition. [/]
[Narrator] She preferred even to walk about the gay streets with Miss Dorset's maid, and look into the shop - windows and speculate what was going to be worn next season. [/]
[Narrator] Poor little girl! [/]
[Narrator] with such innocent and frivolous tastes, it may be supposed she did not find her position as elder sister and housekeeper a very congenial one. [/]
[Narrator] Her father was no more than Incumbent of St. Roque, an old perpetual curacy merged in a district church, which was a poor appointment for an elderly man with a family; he was very clever and superior, but not a man who got on, or who did much to help his children to get on; and had Ursula been of the kind of those who suffer and deny themselves by nature, she would have had her hands full, and abundant opportunity afforded her to exercise those faculties. [/]
[Narrator] But she was not of this frame of mind. [/]
[Narrator] She did what she was obliged to do as well as time and opportunity permitted; but she did not throw herself with any enthusiasm into her duties. [/]
[Narrator] To keep seven children in good condition and discipline in a small house, on a small income, is more, it must be allowed, than most girls of twenty are equal to; only enthusiasm and self - devotion could make such a task possible, and these qualifications poor little Ursula did not possess. [/]
[Narrator] Oh! [/]
[Narrator] how glad she was to get away from it all, from having to think of Janey and Johnny, and Amy and little Robin. [/]
[Narrator] She was not anxious about how things might be going on in her absence, as kind Miss Dorset thought she must be. [/]
[Narrator] The happiness of escaping was first and foremost in her thoughts. [/]

[Narrator] His nose was slightly drawn up, as if an odour of something disagreeable had reached him. [/]

[MrCopperhead] "Here's the Baronet, Clarence,"said Mr. Copperhead, who was slightly flushed;"and he doubts the Turner being genuine. [/]

[MrCopperhead] I am not the sort of man, by George! to be cheated, and they ought to know that. [/]

[Narrator] After all, a laugh could hurt nobody. [/]

[Narrator] That was all the reply she made. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER VII. [/]
[Narrator] SHOPPING. [/]
[Narrator] Next day a telegram came from Southampton, announcing the arrival of the little Dorsets, which Ursula rejoiced over with the rest, yet was dreadfully sorry for in her heart. [/]
[TheSisters] "Now we shall be able to get home,"the sisters said, and she did her best to smile; but to say that she was glad to leave London, with all its delights, the bright streets and the shop - windows, and the theatres, and the excitement of being"on a visit,"would be a great deal more than the truth. [/]
[Narrator] She was glad, sympathetically, and to please the others; but for herself, her heart fell. [/]
[Ursula] It was still winter, and winter is not lively in Carlingford; and there was a great deal to do at home, and many things"to put up with. [/]
[Ursula] "To be sure, that was her duty, this was only her pleasure; but at twenty, pleasure is so much more pleasant than duty. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula did not at all rebel, nor did she make painful contrasts in her mind, as so many young people do; asking why are others so well off, and I so badly off? [/]
[Narrator] but her heart sank. [/]
[Narrator] All the mendings, all the keepings in order, the dinners to be invented with a due regard for the butcher's bill, the tradespeople to be kept in good humour, the servant to be managed, and papa, who was more difficult than the servant, and more troublesome than the children! [/]
[Narrator] If Ursula sighed over the prospect, I do n't think the severest of recording angels would put a very bad mark against her. [/]
[Narrator] She had been free of all this for ten wonderful days. [/]
[Narrator] No torn frocks, no unpleasant baker, no hole in the carpet, no spoiled mutton - chops, had disturbed her repose. [/]
[Narrator] All these troubles, no doubt, were going on as usual at home, and Janey and the maid were struggling with them as best they could. [/]
[Narrator] Had Ursula been very high - minded and given up to her duty, no doubt she would have been too much moved by the thought of what her young sister might be enduring in her absence, to get the good of her holiday; but I fear this was not how she felt it. [/]
[Narrator] Janey, no doubt, would get through somehow; and it was very sweet to escape for ever so short a time, and have a real rest. [/]
[Ursula] Therefore, it must be allowed that, when Ursula went to her bed - room after this news arrived, she relieved herself by"a good cry." [/]
[Narrator] Two or three days longer, what difference could that have made to those children? [/]
[Narrator] But after her headache was relieved in this way, the cloud dispersed a little. [/]
[Narrator] The thought of all she had to tell Janey consoled her. [/]
[Ursula] She counted over the spare contents of her purse, and calculated that, after all, she would have enough to buy the necktie; and she had all her presents to exhibit; the ball - dress, that unhoped - for acquisition; the Venetian beads; the bracelet,"Which is really good--_good _ gold; fancy!"said Ursula to herself, weighing it in her hand. [/]
[Narrator] How Janey would be interested, how she would be dazzled! [/]
[Narrator] There was a great deal of consolation in this thought. [/]
[Ursula] In the afternoon her cousins took her out"shopping,"an occupation which all young girls and women like. [/]
[HerCousins] They bought a great many things"for the spring,"and"for the children,"while Ursula looked on with admiration. [/]
[Narrator] To be able to buy things three months in advance, three months before they could possibly be wanted, what luxury! [/]
[Narrator] and yet the Dorsets were not rich, or so, at least, people said. [/]
[CousinAnne] "Now, Ursula,"said Cousin Anne,"we have made all our purchases. [/]
[CousinAnne] Suppose you choose frocks for the children at home." [/]
[Ursula] "Oh, me?"cried poor Ursula, forgetting grammar. [/]
[Narrator] She blushed very red, and looked, not without indignation, into Anne Dorset's mild eyes. [/]
[Ursula] "You know I have not any money; you know we ca n't afford it!" [/]
[Narrator] she cried, with starting tears. [/]
[CousinAnne] "But I can,"said Cousin Anne;"at least, I have some money just now. [/]
[CousinAnne] Money always goes, whether one buys things or not,"she added, with a little sigh. [/]
[CousinAnne] "It runs through one's fingers. [/]
[CousinAnne] When one has something to show for it, that is always a satisfaction. [/]
[CousinAnne] Come, this would be pretty for little Amy; but it is you who must choose." [/]
[MrsCopperhead] "But, Cousin Anne! [/]
[MrsCopperhead] Dresses! [/]
[Narrator] If it was a necktie or a ribbon; but frocks--""Frocks would be most useful, would n't they? [/]
[CousinAnne] One for Amy, and one for Janey. [/]
[Narrator] I suppose Robin does not wear frocks now?""He has been in knickerbockers these two years,"said Ursula, half proud, half sorry;"and the worst of it is, they ca n't be made at home. [/]
[Ursula] Papa says, boys'clothes made at home are always spoiled, and the tailor is so dear. [/]
[Ursula] Oh, Cousin Anne, are you really, really going to be so very, very good--!"Mrs. Copperhead came into the shop while they were choosing. [/]
[Narrator] Poor little woman! [/]
[Narrator] she who trembled so in her own house, how everybody bowed down before her at Messrs. Margrove and Snelcher's! [/]

[ThePoorWoman] "He is so frightened for what you must think. [/]

[Sophy] She has two. [/]
[Sophy] What are their ages, Ursula? [/]
[Sophy] You, who live in town, and know the fashions, come and help us to choose. [/]
[Sophy] "And how respectful all the shopmen grew when the nameless country party was joined by the great Mrs. Copperhead -- or rather the great Mr. Copperhead's wife, at whose command was unlimited credit, and all the contents of the shop if she chose. [/]
[AHeadMan] One hurried forward to give her a chair, and quite a grand personage, a"head man,"came from another counter to take the charge of pleasing such a customer. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula could not but look upon the whole transaction with awe. [/]
[Ursula] Mrs. Copperhead was a very humble, timid woman, and Mr. Copperhead was not nice; but it was something to command the reverence of all the people in such a grand shop -- a shop which Ursula by herself would scarcely have ventured to enter, and in which she felt timid and overwhelmed, saying,"Sir"to the gentleman who was so good as to ask what she wanted. [/]
[Narrator] But here Mrs. Copperhead was not afraid. [/]
[Narrator] She gave herself up with her whole heart to the delightful perplexity of choice, and when that matter was settled, looked round with searching eyes. [/]

[MrsCopperhead] It reminds me of the days when Clarence was little, when I took such pride in his dress. [/]
[MrsCopperhead] Come with me into the cloak room, my dear, I am sure they must want jackets or something."Ursula resisted with pitiful looks at Cousin Anne, and Sophy whispered into Mrs. Copperhead's ear an explanation, which, instead of quenching her ardour, brought it up instantly to boiling point. [/]
[Narrator] Her pale little languid countenance glowed and shone. [/]
[Narrator] She took both Ursula's hands in hers, half smiling, half crying. [/]
[Ursula] "Oh, my dear,"she said,"you can give me such a pleasure, if you will! [/]
[Ursula] You know we are connections, almost relations. [/]
[Ursula] Let me send them something. [/]
[Ursula] Dear children, I wish I could see them. [/]
[Ursula] Come and look at the little jackets and mantles. [/]
[Ursula] I have often thought, if Providence had given me a little girl, what pleasure I should have had in dressing her. [/]
[Ursula] Hats too! [/]
[Ursula] I am sure they must want hats. [/]
[Ursula] Come, my dear, come and look at them." [/]
[Narrator] Ursula did not know what to do. [/]
[Narrator] A little pride and a great deal of shyness kept her back, but Mrs. Copperhead was too much in earnest to be crossed. [/]
[Narrator] She bought a couple of very smart little upper garments for Amy and Janey, and then, clandestinely taking no one into her confidence, for Ursula herself, and gave secret orders to have them all sent to the Dorsets'lodgings that night. [/]
[Narrator] She was quite transformed so long as this transaction lasted. [/]
[Narrator] Her languid countenance grew bright, her pale eyes lighted up. [/]
[MrsCopperhead] "You have given me such a pleasure,"she said, holding Ursula's hands, and standing up on tip - toe to kiss her. [/]
[MrsCopperhead] "I am so much obliged to you. [/]
[MrsCopperhead] I could almost think that Clarence was little again, or that he had got a little sister, which was always my heart's desire. [/]
[MrsCopperhead] Ah, well! [/]

[Narrator] I can put up with a great deal, but I ca n't go so far as that.""She never did any harm,"said Sophy,"poor little soul! [/]

[Narrator] To tell the truth, Mrs. Copperhead's eagerness to buy everything she could think of for the unknown children at Carlingford, the manner with which she was regarded in the great shop, her lavish liberality, her beautiful carriage, and all the fine things about her, had brought Ursula to this very thought, that it was extremely fine to marry a rich man. [/]
[Narrator] Sophy's irony was lost upon her simple - minded cousin, and so indeed was Mrs. Copperhead's pathos. [/]

[Narrator] She looked confused and puzzled at Sophy, wondering what she meant. [/]
[Narrator] Yes, indeed, to marry a rich man, to be able to buy presents for everyone, to make the children at home perfectly happy without any trouble to one's self! [/]
[Narrator] Could any one doubt that it was very nice? [/]
[Narrator] Alas! [/]
[Narrator] Ursula did not think it at all likely that this would ever be in her power. [/]

[Narrator] And presently Ursula, keeping as close as possible to her cousin's side, but compelled to make way continually for other passers - by, lost herself in a maze of fancies, to which the misty afternoon atmosphere, and the twinkling lights, and the quickly passing crowds lent a confused but not unpleasing background. [/]
[Narrator] She was glad that the noise made all talk impossible, and that she could dream on quietly as they glided and pressed their way through the current of people in Oxford Street and Regent Street, as undisturbed as if she had been shut up in her own room -- nay, more so -- for the external sights and sounds which flitted vaguely by her, disguised those dreams even from herself. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Copperhead had once been poorer than she was, a poor little governess. [/]
[Narrator] What if somewhere about, in some beautiful house, with just such a carriage at the door, a beautiful young hero should be waiting who would give all those dazzling delights to Ursula? [/]
[Narrator] Then what frocks she would buy, what toys, what ornaments! [/]
[Narrator] She would not stop at the girls, but drive to the best tailor's boldly, and bid him send down some one to take Johnnie's measure, and Robin's, and even Reginald's; and then she would go to the toy - shop, and to the bookseller, and I ca n't tell where besides; and finally drive down in the fairy chariot laden with everything that was delightful, to the very door. [/]
[Narrator] She would not go in any vulgar railway. [/]
[Narrator] She would keep everything in her own possession, and give each present with her own hands -- a crowning delight which was impossible to Mrs. Copperhead -- and how clearly she seemed to see herself drawing up, with panting horses, high - stepping and splendid, to the dull door of the poor parsonage, where scarcely anything better than a pony - carriage ever came! [/]
[TheChildren] How the children would rush to the window, and"even papa,"out of his study; and what a commotion would run through Grange Lane, and even up into the High Street, where the butcher and the baker would remember with a shiver how saucy they had sometimes been -- when they saw what a great lady she was. [/]
[Narrator] A dreamy smile hovered upon Ursula's face as she saw all the little scenes of this little drama, mixed up with gleams of the shop - windows, and noises of the streets, and great ghosts of passing omnibuses, and horses steaming in the frosty air. [/]
[Narrator] How many girls, like her, go dreaming about the prosaic streets? [/]
[Narrator] It was not, perhaps, a very elevated or heroic dream, but the visionary chariot full of fine things for the children, was better than Cinderella's pumpkin carriage, or many another chariot of romance. [/]
[Narrator] Her cousins, who were so much her elders, and who shuddered in their very souls at the thought of poor Mrs. Copperhead, and who were talking earnestly about the children they expected next morning, and what was to be done with them, had no clue to Ursula's thoughts. [/]
[Narrator] They did not think much of them, one way or another, but took great care not to lose her from their side, and that she should not be frightened by the crowding, which, after all, was the great matter. [/]
[Narrator] And they were very glad to get back to the comparative quiet of Suffolk Street, and to take off their bonnets and take their cup of tea. [/]
[Narrator] But Ursula, for her part, was sorry when the walk was over. [/]
[Narrator] She had enjoyed it so much. [/]
[Narrator] It was half Regent Street and half Carlingford, with the pleasure of both mixed up together; and she was half little Ursula May with her head in the air, and half that very great lady in the dream - chariot, who had it in her power to make everybody so happy. [/]
[Narrator] Between poor Mrs. Copperhead, who was the most miserable, frightened little slave in the world, with nothing, as she said, but time and money, and Ursula without a penny, and who always had so much to do, what a gulf there was! [/]
[Narrator] a gulf, however, which fancy could bridge over so easily. [/]
[Narrator] But the dream was broken when she got indoors; not even the quiet of her own little room could bring back in all their glory the disturbed images that had floated before her in the street. [/]
[Narrator] This was Ursula's last day in town, and there can be no doubt that it was of a nature, without any aid from Sophy's suggestion, to put a great many ideas into her mind. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER VIII. [/]
[Narrator] THE DORSETS. [/]
[Narrator] Next day the little Dorsets came, an odd little pair of shivering babies, with a still more shivering Ayah. [/]
[Narrator] It was the failing health of the little exotic creatures, endangered by their English blood, though they had never seen England, and talked nothing but Hindostanee, which had brought them"home"at this inhospitable time of the year; and to get the rooms warm enough for them became the entire thought of the anxious aunts, who contemplated these wan babies with a curious mixture of emotions, anxious to be"very fond"of them, yet feeling difficulties in the way. [/]
[Narrator] They were very white, as Indian children so often are, with big blue veins meandering over them, distinct as if traced with colour. [/]
[Narrator] They were frightened by all the novelty round them, and the strange faces, whose very anxiety increased their alarming aspect; they did not understand more than a few words of English, and shrank back in a little heap, leaning against their dark nurse, and clinging to her when their new relations made overtures of kindness. [/]
[Narrator] Children are less easily conciliated in real life than superficial observers suppose. [/]
[Narrator] The obstinate resistance they made to all Anne Dorset's attempts to win their confidence, was enough to have discouraged the most patient, and poor Anne cried over her failure when those atoms of humanity, so strangely individual and distinct in their utter weakness, helplessness, and dependence, were carried off to bed, gazing distrustfully at her still with big blue eyes; creatures whom any moderately strong hand could have crushed like flies, but whose little minds not all the power on earth could command or move. [/]
[Narrator] Strange contrast! [/]
[Narrator] Anne cried when they were carried off to bed. [/]
[Sophy] Sir Robert had escaped from the hot room, which stifled him, long before; and Sophy, half angry in spite of herself, had made up her mind to"take no notice of the little wretches. [/]
[Sophy] ""Fancy!" [/]

[/]

[Narrator] But against such an assertion what could she say? CHAPTER IX. COMING HOME. [/]

[Narrator] CHAPTER X. PAPA. [/]
[TheLittleOnesWhoHadReturnedFromTheirTeaPartyRunningToMeetTheirFatherAtTheDoorMrMayWasVeryGoodExceptByMomentsToHisYoungerChildren] "Ursula has come back!" [/]
[Narrator] cried the little ones, who had returned from their tea - party, running to meet their father at the door. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May was very good, except by moments, to his younger children. [/]
[Narrator] He was not, indeed, an unkind father to any of them; but he had never forgiven Providence for leaving him with his motherless family upon his hands, a man so utterly unfit for the task. [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps he did not put this exactly into words, but he felt it deeply, and had never got over it. [/]
[Narrator] There were so many things that he could have done better, and there were so many people who could have done this better; and yet it was precisely to him, not a person adapted to the charge of children, that it had been given to do it! [/]
[Narrator] This seemed to argue a want of judgment in the regulation of mortal affairs, which irritated him all the more because he was a clergyman, and had to persuade other people that everything that happened to them was for the best. [/]
[Reginald] He was a man of some culture, and literary power, and wrote very pleasant"thoughtful"papers for some of the Church magazines; but these compositions, though very easy to read, were only brought into the world by elaborate precautions on the part of the family, which scarcely dared to speak above its breath when papa was"writing;"for on such occasions he could be very savage, as the occasional offender knew. [/]
[Narrator] He was a man with an imposing person, good - looking, and of very bland and delightful manners, when he chose. [/]
[Narrator] But yet he had never made friends, and was now at fifty - five the incumbent of St. Roque, with a small income and a humble position in the church hierarchy of Carlingford. [/]
[Narrator] He preached better than any other of the Carlingford clergymen, looked better, had more reputation out of the place; and was of sufficiently good family, and tolerably well connected. [/]
[Narrator] Yet he never got on, never made any real advance in life. [/]
[Narrator] Nobody could tell what was the cause of this, for his opinions were moderate and did not stand in his way -- indeed within the limits of moderation he had been known to modify his principles, now inclining towards the high, then towards the low, according as circumstances required, though never going too far in either direction. [/]
[Narrator] Such a man ought to have been successful, according to all rules, but he was not. [/]
[Narrator] He was generally in debt and always needy. [/]
[Narrator] His eldest son, James, was in India, doing well, and had often sent a contribution towards the comfort of the family, and especially to help Reginald at College. [/]
[Narrator] But James had married a year before, and accordingly was in a less favourable position for sending help. [/]
[Narrator] And indeed these windfalls had never produced much effect upon the family, who heard of James'gifts vaguely without profiting by them. [/]
[Narrator] All this _ donna à penser _ to the elder children. [/]
[Narrator] Having no softening medium of a mother's eyes to look at their father through, they were more bold in judging him than, perhaps, they ought to have been; and he did not take pains to fascinate his children, or throw the glamour of love into their eyes. [/]
[Narrator] He took it for granted, frankly and as a part of nature, that he himself was the first person to be considered in all matters. [/]
[Narrator] So he was, of course -- so the father, the bread - winner, the head of the family, ought to be; and when he has a wife to keep him upon that pedestal, and to secure that his worship shall be respected, it becomes natural, and the first article of the family creed; but somehow when a man has to set forth and uphold this principle himself, it is less successful; and in Mr. May's case it was not successful at all. [/]
[Narrator] He was not severe or tyrannical, so that they might have rebelled. [/]
[Narrator] He only held the conviction quite honestly and ingeniously, that his affairs came first, and were always to be attended to. [/]
[TheMother] Nothing could be said against this principle -- but it tells badly in the management of a family unless, indeed, as we have said, it is managed through the medium of the mother, who takes away all imputation of selfishness by throwing an awful importance and tender sanctity over all that happens to be desirable or necessary for"papa."Mr. May had no wife to watch over the approaches of his study, and talk of him with reverential importance to her children. [/]
[Narrator] This was not his fault, but his misfortune. [/]
[Narrator] Bitterly had he mourned and resented the blow which took her from him, and deeply felt the loss she was to him. [/]
[Narrator] This was how he spoke of it always, the loss to him; and probably poor Mrs. May, who had adored and admired her husband to the last day of her life, would have been more satisfied with this way of mourning for her than any other; but naturally Ursula, who thought of the loss to herself and the other children, found fault with this limitation of the misfortune. [/]
[Narrator] A man who has thus to fight for himself does not appear in an amiable aspect to his family, to whom, as to all young creatures, it seemed natural that _ [/]
[Narrator] they _ should be the first objects; and as they were a great trouble and burden to him, perhaps the children did not always bear their most amiable aspect to their father. [/]
[Narrator] Both looked selfish to the other, and Mr. May, no doubt, could have made out quite as good a case as the children did. [/]
[Narrator] He thought all young people were selfish, taking everything they could, trying to extract even the impossible from the empty purse and strained patience of their elders; and they thought that he was indifferent to them, thinking about himself, as it is a capital sin in a parent to do; and both of them were right and both wrong, as indeed may be said in every case to which there are two sides. [/]
[TheTwoLittleOnes] "Ursula has come!" [/]
[Narrator] cried the two little ones. [/]
[Narrator] Amy and Robin could read their father's face better than they could read those instruments of torture called printed books, and they saw that he was in a good humour, and that they were safe to venture upon the playful liberty of seizing him, one by each hand, and dragging him in. [/]
[Narrator] He was a tall man, and the sight of him triumphantly dragged in by these imps, the youngest of whom was about up to his knees, was pretty, and would have gone to the heart of any spectator. [/]
[Narrator] He was not himself unconscious of this, and when he was in a good humour, and the children were neat and tolerably dressed, he did not object to being seen by the passers - by dragged up his own steps by those two little ones. [/]

[TheLady] Them as children hangs onto like that, ai n't got no harm in them. [/]
[Narrator] ""He's a rum un, he is,"said Mr. Tozer in reply. [/]
[Narrator] It was a pity that the pretty spectacle of the clergyman with his little boy and girl should have been thus thrown away upon a couple of Dissenters, yet it was not without its effect. [/]
[Narrator] Amy pulled one arm and Robin pulled the other. [/]
[Narrator] They were dark - haired children like all the Mays, and as this peculiarity is rare among children, it gave these two a certain piquancy. [/]
[ARetiredShopkeeper] "Well, well,"he said,"take me to Ursula,"and after he had kissed his newly - arrived daughter, he sat down in the faded drawing - room with much geniality, and took one child on each knee. [/]
[ARetiredShopkeeper] "I hope you have enjoyed yourself, Ursula,"he said;"of course, we have missed you. [/]
[ARetiredShopkeeper] Janey has done her best, but she is not very clever at housekeeping, nor does she understand many things that people require, as you have learned to do. [/]

[Ursula] The Dorsets were so kind, kinder than anybody ever was before. [/]
[Narrator] ""And, papa, they have sent me a new dress. [/]

[Amy] "You too, Mouse! [/]

[Narrator] she asked, her brother's last word still dwelling in her mind. [/]

[Narrator] He had no more idea of marrying a second time than of flying. [/]

[Narrator] The idea put energy into her. [/]
[Ursula] She ceased to be tired, ceased to feel herself,"after her journey,"capable of nothing but sitting still and hearing of all that had been done since she went away. [/]
[Narrator] In the course of the evening, however, Ursula took advantage of a quiet moment to look into the dictionary and make herself quite safe about the meaning of the word sinecure. [/]
[Narrator] It was not the first time she had heard it, as may be supposed. [/]
[Narrator] She had heard of lucky people who held sinecures, and she had heard them denounced as evil things, but without entering closely into the meaning. [/]
[Narrator] Now she had a more direct interest in it, and it must be confessed that she was not at all frightened by the idea, or disposed to reject it as Reginald did. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula had not learnt much about public virtue, and to get a good income for doing nothing, or next to nothing, seemed to her an ideal sort of way of getting one's livelihood. [/]
[Narrator] She wished with a sigh that there were sinecures which could be held by girls. [/]
[Themselves] But no, in that as in other things"gentlemen"kept all that was good to themselves; and Ursula was disposed to treat Reginald's scruples with a very high hand. [/]
[Narrator] But she did not choose that her father should attack him with all these disagreeable speeches about maintaining him in idleness, and taunts about the money that had been spent on his education. [/]
[Narrator] That was not the way to manage him, the girl felt; but Ursula resolved to take her brother in hand herself, to argue with him how foolish it was, to point out to him that if he did not take it some one else would, and that the country would not gain anything while he would lose, to laugh at his over delicacy, to show him how delightful it would be if he was independent, and what a help to all his brothers and sisters. [/]
[Narrator] In short, it seemed quite simple to Ursula, and she felt her path mapped out before her, and triumphed in every stage of her argument, inventing the very weakest replies for Reginald to make. [/]
[Narrator] Full of the inspiration of this purpose, she felt that it was in every way well that she had come home. [/]
[Narrator] With Reginald settled close by, going away no longer, standing by her in her difficulties, and even perhaps, who could tell? [/]
[Narrator] taking her to parties, and affording her the means now and then of asking two or three people to tea, the whole horizon of her life brightened for Ursula. [/]
[Narrator] She became reconciled to Carlingford. [/]
[Narrator] All that had to be done was to show Reginald what his duty was, and how foolish he was to hesitate, and she could not allow herself to suppose that _ [/]
[Narrator] when it was put before him properly _ there could long remain much difficulty upon that score. CHAPTER XI. [/]
[Narrator] PHOEBE'S PREPARATIONS. [/]
[Narrator] A few days after Ursula's return home, another arrival took place in Carlingford. [/]
[Phoebe] Phoebe Beecham, after considering the case fully, and listening with keen interest to all the indications she could pick up as to the peculiarities of her grandfather's house, and the many things in life at Carlingford which were"unlike what she had been used to,"had fully made up her mind to dare the difficulties of that unknown existence, and to devote herself in her mother's place to the care of her grandmother and the confusion of Mrs. Tom. [/]
[Narrator] This was partly undertaken out of a sense of duty, partly out of that desire for change and the unknown, which has to content itself in many cases with the very mildest provision, and partly because Phoebe's good sense perceived the necessity of the matter. [/]
[Phoebe] She was by no means sure what were the special circumstances that made"Mrs. Tom"disagreeable to her mother, but she was deeply sensible of the importance of preventing Mrs. Tom from securing to herself and her family all that Mr. and Mrs. Tozer had to leave. [/]
[Phoebe] Phoebe was not mercenary in her own person, but she had no idea of giving up any"rights,"and she felt it of the utmost importance that her brother, who was unfortunately by no means so clever as herself, should be fully provided against all the contingencies of life. [/]
[Narrator] She was not concerned about herself in that particular. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe felt it a matter of course that she should marry, and marry well. [/]
[Narrator] Self - confidence of this assured and tranquil sort serves a great many excellent purposes -- it made her even generous in her way. [/]
[Narrator] She believed in her star, in her own certain good - fortune, in herself; and therefore her mind was free to think and to work for other people. [/]
[Narrator] She knew very well by all her mother said, and by all the hesitations of both her parents, that she would have many disagreeable things to encounter in Carlingford, but she felt so sure that nothing could really humiliate _ her _, or pull her down from her real eminence, that the knowledge conveyed no fears to her mind. [/]
[Narrator] When this confidence in her own superiority to all debasing influences is held by the spotless princess in the poem, it is the most beautiful of human sentiments, and why it should not be equally elevated when entertained by a pink and plump modern young woman, well up in all nineteenth century refinements, and the daughter of the minister of the Crescent Chapel, it would be hard to say. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe held it with the strongest faith. [/]
[Phoebe] "Their ways of thinking, perhaps, and their ways of living, are not those which I have been used to,"she said;"but how does that affect me? [/]
[Phoebe] I am myself whatever happens; even if poor dear grandmamma's habits are not refined, which I suppose is what you mean, mamma, that does not make me unrefined. [/]
[Phoebe] A lady must always be a lady wherever she is -- Una,"she continued, using strangely enough the same argument which has occurred to her historian,"is not less a princess when she is living among the satyrs. [/]
[Narrator] Of course, I am not like Una -- and neither are they like the wild people in the wood."Mrs. Beecham did not know much about Una, except that she was somebody in a book; but she kissed her daughter, and assured her that she was"a real comfort,"and devoted herself to her comfort for the few days that remained, doing everything that it was possible to do to show her love, and, so to speak, gratitude to the good child who was thus throwing herself into the breach. [/]
[Narrator] The Beechams were in no want of money to buy what pleased them, and the mother made many additions to Phoebe's wardrobe which that young lady herself thought quite unnecessary, not reflecting that other sentiments besides that of simple love for herself were involved. [/]
[Phoebe] "They shall see that my daughter is not just like one of their common - looking girls,"Mrs. Beecham said to her husband; and he shared the feeling, though he could not but think within himself that her aspect was of very much more importance than the appearance of Phoebe Tozer's child could possibly be as _ his _ daughter. [/]
[HerHusband] "You are quite right, my dear,"he replied,"vulgar people of that sort are but too ready to look down upon a pastor's family. [/]
[HerHusband] They ought to be made to see the difference." [/]
[Narrator] The consequence of this was that Phoebe was fitted out like a young princess going on her travels. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula May would have been out of her wits with delight, had half these fine things come her way; but Phoebe took them very calmly. [/]
[Phoebe] "I have never undervalued dress,"she said,"as some girls do; I think it is a very important social influence. [/]
[Phoebe] And even without that, mamma, so long as it pleases you--"So with this mixture of philosophy and affection all went well. [/]
[Phoebe] "We must call on Mrs. Copperhead before you go; they would think it strange, after all the interest they have shown in us. [/]

[Phoebe] "Of course we must call -- and Mrs. Copperhead is a lady, but as for Mr. Copperhead, mamma--""Hush! [/]

[Phoebe] "Phoebe! slang from you -- who have always set your face against it. [/]

[Narrator] Mrs. Copperhead was very civil to the pastor's family. [/]

[MrsCopperhead] "And you can see the difference at once. [/]
[MrsCopperhead] I dare say Miss Beecham is a very clever young woman, but between her and Miss May what a difference there is! [/]
[Narrator] Any one can see it--""I am afraid then I am stupid, for I ca n't see it, mother. [/]
[MrCopperhead] They are both pretty girls, but for amusing you and that sort of thing give me Phoebe. [/]
[MrCopperhead] She is worth twenty of the other. [/]
[MrCopperhead] As sharp as a needle, and plenty to say for herself. [/]
[Narrator] This is the kind of girl I like.""I am very sorry for it. [/]
[MrsCopperhead] I hope that is not the kind of wife you will like,"said Mrs. Copperhead, with a sigh. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "Oh, wife! [/]

[MrCopperhead] "In the mean time Mrs. Beecham and Phoebe were walking up the broad pavement of Portland Place towards their home. [/]
[Phoebe] "It is pleasant to see the mother and the son together,"said Mrs. Beecham, who was determined to see everything in the best light that concerned the Copperheads. [/]
[Phoebe] "They are so devoted to each other, and, Phoebe, dear -- I do n't like to talk in this way to a sensible girl like you, but you must see it with your own eyes. [/]

[Phoebe] "Mrs. Beecham looked anxiously in her daughter's face. [/]
[Phoebe] "Is it from prudence, Phoebe, or is it that you do n't like him, that you would n't have him if he asked you?""We must wait till he does ask me,"said Phoebe, decisively. [/]
[Phoebe] "Till then I ca n't possibly tell. [/]
[Phoebe] But I do n't want him at Carlingford. [/]
[Phoebe] I know that grandpapa and grandmamma are -- in trade. [/]
[Phoebe] ""Yes, dear,"said Mrs. Beecham, in a subdued voice. [/]
[Phoebe] "Dissenters, and in trade; and he is going to stay with the Dorsets, fine county people. [/]
[Phoebe] Do n't give him the address; if we meet by chance, there is no harm done. [/]
[Phoebe] I am not ashamed of any one belonging to me. [/]
[Narrator] But you can say that you do n't think his father would like him to be visiting me at Carlingford -- which I am sure would be quite true.""Indeed he might go much farther without finding any one so well worth visiting,"said the mother, indignant, to which Phoebe nodded her head in tranquil assent. [/]
[TheMother] "That is neither here nor there,"she said;"you can always tell him so, and that will please Mr. Copperhead, if ever he comes to hear of it. [/]
[TheMother] He thought at one time that I was too entertaining. [/]
[TheMother] One knows what that means. [/]
[TheMother] I should like him to see how little I cared." [/]
[Phoebe] "But, my dear, Clarence Copperhead would be worth -- a little attention. [/]
[Phoebe] He could give a girl -- a very nice position,"Mrs. Beecham faltered, looking at her daughter between every word. [/]
[Phoebe] "I am not saying anything against Clarence Copperhead,"said Phoebe, with composure,"but I should like his dear papa to know how little I care, and that you have refused him my address. [/]
[Phoebe] "This was all she said on the subject. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe was quite ready to allow that Clarence was everything that her mother had said, and she had fully worked out her own theory on marriage, which will probably be hereafter expounded in these pages, so that she was not at all shocked by having his advantages thus pointed out to her. [/]
[Narrator] But there was no hurry, she said to herself. [/]
[Narrator] If it was not Clarence Copperhead, it would be some one else, and why should she, at this early stage of her career, attempt to precipitate the designs of Providence? [/]
[ThatArrogantAndOffensiveLeadingMemberWhoMadeTheLifeOfTheOfficeBearersInTheCrescentABurdenToThem] She had plenty of time before her, and was in no hurry for any change; and a genuine touch of nature in her heart made her anxious for an opportunity of showing her independence to that arrogant and offensive"leading member,"who made the life of the office - bearers in the Crescent a burden to them. [/]
[Narrator] If she could only so drive him into a corner, that he should be obliged to come to her in his despair, and beg her to accept his son's hand to save him from going off in a galloping consumption, that would have been a triumph after Phoebe's heart. [/]
[Narrator] To be sure this was a perfectly vain and wildly romantic hope -- it was the only bit of wild and girlish romance in the bosom of a very well - educated, well - intentioned, and sensible young woman. [/]
[Narrator] She had seen her parents put up with the arrogance of the millionnaire for a long time without rebelling any more than they did; but Mr. Copperhead had gone further than Phoebe could bear; and thoroughly as she understood her own position, and all its interests, this one vain fancy had found a footing in her mind. [/]
[Narrator] If she could but humble him and make him sue to her. [/]
[Narrator] It was not likely, but for such a triumph the sensible Phoebe would have done much. [/]
[Narrator] It was the one point on which she was silly, but on that she was as silly as any cynic could desire. [/]
[Narrator] And thus with a huge trunk full of charming dresses, a dressing - case fit for any bride, the prettiest travelling costume imaginable, and everything about her fit, Mrs. Beecham fondly thought, for a duke's daughter, Phoebe junior took her departure, to be the comfort of her grandmamma, and to dazzle Carlingford. [/]
[Narrator] Her fond parents accompanied her to the station and placed her in a carriage, and fee'd a guard heavily to take care of and watch over her. [/]
[HerFondParents] "Not but that Phoebe might be safely trusted to take care of herself anywhere,"they said. [/]
[Narrator] In which expression of their pride in their daughter, the observant reader may see a proof of their own origin from the humbler classes. [/]
[Narrator] They would probably have prided themselves on her timidity and helplessness had they been a little better born. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER XII. GRANGE LANE. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. and Mrs. Tozer had retired from business several years before. [/]
[Narrator] They had given up the shop with its long established connection, and all its advantages, to Tom, their son, finding themselves to have enough to live upon in ease, and indeed luxury; and though Mrs. Tozer found the house in Grange Lane shut in by the garden walls to be much duller than her rooms over the shop in High Street, where she saw everything that was going on, yet the increase in gentility was unquestionable. [/]
[Narrator] The house which they were fortunate enough to secure in this desirable locality had been once in the occupation of Lady Weston, and there was accordingly an aroma of high life about it, although somebody less important had lived in it in the mean time, and it had fallen into a state of considerable dilapidation, which naturally made it cheaper. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Tozer had solidly repaired all that was necessary for comfort, but he had not done anything in those external points of paint and decoration, which tells so much in the aspect of a house. [/]
[Narrator] Lady Weston's taste had been florid, and the walls continued as she had left them, painted and papered with faded wreaths, which were apt to look dissipated, as they ought to have been refreshed and renewed years before. [/]
[Narrator] But outside, where the wreaths do not fade, there was a delightful garden charmingly laid out, in which Lady Weston had once held her garden parties, and where the crocuses and other spring bulbs, which had been put in with a lavish hand, during Lady Weston's extravagant reign, had already begun to blow. [/]
[Narrator] The violets were peeping out from among their leaves on a sheltered bank, and Christmas roses, overblown, making a great show with their great white stars, in a corner. [/]
[Narrator] Tozer himself soon took a great interest in this little domain out of doors, and was for ever pottering about the flowers, obeying, with the servility of ignorance, the gardener's injunctions. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Tozer, however, who was in weak health, and consequently permitted to be somewhat cross and contradictory, regretted the High Street. [/]
[MrsTozer] "Talk of a garden,"she said,"a thing as never changes except according to the seasons! [/]
[MrsTozer] Up in the town there was never a day the same, something always happening -- Soldiers marching through, or Punch and Judy, or a row at the least. [/]
[MrsTozer] It is the cheerfullest place in the whole world, I do believe; shut up here may do for the gentry, but I likes the streets and what's going on. [/]
[MrsTozer] You may call me vulgar if you please, but so I do." [/]
[Narrator] Tozer prudently said nothing to such outbursts except a soothing exhortation to wait till summer, when she would find the benefit of the fresh air, not to speak of the early vegetables; and he himself found the garden an unspeakable resource. [/]
[Narrator] At first, indeed, he would stroll up to the shop of a morning, especially if any new consignment of first - rate York hams, or cheese, was coming in, which he loved to turn over and test by smell and touch; but by and by the ancient butterman made a discovery, such as we are all apt to make when we get old and step out of the high road of life. [/]
[Narrator] He found out that his son did not appreciate his advice, and that Mrs. Tom cared still less for his frequent appearances. [/]
[MrsTozer] Indeed, he himself once saw her bounce out of the shop as he entered, exclaiming audibly,"Here's that fussy old man again." [/]
[Narrator] Tozer was an old man, it is true, but nobody (under eighty) cares to have the epithet flung in his teeth; and to be in the way is always unpleasant. [/]
[Narrator] He had self - command enough to say nothing about it, except in a very modified shape to his wife, who was ready enough to believe anything unpleasant about Mrs. Tom; but he took to gardening with ardour from that day; and learned all about the succession of the flowers, and how long one set lasted, and which kind should be put into the ground next. [/]
[Narrator] He would even take off his coat and do a tolerable day's work under the gardener's direction, to the great advantage of his health and temper, while Mrs. Tozer grumbled upstairs. [/]
[Narrator] She was getting more and more helpless about the house, unable to see after the stout maid - of - all - work, who in her turn grumbled much at the large house, for which one maid was not enough. [/]
[Narrator] Many altercations took place in consequence between the mistress and servant. [/]
[MrsTozer] "The ungrateful hussy has n't even as many rooms to do as she had in the High Street, when there was the'prentices'beds to make,"Mrs. Tozer said indignantly to her husband; but Jane on her side pointed to the length of passage, the stairs, the dining and drawing - rooms, where there had once only been a parlour. [/]
[Jane] "Cook and'ousemaid's little enough,"said Jane;"there did ought to be a man in this kind of'ouse; but as there's only two in family, should n't say nothing if I had a girl under me. [/]
[Jane] "Things were gravitating towards this girl at the time of Phoebe's arrival; but nothing had as yet been finally decided upon. [/]
[Narrator] Jane, however, had bestirred herself to get the young lady's room ready with something like alacrity. [/]
[TheGirl] A young person coming to the house promised a little movement and change, which was always something, and Jane had no doubt that Phoebe would be on her side in respect to the"girl." [/]
[Jane] "She'll want waiting upon, and there'll always be sending of errands,"Jane said to herself. [/]
[Jane] She knew by experience"what young'uns is in a house. [/]
[Jane] "There was something, perhaps, in all the preparations for her departure which had thrown dust in Phoebe Beecham's eyes. [/]
[Jane] She had been too sharp - sighted not to see into her mother's qualms and hesitations about her visit to Carlingford, and the repeated warnings of both parents as to the"difference from what she had been accustomed to;"and she thought she had fully prepared herself for what she was to encounter. [/]
[Narrator] But probably the elaborate outfit provided by her mother and the importance attached to her journey had to some degree obliterated this impression, for it is certain that when Phoebe saw an old man in a shabby coat, with a wisp of a large white neckcloth round his throat, watching anxiously for the arrival of the train as it came up, she sustained a shock which she had not anticipated. [/]
[Narrator] It was about five years since she had seen her grandfather, an interval due to hazard rather than purpose, though, on the whole, the elder Beechams had not been sorry to keep their parents and their children apart. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe, however, knew her grandfather perfectly well as soon as she saw him, though he had not perceived her, and was wandering anxiously up and down in search of her. [/]
[Narrator] She held back in her corner for the moment, to overcome the shock. [/]
[Narrator] Yes, there could be no doubt about it; there he was, he whom she was going to visit, under whose auspices she was about to appear in Carlingford. [/]
[Narrator] He was not even like an old Dissenting minister, which had been her childish notion of him. [/]
[Narrator] He looked neither more nor less than what he was, an old shopkeeper, very decent and respectable, but a little shabby and greasy, like the men whose weekly bills she had been accustomed to pay for her mother. [/]
[HerGrandfather] She felt an instant conviction that he would call her"Ma'am,"if she went up to him, and think her one of the quality. [/]
[Narrator] Poor Phoebe! [/]
[Narrator] she sat back in her corner and gave a gasp of horror and dismay, but having done this, she was herself again. [/]
[Narrator] She gave herself a shake, like one who is about to take a plunge, rose lightly to her feet, took up her bag, and stepped out of the carriage, just as Mr. Tozer strolled anxiously past for the third time. [/]
[Phoebe] "Grandpapa!" [/]
[Narrator] she cried with a smile. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Tozer was almost as much taken aback by this apparition as Phoebe herself had been. [/]
[Narrator] He knew that his daughter had made great strides in social elevation, and that her children, when he had seen them last, had been quite like"gentlefolk's children;"but to see this young princess step forth graciously out of a first - class carriage, and address him as"grandpapa,"took away his breath. [/]
[MrTozer] "Why -- why -- why, Miss! [/]
[MrTozer] you ai n't little Phoebe?"he cried, scared out of his seven senses, as he afterwards said. [/]
[HisDaughter] "Yes, indeed, I am little Phoebe,"she said, coming up and kissing him dutifully. [/]
[Narrator] She was half - disgusted, he half - frightened; but yet it was right, and Phoebe did it. [/]
[HisDaughter] "I have only two boxes and a bag,"she said,"besides my dressing - case. [/]
[HisDaughter] If you will get a cab, grandpapa, I will go and see after the luggage. [/]
[HisDaughter] "Old Tozer thought he could have carried the bag himself, and left the boxes to follow; but he succumbed humbly and obeyed. [/]
[MrTozer] "She do n't seem a bit proud,"he said to himself;"but, good Lord, what'll she ever say to my old woman? [/]
[MrTozer] "He saw the contrast very clearly between his wife and this splendid grandchild. [/]
[Narrator] It did not strike him so much in his own case. [/]
[Phoebe] "How is grandmamma?"said Phoebe, blandly;"better, I hope? [/]
[Phoebe] Mamma was so sorry not to come herself; but you know, of course, she has a great many things to do. [/]
[Phoebe] People in town are obliged to keep up certain appearances. [/]
[Narrator] You are a great deal better off in the country, grandpapa.""Lord bless you, my dear, do you call Carlingford the country?"said Mr. Tozer. [/]
[MrTozer] "That is all you know about it. [/]
[MrTozer] Your granny and I are humble folks, but the new minister at Salem is one as keeps up appearances with the best. [/]
[MrTozer] Your mother was always inclined for that. [/]
[MrTozer] I hope she has not brought you up too fine for the likes of us. [/]
[Narrator] ""I hope not, indeed,"said Phoebe. [/]
[Phoebe] "No fear of my being too fine for my duty, grandpapa. [/]
[Phoebe] Do you live down this nice road? [/]
[Phoebe] How pretty it is! [/]
[Phoebe] how delightful these gardens must be in summer. [/]
[Phoebe] I beg your pardon for calling it the country. [/]
[Phoebe] It is so quiet and so nice, it seems the country to me." [/]
[MrTozer] "Ah, to be sure; brought up in the London smoke,"said Mr. Tozer. [/]
[MrTozer] "I do n't suppose, now, you see a bit of green from year's end to year's end? [/]
[MrTozer] Very bad for the'ealth, that is; but I ca n't say you look poorly on it. [/]
[MrTozer] Your colour's fresh, so was your mother's before you. [/]
[MrTozer] To be sure, she was n't cooped up like you. [/]
[Narrator] ""Oh, we do get a little fresh air sometimes -- in the parks, for instance,"said Phoebe. [/]
[Narrator] She was somewhat piqued by the idea that she was supposed to live in London smoke. [/]
[MrTozer] "Ah, the parks are always something; but I suppose it takes you a day's journey to get at them,"said Mr. Tozer, shaking his head. [/]
[MrTozer] "You must n't mind your grandmother's temper just at first, my dear. [/]
[MrTozer] She's old, poor soul, and she ai n't well, and she's sometimes cross above a bit. [/]
[Narrator] But she'll be that proud of you, she wo n't know if she's on her'eels or'er'ead; and as for a cross word now and again, I hope as you wo n't mind--""I sha n't mind anything, grandpapa,"said Phoebe, sweetly,"so long as I can be of use. [/]
[Phoebe] "And these were, indeed, the dutiful sentiments with which she made her entry upon this passage in her life, not minding anything but to be of use. [/]
[Narrator] The first glimpse of old Tozer, indeed, made it quite evident to Phoebe that nothing but duty could be within her reach. [/]
[Narrator] Pleasure, friends, society, the thought of all such delights must be abandoned. [/]
[Narrator] And as for Clarence Copperhead and the Miss Dorsets, the notion of meeting or receiving them was too absurd. [/]
[Narrator] But Duty remained, and Phoebe felt herself capable of the sacrifice demanded from her. [/]
[Narrator] That confidence in herself which we have already indicated as a marked feature in her character, gave her the consoling certainty that she could not suffer from association with her humble relations. [/]
[Narrator] Whosoever saw her must do her justice, and that serene conviction preserved her from all the throes of uneasy pride which afflict inferior minds in similar circumstances. [/]
[Narrator] She had no wish to exhibit her grandfather and grandmother in their lowliness, nor to be ostentatious of her homely origin, as some people are in the very soreness of wounded pride; but if hazard produced the butterman in the midst of the finest of her acquaintances, Phoebe would still have been perfectly at her ease. [/]
[Narrator] She would be herself, whatever happened. [/]
[Narrator] In the mean time, however, it was apparent that Duty was what she had to look to; Duty, and that alone. [/]
[Narrator] She had come here, not to amuse herself, not to please herself, but to do her duty; and having thus concluded upon her object, she felt comparatively happy, and at her ease. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Tozer had put on her best cap, which was a very gorgeous creation. [/]
[MrsTozer] She had dressed herself as if for a party, with a large brooch, enclosing a curl of various coloured hair cut from the heads of her children in early life, which fastened a large worked collar over a dress of copper - coloured silk, and she rustled and shook a good deal as she came downstairs into the garden to meet her grandchild, with some excitement and sense of the"difference"which could not but be felt on one side as well as on the other. [/]
[TheYoungLady] She, too, was somewhat frightened by the appearance of the young lady, who was her Phoebe's child, yet was so unlike any other scion of the Tozer race; and felt greatly disposed to curtsey and say"Ma'am"to her. [/]
[MrsTozer] "You've grown a deal and changed a deal since I saw you last,"she said, restraining this impression, and receiving Phoebe's kiss with gratified, yet awe - struck feeling; and then her respectful alarm getting too much for her, she added, faltering,"You'll find us but humble folks; perhaps not altogether what you've been used to--"Phoebe did not think it expedient to make any reply to this outburst of humility. [/]
[Phoebe] "Grandmamma, I am afraid you have over - exerted yourself, coming downstairs to meet me,"she said, taking the old lady's hand, and drawing it within her arm. [/]
[Phoebe] "Yes, I have grown; I am tall enough to be of some use; but you must not treat me as if I were a stranger. [/]
[Phoebe] No, no; never mind my room. [/]
[Phoebe] I am not tired; the journey is nothing. [/]
[Phoebe] Let me take you back to your chair and make you comfortable. [/]
[Phoebe] I feel myself quite at home already. [/]
[Phoebe] The only odd thing is that I have never been here before. [/]
[Phoebe] ""Ah, my dear, your mother thought too much of you to send you to the likes of us; that's the secret of it. [/]
[Phoebe] She was always fond of fine folks, was my Phoebe; and I do n't blame her, bringing you up quite the lady as she's done." [/]
[Phoebe] "You must not find fault with mamma,"said Phoebe, smiling. [/]
[Phoebe] "What a nice cozy room! [/]
[Phoebe] This is the dining - room, I suppose; and here is your cushion, and your footstool at this nice window. [/]
[Phoebe] How pleasant it is, with the crocuses in all the borders already! [/]
[Narrator] I am not at all tired; but I am sure it must be tea - time, and I should so like a cup of tea.""We thought,"said Mrs. Tozer,"as perhaps you might n't be used to tea at this time of day. [/]
[Narrator] ""Oh, it is the right time; it is the fashionable hour,"said Phoebe;"everybody has tea at five. [/]
[Phoebe] I will run upstairs first, and take off my hat, and make myself tidy. [/]
[Phoebe] Jane -- is that her name? --don't trouble, grandmamma; Jane will show me the way." [/]
[MrTozer] "Well?"said Mr. Tozer to Mrs. Tozer, as Phoebe disappeared. [/]
[Narrator] The two old people looked at each other with a little awe; but she, as was her nature, took the most depressing view. [/]
[Narrator] She shook her head. [/]
[MrsTozer] "She is a deal too fine for us, Tozer,"she said. [/]
[MrsTozer] "She'll never make herself'appy in our quiet way. [/]
[MrsTozer] Phoebe's been and brought her up quite the lady. [/]
[MrsTozer] It ai n't as her dress is much matter. [/]
[MrsTozer] I'd have given her a silk myself, and never thought of it twice; and something lively like for a young person,'stead of that gray stuff, as her mother might wear. [/]
[MrsTozer] But all the same, she ai n't one of our sort. [/]
[MrsTozer] She'll never make herself'appy with you and me." [/]
[MrTozer] "Well,"said Tozer, who was more cheerful,"she ai n't proud, not a bit; and as for manners, you do n't pay no more for manners. [/]
[MrTozer] She came up and give me a kiss in the station, as affectionate as possible. [/]
[MrTozer] All I can say for her is as she ai n't proud. [/]
[MrTozer] "Mrs. Tozer shook her head; but even while she did so, pleasanter dreams stole into her soul. [/]
[Narrator] "I hope I'll be well enough to get to chapel on Sunday,"she said,"just to see the folk's looks. [/]
[TheOldLady] The minister need n't expect much attention to his sermon. [/]
[TheOldLady] 'There's Phoebe Tozer's daughter!' [/]
[TheOldLady] they'll all be saying, and a - staring, and a - whispering. [/]
[TheOldLady] It ai n't often as anything like her is seen in chapel, that's a fact,"said the old lady, warming into the exultation of natural pride. [/]
[HerGrandmother] Phoebe, it must be allowed, had a good cry when she got within the shelter of her own room, which had been very carefully prepared for her, with everything that was necessary for comfort, according to her grandmother's standard; but where the"tent"bed hung with old - fashioned red and brown chintz, and the moreen curtains drooping over the window, and the gigantic flowers on the carpet, made Phoebe's soul sick within her. [/]
[Narrator] Notwithstanding all her courage, her heart sank. [/]
[Phoebe] She had expected"a difference,"but she had not looked for her grandfather's greasy coat and wisp of neckcloth, or her grandmother's amazing cap, or the grammatical peculiarities in which both indulged. [/]
[Narrator] She had a good hot fit of crying, and for the moment felt so discouraged and depressed, that the only impulse in her mind was to run away. [/]
[Narrator] But her temperament did not favour panics, and giving in was not in her. [/]
[Narrator] If somebody must do it, why should not she do it? [/]
[Narrator] she said to herself. [/]
[Narrator] How many times had she heard in sermons and otherwise that no one ought to look for the sweet without the bitter, and that duty should never be avoided or refused because it is unpleasant? [/]
[Narrator] Now was the time to put her principles to the test; and the tears relieved her, and gave her something of the feeling of a martyr, which is always consolatory and sweet; so she dried her eyes, and bathed her face, and went downstairs cheerful and smiling, resolved that, at all costs, her duty should be done, however disagreeable it might be. [/]
[Narrator] What a good thing the new fashion of five o'clock tea is for people who have connections in an inferior path of life who make tea a meal, and do n't dine, or dine in the middle of the day! [/]
[Narrator] This was the thought that passed through Phoebe's mind as she went into the dining - room, and found the table covered, not to say groaning under good things. [/]
[Narrator] She took her place at it, and poured out tea for the old people, and cut bread - and - butter with the most gracious philosophy. [/]
[Narrator] Duchesses did the same every day; the tea - table had renewed its ancient sway, even in fashionable life. [/]
[Narrator] It can not be told what a help and refreshment this thought was to Phoebe's courageous heart. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER XIII. [/]
[Narrator] THE TOZER FAMILY. [/]
[TheTozerFamily] When Phoebe woke next morning, under the huge flowers of the old fashioned cotton drapery of her"tent"bed, to see the faint daylight struggling in through the heavy curtains which would not draw back from the window, the discouragement of her first arrival for a moment overpowered her again -- and with even more reason -- for she had more fully ascertained the resources of the place in which she found herself. [/]
[Narrator] There were no books, except some old volumes of sermons and a few back numbers of the Congregational Magazine, no visitors, so far as she could make out, no newspaper but the Carlingford Weekly Gazette, nothing but her grandmother's gossip about the chapel and Mrs. Tom to pass the weary hours away. [/]
[Narrator] Even last night Mrs. Tozer had asked her whether she had not any work to beguile the long evening, which Phoebe occupied much more virtuously, from her own point of view, in endeavouring to amuse the old people by talking to them. [/]
[Narrator] Though it was morning, and she ought to have been refreshed and encouraged by the repose of the night, it was again with a few hot tears that Phoebe contemplated her prospects. [/]
[Narrator] But this was only a passing weakness. [/]
[Narrator] When she went down to breakfast, she was again cheerful as the crocuses that raised their heads along the borders with the promise of summer in them. [/]

[Narrator] Phoebe felt, in addition to all the rest, that she was to be made a show of to all the connection, as a specimen of what the Tozer blood could come to, and she did not even feel sure that something of the same feeling had not been in her mother's bosom when she fitted her out so perfectly. [Narrator] CHAPTER XIV. STRANGERS. [/]
[MrsHurst] "Well, Ursula, how do you do?"said Mrs. Sam Hurst, meeting her young neighbour with outstretched hands. [/]
[Narrator] She was a portly good - looking woman with an active mind, and nothing, or next to nothing to do, and instead of being affronted as some persons might have been, she was amused, and indeed flattered, by the suspicion and alarm with which all the young Mays regarded her. [/]
[MrsHurst] Whether she had the least intention of ever giving any justification to their alarms it would be impossible to say, for indeed to a sensible woman of forty - five, well to do and comfortable, a husband with"a temper of his own,"and a large poor unruly family, was, perhaps, not so tempting as he appeared to be to his jealous children. [/]

[Narrator] She was cordial in her manner to the Mays as to everybody she knew. [/]

[/]

[Narrator] ""That is all you know, you girls. [/]

[Narrator] I declare I think they pay dear for rising in the world, or their poor children pay dear--""You seem to take a great deal of interest in the Tozers,"said Ursula, glad to administer a little correction;"even if they came to St. Roque's I could understand it -- but Dissenters!" [/]
[Narrator] This arrow struck home. [/]
[MrsHurst] "Well,"said Mrs. Hurst, colouring,"of all people to take an interest in Dissenters I am the last; but I was struck, I must admit, to see that old Mrs. Tozer, looking like an old washerwoman, with a girl in a twenty - guinea dress, you may take my word for it, though as plain as that little brown frock of yours, Ursula. [/]
[MrsHurst] That was a sight to wake any one up."Ursula looked down at the little brown frock thus contemptuously referred to, with mingled offence and consciousness of inferiority. [/]
[Narrator] It had not cost as many shillings, and had been made up at home, and was not a shining example of the dressmaker's art. [/]
[MrsHurst] "If you value people according to what their dress costs--""I ca n't know much about her moral qualities, can I?"said Mrs. Hurst,"and I do n't suppose she has any position, being old Tozer's grandchild. [/]
[MrsHurst] But she was n't amiss in her looks, and I declare I should have taken her for a lady if I had met her in the street. [/]
[MrsHurst] It shows how one may be taken in. [/]
[MrsHurst] And this is a lesson for you, young girls; you must never trust to appearances. [/]
[MrsHurst] I confess I'd like to find out some more about her. [/]
[MrsHurst] Going in, Ursula? [/]
[MrsHurst] Well, my dear, perhaps I'll step in for a talk in the evening. [/]
[MrsHurst] You must be dull after your gaiety. [/]
[MrsHurst] Tell your dear papa,"said Mrs. Hurst with a laugh,"that I am coming to sit with you after tea. [/]
[MrsHurst] Now mind you give him my message. [/]
[MrsHurst] He does not like to miss me when I come to the Parsonage, does he now? [/]
[MrsHurst] Good - bye for the present. [/]
[MrsHurst] Till eight o'clock. [/]
[Narrator] ""Oh, how I hate her,"cried Janey,"except sometimes when she makes me laugh and I feel tempted to like her; but I always resist it. [/]
[Narrator] Do you think really, Ursula, that papa could be -- such a -- stupid--""Oh, please do n't ask me,"cried Ursula. [/]
[Ursula] "How can I tell? [/]
[Narrator] I do n't know what he may do; but if he does -- and if she does -- oh, then, Janey--""Yes, indeed, then!"said Janey, breathing hard. [/]
[Narrator] This mysterious threat seemed very horrible to both of them, though what they meant by it, it would have been very hard for either of them to tell. [/]
[Narrator] They waited within the little shrubbery whispering to each other till they heard Mrs. Hurst close her own door, for they did not want any more of her society, though they had no intention of going in. [/]
[Narrator] When she was safe out of the way, they stole out and continued their walk in the opposite direction. [/]
[Ursula] "I wanted to have gone into the town,"said Ursula. [/]
[Ursula] "It _ is _ hard to have that woman next door; one ca n't go anywhere or do anything! [/]
[Ursula] I wanted some braid for your new frock, Janey, and twist to make the button - holes; but if we had said we were going up into Carlingford, she would have come too. [/]
[Ursula] Never mind; a walk is better than nothing. [/]
[Ursula] Walk fast, and let us try how far we can go before tea. [/]
[Ursula] "Upon this idea the two girls set out walking as if for a race, which did them all the good in the world, quickening the blood in their veins, sending the colour to their cheeks, and dispersing all the cobwebs from their minds, since they soon got into the spirit of the race, and pursued it with eagerness, with little outbursts of laughter, and breathless adjurations to each other to keep within the proper pace, and not to run. [/]
[Narrator] It was not a very inviting road along which they took their walk. [/]
[Narrator] Beyond St. Roque the land was divided into allotments for the working people, not very tidily kept, and rough with cut cabbages, plants, and dug - up potatoes. [/]
[Narrator] Beyond this lay a great turnip - field, somewhat rank in smell, and the east wind swept chill along the open road, which was not sheltered by a single tree, so that the attractions of the way soon palled upon pedestrians. [/]
[Narrator] Looking back to Grange Lane, the snug and sheltered look of that genteel adjunct to the town was comforting to behold. [/]
[Narrator] Even Grange Lane was not gay; a line of garden walls, however they may shelter and comfort the gardens within, are not lovely without; but yet the trees, though leafless, waved over the red lines of brick, and the big laurels hung out bushes of dark verdure and long floating sprays of ivy. [/]
[Ursula] "Let's turn back; perhaps she may not be at the window,"cried Ursula. [/]
[Ursula] "It is so dull here. [/]
[Ursula] "Janey stopped short in the heat of the walk, objecting for the moment. [/]
[Janey] "I wish you had not gone to London. [/]
[Janey] You never used to care for the streets and the shops; now a regular good walk is too much for you,"cried Janey. [/]
[Ursula] "With a turnip - field on one side and a potato - field on the other!"said Ursula, in high disdain. [/]
[Janey] "I tell you what!"cried Janey. [/]
[Janey] "I do n't think I like you since you came back. [/]
[Janey] The Dorsets are fine people, and we are not fine. [/]
[Janey] There are no grand parties, nor theatres, nor balls at Carlingford. [/]
[Janey] When we go out here, we go to walk, not to see things, as you have been used to doing. [/]
[Janey] I do n't know what you mean by it; nineteen years with us, and one fortnight with them! [/]
[Janey] and the fortnight counts for more than all the years!"Janey was not in the habit of restraining her voice any more than anything else about her, and she spoke this out with loud school - girl tones, reckless who might hear her. [/]
[Narrator] In most cases she might have done this with the utmost impunity, and how was she to know, as she said to her sister afterwards, in self - defence, that any one, especially any gentleman, could be lurking about, spying upon people, among those nasty allotments? [/]
[Narrator] There was some one there, however, who came down the muddy path, all cut up by the wheel - barrows, with a smile upon his face. [/]
[Narrator] A gentleman? [/]
[Narrator] Janey called him so without a doubt on the subject; but Ursula, more enlightened and slightly irritated, had her doubts. [/]
[Narrator] He was dressed, not with any care of morning costume, but wore a black frock - coat of the most formal description, with a white cravat carelessly tied, semi - clerical, and yet not clerical. [/]
[Narrator] He had a smile on his face, which, on the whole, was rather a handsome face, and looked at them, showing evident signs of having heard what Janey said. [/]
[Narrator] To be sure, he did not say anything, but Ursula felt that his look was just the same as if he had spoken, and coloured high, resenting the intrusion. [/]
[Narrator] By this stranger's side was one of the men who had been working at the allotments, whose hands were not clean, and whose boots were heavy with the clinging, clayey soil. [/]
[Narrator] When they had nearly reached the road, the gentleman turned round and shook hands with his companion, and then walked on towards Carlingford, throwing another look towards the girls as he passed. [/]
[Narrator] It would be hard to say whether curiosity or anger was strongest in Ursula. [/]
[Narrator] In Janey, the former sentiment carried everything before it. [/]
[Janey] "Oh, I wonder who he is?" [/]
[Narrator] she cried, low, but eager, in her sister's ear. [/]
[Janey] "Who can he be, Ursula, who can he be? [/]
[Janey] We know all the men about here, every one, as well as we know Reginald. [/]
[Narrator] Oh, Ursula, who do you think he can be?""He is very impertinent,"cried Ursula, with an angry blush. [/]
[Ursula] "How should I know? [/]
[Ursula] And oh! [/]
[Narrator] how very silly of you, Janey, to talk so loud, and make impudent men stare at us so.""Impudent!"cried Janey. [/]
[Janey] "I did n't talk loud. [/]
[Janey] He looked rather nice, on the contrary. [/]
[Janey] Why, he laughed! [/]
[Janey] Do you call that impudent? [/]
[Janey] It ca n't be anybody from the town, because we know everybody; and did you see him shaking hands with that man? [/]
[Janey] How very funny! [/]
[Janey] Let us run in and tell Mrs. Sam Hurst, and ask her who she thinks he is. [/]
[Narrator] She is sure to know.""Janey,"said Ursula, severely,"if you live very long, you will be as great a gossip and as fond of news as Mrs. Sam Hurst herself. [/]
[Narrator] ""I do n't care,"cried Janey;"you're just as fond of news as I am, only you wo n't confess it. [/]
[Janey] I am dying to know who he is. [/]
[Janey] He is quite nice - looking, and tall and grand. [/]
[Janey] A new gentleman! [/]
[Narrator] Come, quick, Ursula; let us get back and see where he goes.""Janey!" [/]
[Narrator] cried the elder sister. [/]
[Narrator] She was half curious herself, but Ursula was old enough to know better, and to be ashamed of the other's naïve and undisguised curiosity. [/]
[Ursula] "Oh, what would Cousin Anne say! [/]
[Ursula] A girl running after a gentleman (even if he is a gentleman), to see where he goes!" [/]
