[Janey] Who cares for Cousin Anne? [/]
[Janey] She is an old maid. [/]
[Janey] Why, if it had been a lady, I should n't have minded. [/]
[Janey] There are so many ladies; but a new gentleman! [/]
[Janey] If you wo n't come on, I will run by myself. [/]
[Narrator] How pleased Mrs. Sam Hurst will be!""I thought you hated Mrs. Sam Hurst?" [/]
[Janey] "So I do when I think of papa; but when there's anything going on, or anything to find out, I like her dearly. [/]
[Janey] She's such fun! [/]
[Janey] She never shilly - shallies, like you. [/]
[Janey] She's not an old maid like your Cousin Anne that you are always talking of. [/]
[Janey] Come along! [/]
[Janey] if anybody else finds out who he is before we do,"cried Janey, with almost despairing energy,"I shall break my heart!"Ursula stoically resisted the tug upon her, but she went back to Grange Lane, to which, indeed, she had turned her face before they met the stranger, and she could not help seeing the tall black figure in front of her which Janey watched so eagerly. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula was not eager, but she could not help seeing him. [/]
[Narrator] He walked up the street quickly, not as if he thought himself of interest to any one, but when he had got half way up Grange Lane, crossed to speak to somebody. [/]
[Narrator] This filled Janey with consternation. [/]
[Janey] "He is not such a stranger after all,"she cried. [/]
[Janey] "He knows some one. [/]
[Janey] He will not be quite a discovery. [/]
[Janey] Who is it he is talking to, I wonder? [/]
[Janey] He is standing at one of the doors, but it is not Miss Humphreys, nor Miss Griffiths, nor any of the Charters. [/]
[Janey] Perhaps she is a stranger too. [/]
[Janey] If he is married he wo n't be half so interesting, for there are always plenty of ladies. [/]
[Janey] Perhaps he has just come by the railway to spend the day -- but then there is nothing to see in Carlingford, and how did he know that man at the lots? [/]
[Janey] Oh, Ursula, why do n't you answer me? [/]
[Janey] why do n't you say something? [/]
[Janey] have you no feeling? [/]
[Janey] I am sure it do n't matter a bit to me, for I am not out; I am never asked to parties -- but I take an interest for you other girls'sake. [/]
[Janey] "Before this time, however, Ursula had found a new object of interest. [/]
[Narrator] She had not been quite so unmoved as Janey supposed. [/]
[Narrator] A new gentleman was a thing to awaken anybody who knew Carlingford, for, indeed, gentlemen were scarce in the society of the little town, and even at the most mild of tea - parties it is ludicrous to see one man (and that most likely a curate) among a dozen ladies -- so that even when she appeared to Janey to wonder, she felt that her sister's curiosity was not unjustifiable. [/]
[TheStranger] But while thus engaged in the enterprise of discovering"a new gentleman"for the good of society, Ursula's eyes and her attention were caught by another interest. [/]
[Narrator] The stranger had crossed the street to talk to a lady, who had been walking down the Lane, and whom Ursula felt she had seen somewhere. [/]
[Narrator] Who was it? [/]
[Narrator] Certainly not Miss Humphreys, nor Miss Griffiths, nor any other of the well - known young ladies of Grange Lane. [/]
[Narrator] The setting sun, which had come out suddenly after a dull day, threw a slanting, long - drawn ray up the street, which fell upon the strangers, as they stood talking. [/]
[Narrator] This ray caught the young lady's hair, and flashed back a reflection out of the shining coils which looked to Ursula (being dark herself, she admired golden hair more than anything) as bright as the sunshine. [/]
[Ursula] And in the light she caught the out - line of a pretty head, and of a nose slightly"tip - tilted,"according to the model which the Laureate has brought into fashion. [/]
[Narrator] Where had she seen her before? [/]
[Narrator] She remembered all at once with a rush of bewildered pleasure. [/]
[Ursula] "Janey! [/]
[Ursula] Oh, Janey!" [/]
[Ursula] she cried,"Listen! [/]
[Ursula] This is too extraordinary. [/]
[Ursula] There is the young lady in black!"Janey, as may be supposed, had heard every detail of Mrs. Copperhead's ball, and knew what Ursula meant as well as Ursula herself did. [/]
[Narrator] She grew pale with excitement and curiosity. [/]
[Narrator] "No!"she said,"you ca n't mean it. [/]
[ThisSusceptibleYoungWoman] Are you sure, are you quite sure? [/]
[ThisSusceptibleYoungWoman] Two new people in one day! [/]
[ThisSusceptibleYoungWoman] Why, everybody must be coming to Carlingford. [/]
[ThisSusceptibleYoungWoman] It makes me feel quite strange!"said this susceptible young woman;"the young lady in black!" [/]
[Ursula] "Oh, yes, there ca n't be any mistake,"said Ursula, hurrying on in her excitement,"I looked at her so much. [/]
[Ursula] I could n't mistake her. [/]
[Ursula] Oh, I wonder if she will know me, I wonder if she will speak to me! [/]
[Ursula] or if she is going to see the Dorsets, or what has brought her to Carlingford. [/]
[Ursula] Only fancy, Janey, the young lady in black whom I have talked so much of; oh, I wonder, I do wonder what has brought her here. [/]
[Ursula] "They were on the opposite side of the lane, so that their hurried approach did not startle the strangers; but Phoebe, looking up at the sound of the footsteps, saw a face she knew looking wistfully, eagerly at her, with evident recognition. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe had a faculty quite royal of remembering faces, and it took but a moment to recall Ursula's to her. [/]
[Narrator] Another moment was spent in a rapid discussion with herself, as to whether she should give or withhold the salutation which the girl evidently sought. [/]
[Narrator] But what harm could it do? [/]
[Phoebe] and it would be pleasant to know some one; and if on finding out who she was, Miss Dorset's little relation shrank from her acquaintance, why then, Phoebe said to herself,"I shall be no worse than before." [/]
[Phoebe] So she sent a smile and a bow across the road and said,"How do you do?" [/]
[Narrator] in a pause of her conversation. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula was too shy to feel on equal terms with the young lady in black, who was so much more self - possessed than she was. [/]
[Ursula] She blushed and smiled, answered,"Quite well, thank you,"across the lane like a child, and notwithstanding a great many pokes from Janey's energetic elbow, went on without further response. [/]
[Janey] "Oh, why ca n't you run across and speak to her?"cried Janey,"oh, how funny you are, and how disagreeable! [/]
[Narrator] would _ I _ pass any one I knew, like that!""You do n't understand, you are only a child,"said Ursula, frightened and agitated, yet full of dignity,"we have only met -- in society. [/]
[Ursula] When you are introduced to any one in society it does not count. [/]
[Ursula] Perhaps they might not want to know you; perhaps -- but anyhow you ca n't rush up to them like two girls at school. [/]
[Ursula] You have to wait and see what they will do. [/]
[Narrator] ""Well, I declare!"cried Janey;"then what is the good of society? [/]
[Janey] You know them, and yet you must n't know them. [/]
[Janey] I would never be such a fool as that. [/]
[Janey] Fancy looking at her across the lane and saying'quite well, thank you,'after she had begun to speak. [/]
[Janey] I suppose that's Cousin Anne's way? [/]
[Janey] I should have rushed across and asked where she was staying, and when she would come to see us. [/]
[Janey] Ursula, oh,"cried Janey, suddenly changing her tone, and looking at her sister with eyes which had widened to twice their natural size with the grandeur of the idea,"you will have to ask her to tea!" [/]
[Janey] "Oh, you silly girl, do you think she would come? [/]
[Janey] you should have seen her at the ball. [/]
[Janey] She knew everybody, and had such quantities of partners. [/]
[Janey] Mr. Clarence Copperhead was always dancing with her. [/]
[Janey] Fancy her coming to tea with us." [/]
[Narrator] But Ursula herself was somewhat breathless with the suggestion. [/]
[Narrator] When a thing has been once said, there is always a chance that it may be done, and the two girls walked up very quickly into the High Street after this, silent, with a certain awe of themselves and their possibilities. [/]
[Narrator] It might be done, now that it had been said. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER XV. [/]
[Narrator] A DOMESTIC CRISIS. [/]
[Narrator] The interest shown by the two girls in the stranger whom they had noted with so much attention was not destined to meet with any immediate reward. [/]
[TheStrangerWhomTheyHadNotedWithSoMuchAttention] Neither he, nor"the young lady in black,"whom he hurried across the street to meet, could be heard of, or was seen for full two days afterwards, to the great disappointment of the young Mays. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula, especially, who had been entertaining vague but dazzling thoughts of a companionship more interesting than Janey's, more novel and at the same time more equal than that which was extended to her by the Miss Griffiths in Grange Lane, who were so much better off and had so much less to do than she. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula did not recollect the name of the fortunate girl who was so much in the ascendant at Mr. Copperhead's ball, though Phoebe had been introduced to her; but she did recollect her popularity and general friendliness, and the number of partners she had, and all those delightful signs of greatness which impress a poor little stranger, to whom her first dance is not unmingled pleasure. [/]
[Narrator] She whispered to Janey about her even in the drawing - room when all the family were assembled. [/]
[Ursula] "Do you think she will call?"said Ursula, asking counsel even of Janey's inexperience, of which she was so contemptuous on other occasions. [/]
[Janey] "Call! [/]
[Janey] how can she, if she is a stranger?"said Janey. [/]
[Ursula] "As if you knew anything about it!" [/]
[Narrator] Ursula retorted with great injustice. [/]
[Janey] "If I do n't know, then why do you ask me?"complained Janey with reason. [/]
[Narrator] The room looked more cheerful since Ursula had come home. [/]
[Narrator] The fire, no longer choked with cinders, burned clear and red. [/]
[Narrator] The lamp, though it was a cheap one, and burned paraffin oil, did not smell. [/]
[Narrator] The old curtains were nicely drawn, and the old covers smoothed over the chairs. [/]
[Narrator] All this did not make them look less old; but it made their antiquity natural and becoming. [/]
[Narrator] Johnnie, the school - boy, was learning his lessons on the rug before the fire. [/]
[Narrator] Reginald sat writing, with a candle all to himself, at a writing - table in a corner. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula and Janey were working at the centre table by the light of the lamp. [/]
[Narrator] They had no time, you may imagine, for fancy - work. [/]
[APoorDressMakerWhoWentOut] Janey, with many contortions of her person, especially of her mouth, with which she seemed to follow the movements of her needle, was stitching up a sleeve of her new frock which Miss Dorset had sent her, and which a poor dress - maker, who"went out,"was at this moment making up in the schoolroom; while Ursula was still busy with the basket of stockings which she had found awaiting her on their return. [/]
[Narrator] What Reginald was doing at the writing - table was probably a great deal less useful, but the girls respected his occupation as no one ever thought of respecting theirs, and carried on their conversation under their breath, not to interrupt him. [/]
[Narrator] The little children had gone to bed, tea was over, and several hours of the long winter evening still before them. [/]
[Narrator] Janey had given over lessons, partly because there was no one to insist upon her doing them. [/]
[Narrator] Once in a week or so her father gave her a lecture for her ignorance, and ordered her into his study to do a long sum in arithmetic out of the first old"Colenso"that could be picked up; and about once a week too, awakening suddenly to a sense of her own deficiencies, she would"practise"energetically on the old piano. [/]
[Narrator] This was all that was being done for Janey in the way of education. [/]
[Narrator] She was fifteen, and as Johnnie, and Amy, and Robin were at an age when school is a necessity, the only retrenchment possible was to keep Janey at home. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula had got what education she possessed in the same irregular way. [/]
[Narrator] It was not much. [/]
[Narrator] Besides reading and writing, she had pretty manners, which came by nature like those other gifts. [/]
[Narrator] A girl is not so badly off who can read and write and has pretty manners. [/]
[Narrator] Janey possessed the two first faculties, but neither had nor apparently could acquire the third. [/]
[Narrator] The two dark brown heads were close together as they worked -- Ursula's shining and neat, and carefully arranged, Janey's rough with elf - locks; but they were more interesting than Reginald, though he was so much better informed. [/]
[Narrator] As for Johnnie, he lay extended on the rug, his head slightly raised on his two hands, his book on a level with the rest of his person, saying over his lesson to himself with moving lips. [/]
[Narrator] And now and then, when the girls'whispered chatter was silent, the sound of Reginald's pen scratching across the paper would fill up the interval; it was a sound which filled them all with respect. [/]
[Narrator] This peaceful domestic scene was broken in upon by the entry of Mr. May. [/]
[Narrator] From the moment that he closed the hall - door behind him, coming in, a little thrill ran through the family party. [/]
[Narrator] The girls looked at each other when they heard that sound, and Johnnie, without stopping his inward repetition, shifted himself and his book adroitly, with the cleverness of practice, to the side instead of the front of the fire. [/]
[Narrator] Reginald's pen stopped its scratching, and he wheeled round on his chair to give an appealing glance at his sisters. [/]
[Reginald] "What is it now?" [/]
[Narrator] he said hurriedly. [/]
[Narrator] Every one knew that when the door was closed like that it meant something like a declaration of war. [/]
[Narrator] But they had not much time to wait and wonder. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May came in, pushing the door wide open before him, and admitting a gust of chill air of the January night. [/]
[Reginald] He looked at the peaceable domestic scene with a"humph"of dissatisfaction, because there was nothing to find fault with, which is as great a grievance as another when one is in the mood for grievances. [/]
[Narrator] He had come in cross and out of sorts, with a private cause for his ill - temper, which he did not choose to reveal, and it would have been a relief to him had he found them all chattering or wasting their time, instead of being occupied in this perfectly dutiful way -- even Johnnie at his lessons, repeating them over under his breath. [/]
[Narrator] What was the world coming to? [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May was disappointed. [/]
[Narrator] Instead of leading up to it gradually by a general _ battue _ of his children all round, he had to open upon his chief subject at once, which was not nearly so agreeable a way. [/]
[Reginald] "What are you doing, Reginald?" [/]
[Narrator] he asked, roughly, pulling his chair to the other side of the fire, opposite the corner to which Johnnie had scuffled out of the way. [/]
[Johnnie] "I have come in especially to speak to you. [/]
[Johnnie] It is time this shilly - shallying was done with. [/]
[Johnnie] Do you mean to accept the College chaplaincy or not? [/]
[Johnnie] an answer must be given, and that at once. [/]
[Narrator] Are you so busy that you ca n't attend to what I say?""I am not busy at all, sir,"said Reginald, in a subdued voice, while his sisters cast sympathetic looks at him. [/]
[Narrator] Both the girls, it is true, thought him extremely foolish, but what of that? [/]
[Narrator] Necessarily they were on his side against papa. [/]
[TheGirls] "I thought as much; indeed it would be hard to say what you could find to be busy at. [/]
[TheGirls] But look here, this must come to an end one way or another. [/]
[TheGirls] You know my opinion on the subject. [/]
[Narrator] ""And you know mine, sir,"said Reginald, rising and coming forward to the fire. [/]
[Reginald] "I do n't say anything against the old College. [/]
[Reginald] For an old man it might be quite a justifiable arrangement -- one who had already spent his strength in work -- but for me -- of course there is nothing in the world to do." [/]
[Narrator] "And two hundred and fifty a year for the doing of it -- not to speak of the house, which you could let for fifty more.""Father! [/]
[Reginald] do n't you see that is just the very thing that I object to, so much for nothing. [/]
[Reginald] ""You prefer nothing for nothing,"said Mr. May, with a smile;"well, I suppose that is more fair, perhaps -- to the public; --but how about me? [/]
[Reginald] A son of three - and - twenty depending upon me for everything, useless and bringing in nothing, does not suit me. [/]
[Narrator] You are all the same,"he said,"all taking from me, with a thousand wants, education, clothes, amusement--""I am sure,"said the irrepressible Janey,"it is not much clothes we get, and as for amusements -- and education!""Hold your tongue,"cried her father. [/]
[Reginald] "Here are six of you, one more helpless than another, and the eldest the most helpless of all. [/]

[Reginald] yours and the others who weigh me down to the ground, and never try to help or do anything for themselves. [/]

[/]
[Narrator] Reginald knew that his education had in reality cost his father very little, and that his father knew this. [/]

[Narrator] He had done nothing to call forth the paternal wrath. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May could not resist this temptation. [/]

[Reginald] "Johnnie stumbled to his feet appalled; he was too much startled to say anything. [/]

[/]
[ThisImpartialFather] "I said your own room, sir,"cried this impartial father, distributing to all alike an equal share of his urbanities. [/]

[Janey] I ca n't hush, I wo n't hush! [/]

[I] "Ursula, you will help me to pack my things, wo n't you? [/]

[/]

[/]

[Ursula] As for Janey, she made a dash at the writing - table and brought him paper and pens and ink,"Say yes, say yes,"she cried;"oh, Reginald, if it was only to spite papa!"CHAPTER XVI. [/]
[Narrator] THE NEW GENTLEMAN. [/]
[TheNewGentleman] It seems difficult to imagine what connection there could be between Phoebe Beecham's appearance in Grange Lane and the interview which took place there between her and the"new gentleman,"and Mr. May's sudden onslaught upon his family, which ended in Reginald's acceptance of the chaplaincy. [/]
[Narrator] But yet the connection was very distinct. [/]
[Narrator] Not even the Mays, in their excitement over the appearance of a stranger in Carlingford, could be more surprised than Phoebe was when her solitary walk was interrupted by the apparition across the street of a known person, a face familiar to her in other regions. [/]
[Phoebe] "Mr. Northcote!" [/]
[Narrator] she cried, with a little start of surprise. [/]
[Narrator] As for the stranger, he made but two steps across Grange Lane in his delight at the sight of her. [/]
[ABrotherClergyman] Not that he was Phoebe's lover, or possessed by any previous enthusiasm for the girl whom he had met about half - a - dozen times in his life, and of whom he knew little more than that she was the daughter of a"brother clergyman;"for both Mr. Beecham and he were in the habit of using that word, whether appropriate or inappropriate. [/]
[Narrator] This was the explanation of the white necktie and the formal dress which had puzzled Ursula. [/]
[Narrator] Horace Northcote was not of Mr. Beecham's class. [/]
[Narrator] He was not well - to - do and genial, bent upon keeping up his congregation and his popularity, and trying to ignore as much as he could the social superiority of the Church without making himself in any way offensive to her. [/]
[Narrator] He was a political Nonconformist, a vigorous champion of the Disestablishment Society, more successful on the platform than in the pulpit, and strenuously of opinion in his heart of hearts that the Church was the great drawback to all progress in England, an incubus of which the nation would gladly be rid. [/]
[Narrator] His dress was one of the signs of his character and meaning. [/]
[HoraceNorthcote] Strong in a sense of his own clerical position, he believed in uniform as devoutly as any Ritualist, but he would not plagiarise the Anglican livery and walk about in a modified soutane and round hat like"our brethren in the Established Church,"as Mr. Beecham kindly called them. [/]
[Narrator] To young Northcote they were not brethren, but enemies, and though he smiled superior at the folly which stigmatised an M. B. waistcoat, yet he scorned to copy. [/]
[Narrator] Accordingly his frock coat was not long, but of the extremest solemnity of cut and hue, his white tie was of the stiffest, his tall hat of the most uncompromising character. [/]
[Narrator] He would not veil for a day in easier and more ordinary habiliments the distinct position he assumed as clerical, yet not of the clergy; a teacher of men, though not a priest of the Anglican inspiration. [/]
[Narrator] He could not help feeling that his appearance, as he moved about the streets, was one which might well thrill Anglican bosoms with a flutter of terror. [/]
[Narrator] He was the Church's avowed enemy, and upon this he stood as his claim to the honour of those who thought with him. [/]
[Narrator] This was very different from the views held by the pastor of the Crescent Chapel, who was very willing to be on the best terms with the Church, and would have liked to glide into closer and closer amity, and perhaps finally to melt away altogether in her broad bosom, like a fat raindrop contributing noiselessly to swell the sea. [/]
[Narrator] It was not, however, any feeling of this difference which made Phoebe draw herself back instinctively after the first start of recognition. [/]
[Narrator] Across her mind, even while she held out her hand to the stranger, there flashed a sudden recollection of her grandmother and her grandfather, and all the homely belongings which he, a minister of the connection, could not be kept in ignorance of. [/]
[Narrator] It was but a momentary pang. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe was not so foolish as to shrink before the inevitable, or to attempt by foolish expedients to stave off such a danger. [/]
[Narrator] She shrank for a second, then drew herself up and shook off all such ignoble cares. [/]
[Narrator] "I am myself whatever happens,"was her reflection; and she said with something like security:"I am so glad to meet you, Mr. Northcote; what an unexpected pleasure to see you here!""It is a most unexpected pleasure for me, I assure you,"he said,"and a very great one." [/]
[Narrator] He spoke with unaffected honesty; for indeed his plunge into the society of Salem Chapel had given him a shock not easily got over, and the appearance of a being of his own species, among all these excellent poulterers and grocers, was a relief unspeakable; and then he added,"May I walk with you, if you are going to walk?""Surely,"said Phoebe with momentary hesitation, and it was just at this moment that she perceived Ursula on the other side of the road, and, glad of the diversion, waved her hand to her, and said,"How do you do?""A friend of yours?"said Mr. Northcote, following her gesture with his eyes, and feeling more and more glad that he had met her. [/]
[Northcote] "I passed those young ladies just now, and heard some of their conversation, which amused me. [/]
[Northcote] Do they belong to our people? [/]
[Northcote] If you will not be angry, Miss Beecham, I must say that I should be glad to meet somebody belonging to us, who is not -- who is more like -- the people one meets elsewhere. [/]
[Narrator] ""Well,"said Phoebe,"we are always talking of wanting something original; I think on the whole I am of your opinion; still there is nothing very great or striking about most of the people one meets anywhere. [/]
[Narrator] ""Yes; society is flat enough,"said the young man. [/]
[TheYoungMan] "But -- it is strange and rather painful, though perhaps it is wrong to say so -- why, I wonder, are all our people of one class? [/]
[TheYoungMan] Perhaps you have not seen much of them here? [/]
[Narrator] All of one class, and that--""Not an attractive class,"said Phoebe, with a little sigh. [/]
[Phoebe] "Yes, I know." [/]
[Phoebe] "Anything but an attractive class; not the so - called working men and such like. [/]
[Phoebe] One can get on with them. [/]
[Phoebe] It is very unpleasant to have to say it; buying and selling now as we have it in Manchester does not contract the mind. [/]
[Phoebe] I suppose we all buy and sell more and less. [/]
[Phoebe] How is it? [/]
[Phoebe] When it is tea and sugar--""Or butter and cheese,"said Phoebe with a laugh, which she could not quite keep from embarrassment. [/]
[Phoebe] "I must be honest and tell you before you go any further. [/]
[Phoebe] You do n't know that I belong to the Tozers, Mr. Northcote, who are in that line of business. [/]
[Phoebe] Do n't look so dreadfully distressed. [/]
[Phoebe] Perhaps I should n't have told you, had you not been sure to find out. [/]
[Phoebe] Old Mr. Tozer is my grandfather, and I am staying there. [/]
[Phoebe] It is quite simple. [/]
[Phoebe] Papa came to Carlingford when he was a young clergyman, newly ordained. [/]
[Phoebe] He was pastor at Salem Chapel, and married mamma, who was the daughter of one of the chief members. [/]
[Phoebe] I did not know myself when I came to Carlingford that they actually kept a shop, and I did not like it. [/]
[Phoebe] Do n't apologize, please. [/]
[Phoebe] It is a very difficult question,"said Phoebe philosophically, partly to ease herself, partly to set him at his ease,"what is best to do in such a case. [/]
[Phoebe] To be educated in another sphere and brought down to this, is hard. [/]
[Phoebe] One can not feel the same for one's relations; and yet one's poor little bit of education, one's petty manners, what are these to interfere with blood relationships? [/]
[Phoebe] And to keep everybody down to the condition they were born, why, that is the old way--""Miss Beecham, I do n't know what to say. [/]
[Phoebe] I never meant -- I could not tell. [/]
[Phoebe] There are excellent, most excellent people in all classes.""Exactly so,"said Phoebe, with a laugh. [/]
[Phoebe] "We all know that; one man is as good as another -- if not better. [/]
[Phoebe] A butterman is as good as a lord; but--"she added, with a little elevation of her eyebrows and shrug of her shoulders,"not so pleasant to be connected with. [/]
[Phoebe] And you do n't say anything about my difficulty, Mr. Northcote. [/]
[Phoebe] You do n't realize it perhaps, as I do. [/]
[Phoebe] Which is best: for everybody to continue in the position he was born in, or for an honest shopkeeper to educate his children and push them up higher until they come to feel themselves members of a different class, and to be ashamed of him? [/]
[Phoebe] Either way, you know, it is hard. [/]
[Phoebe] "Northcote was at his wit's end. [/]
[Narrator] He had no fellow - feeling for this difficulty. [/]
[Narrator] His friends were all much better off than he was as a poor minister. [/]
[Narrator] They were Manchester people, with two or three generations of wealth behind them, relations of whom nobody need be ashamed; and he was himself deeply humiliated and distressed to have said anything which could humiliate Phoebe, who rose immeasurably in his estimation in consequence of her bold avowal, though he himself would have sacrificed a great deal rather than put himself on the Tozer level. [/]
[Narrator] He did not know what to say. [/]
[Northcote] "Miss Beecham, you know as well as I do, how falsely our opinions are formed in this respect, how conventional we are. [/]
[Northcote] What is position after all? [/]
[Northcote] To a grand Seigneur, for instance, the difference between his steward and his laquais seems nothing, but to the steward it is a great gulf. [/]
[Northcote] I -- I mean -- the whole question is conventional -- position, or station, or rank--"Phoebe smiled. [/]
[Phoebe] "I do n't think that is quite the question,"she said,"but never mind. [/]
[Phoebe] I suppose you are here on some mission? [/]
[Phoebe] You would not come to Carlingford for pleasure. [/]
[Narrator] ""Nay,"said Northcote, with a reproachful tone. [/]
[Northcote] "I should have thought you must have heard of our Meeting. [/]
[Northcote] It is for to - night. [/]
[Northcote] I have come from the Disestablishment Society with some other friends; but it has been my fate to come on before to make the arrangements. [/]
[Narrator] The others come to - day.""A hard fate, Mr. Northcote.""I thought so this morning. [/]
[Northcote] I have not been much in the way of the country congregations. [/]
[Northcote] I was confounded; but, Miss Beecham, I no longer think my fate hard since I have met you. [/]
[Northcote] Your noble simplicity and frankness have taught me a lesson. [/]
[Narrator] ""It is not noble at all,"said Phoebe;"if I had not been sure you must find out I should have said nothing about it. [/]
[Phoebe] Now I fear I must turn back. [/]
[Narrator] ""But you will come to the Meeting,"he said, turning with her. [/]
[Narrator] He felt it necessary to be obsequious to Phoebe, after the terrible mistake he had made. [/]
[Phoebe] "Not unless grandpapa insists. [/]

[Phoebe] I do n't think England would be much nicer if we were all Dissenters. [/]
[Narrator] To be sure we might be more civil to each other.""If there were no Dissenters, you mean. [/]
[Narrator] ""It comes to much the same thing; congregations are not pleasant masters, are they, Mr. Northcote? [/]
[Phoebe] I know some people -- one at least,"said Phoebe,"who is often very insolent to papa; and we have to put up with it -- for the sake of peace, papa says. [/]
[Phoebe] I do n't think in the Church that any leading member could be so insolent to a clergyman. [/]
[Narrator] ""That is perhaps rather -- forgive me -- a narrow, personal view. [/]
[Narrator] ""Wait till you get a charge, and have to please the congregation and the leading members!" [/]
[Narrator] cried Phoebe. [/]
[Phoebe] "I know what you are thinking: it is just like a woman to look at a public question so. [/]
[Narrator] Very well; after all women are half the world, and their opinion is as good as another.""I have the greatest respect for your opinion,"said young Northcote;"but we must not think of individual grievances. [/]
[Northcote] The system, with all its wrongs, is what occupies me. [/]
[Northcote] I have heard something -- even here -- this very day -- What is it, my good friend? [/]
[Northcote] I am busy now -- another time; or if you want me, my lodgings are--"A glance, half of pain, half of fun, came into Phoebe's eyes. [/]
[Phoebe] "It is grandpapa!" [/]
[Narrator] she said. [/]
[MrTozer] "You should n't speak in that tone, sir, not to your elders, and maybe your betters,"said Tozer, in his greasy old coat. [/]
[MrTozer] "Ministers take a deal upon them; but an old member like me, and one as has stood by the connection through thick and thin, ai n't the one to be called your good friend. [/]
[MrTozer] Well, if you begs pardon, of course there ai n't no more to be said; and if you know our Phoebe -- Phoebe, junior, as I calls her. [/]
[MrTozer] What of the meeting, Mr. Northcote? [/]
[MrTozer] I hope you'll give it them Church folks'ot and strong, sir. [/]
[MrTozer] They do give themselves airs, to be sure, in Carlingford. [/]
[MrTozer] Most of our folks is timid, seeing for one thing as their best customers belong to the Church. [/]
[MrTozer] That do n't touch me, not now - a - days,"said Tozer, with a laugh,"not that I was ever one as concealed my convictions. [/]
[MrTozer] I hope you'll give it'em'ot and strong. [/]
[Narrator] ""I shall say what I think,"said the young man bewildered. [/]
[Narrator] He was by no means broken into the ways of the connection, and his pride rebelled at the idea of being schooled by this old shopkeeper; but the sight of Phoebe standing by not only checked his rebellious sentiments, but filled him with a sympathetic thrill of feeling. [/]
[Narrator] What it must be for that girl to own this old man, to live with him, and feel herself shut into his society and friends of his choosing -- to hear herself spoken of as Phoebe, junior! [/]
[Narrator] The idea made him shiver, and this caught old Tozer's always hospitable eye. [/]
[TheYoungMan] "You're chilly,"he said,"and I do n't wonder after the dreadful weather we've had. [/]
[TheYoungMan] Few passes my door without a bite or a sup, specially at tea - time, Mr. Nor'cote, which is sociable time, as I always says. [/]
[TheYoungMan] Come in and warm yourself and have a cup of tea. [/]
[TheYoungMan] There is nothing as pleases my old woman so much as to get out her best tea - things for a minister; she'as a great respect for ministers, has Mrs. Tozer, sir; and now she's got Phoebe to show off as well as the chiney. [/]
[TheYoungMan] Come along, sir, I ca n't take no refusal. [/]
[TheYoungMan] It's just our time for tea."Northcote made an unavailing attempt to get away, but partly it appeared to him that to refuse the invitation might look to Phoebe like a pretence of superiority on his part, and partly he was interested in herself, and was very well aware he should get no company so good in Carlingford, even with the drawback of the old shop - people among whom she lived. [/]
[Narrator] How strange it was to see her in the dress of which Mrs. Sam Hurst had raved, and of which even the young Nonconformist vaguely divined the excellence, putting her daintily - gloved hand upon old Tozer's greasy sleeve, walking home with the shuffling old man, about whose social position no one could make the least mistake! [/]
[Narrator] He turned with them, with a sensation of thankfulness that it was in Grange Lane, Carlingford, where nobody knew him. [/]
[Narrator] As for Phoebe, no such comfort was in her mind; everybody knew her here, or rather, everybody knew old Tozer. [/]
[Narrator] No disguise was possible to her. [/]
[Narrator] The only way to redeem the position was to carry it with a high hand, as she did, holding her head erect, and playing her part so that all the world might see and wonder. [/]
[Phoebe] "I think you had better come, Mr. Northcote, and have some tea,"she said graciously, when the awe - stricken young man was floundering in efforts to excuse himself. [/]
[Narrator] Old Tozer chuckled and rubbed his hands. [/]
[Narrator] "Take Phoebe's advice,"he said,"Phoebe's the sensiblest girl I know; so was her mother before her, as married one of the most popular preachers in the connection, though I say it as should n't. [/]
[TheAdmiringGrandfather] My old woman always said as our Phoebe was cut out for a minister's wife. [/]
[TheAdmiringGrandfather] And Phoebe junior's just such another,"cried the admiring grandfather. [/]
[Narrator] Heavens above! [/]
[Narrator] did this mean traps and snares for himself, or did the old shopkeeper think of him, Horace Northcote, as another possible victim? [/]
[Narrator] If he had but known with what sincere compassionate toleration Phoebe regarded him, as a young man whom she might be kind to, he might have been saved all alarm on this point. [/]
[Narrator] The idea that a small undistinguished Dissenting minister should think her capable of marrying him, was a humiliation which did not enter into Phoebe's head. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER XVII. [/]
[Narrator] A PUBLIC MEETING. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe's philosophy, however, was put to the test when, after the young pastor had taken tea and got himself away from the pressing hospitalities of the Tozers, her grandfather also disappeared to put on his best coat in order to attend the Meeting. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Tozer, left alone with her granddaughter, immediately proceeded to evolve her views as to what Phoebe was expected to do. [/]
[MrsTozer] "I never see you out o'that brown thing, Phoebe,"she said;"ai n't you got a silk dress, child, or something that looks a bit younger - looking? [/]
[MrsTozer] I'd have thought your mother would have took more pride in you. [/]
[MrsTozer] Surely you've got a silk dress. [/]
[Narrator] ""Oh, yes, more than one,"said Phoebe,"but this is considered in better taste." [/]
[TheOldLady] "Taste, whose taste?"cried the old lady;"my Phoebe did n't ought to care for them dingy things, for I'm sure she never got no such example from me. [/]
[TheOldLady] I've always liked what was bright - looking, if it was only a print. [/]
[TheOldLady] A nice blue silk now, or a bright green, is what you'd look pretty in with your complexion. [/]
[TheOldLady] Go now, there's a dear, and put on something very nice, something as will show a bit; you're going with your grandfather to this Meeting. [/]
[Narrator] ""To the Meeting? [/]
[Phoebe] oh, I hope not,"said Phoebe with fervour. [/]
[MrsTozer] "And why should you hope not? [/]
[MrsTozer] is n't it natural as a young creature like you should get out a bit when she can, and see what's to be seen? [/]
[MrsTozer] I do n't hold with girls moping in a house. [/]
[MrsTozer] Besides, it's very instructive, as I've always heard: and you as is clever, of course you'll understand every word. [/]
[MrsTozer] Mr. Northcote is a nicish - looking sort of young man. [/]
[MrsTozer] Ministers may n't be much,"said Mrs. Tozer,"though just see how your papa has got on, my dear. [/]
[MrsTozer] Nobody else as Phoebe could have married would have got up in the world like that; you may make a deal more money in trade, but it ai n't so genteel, there's always that to be said. [/]
[MrsTozer] Now it's just as well as you should have your chance with the rest and let yourself be seen, Phoebe. [/]
[MrsTozer] Run, there's a darling, and put on something bright, and a nice lace collar. [/]
[MrsTozer] You can have mine if you like. [/]
[MrsTozer] I should n't grudge nothing, not a single thing I've got, to see you looking as nice as the best there; and so you will if you take a little pains. [/]
[MrsTozer] I'd do up my hair a bit higher if I was you; why, Phoebe, I declare! [/]
[MrsTozer] you have n't got a single pad. [/]
[Narrator] Now what is the use of neglecting yourself, and letting others get ahead of you like that?""Pads are going out of fashion, grandmamma,"said Phoebe gravely,"so are bright colours for dresses. [/]
[Phoebe] You ca n't think what funny shades we wear in town. [/]
[Phoebe] But must I go to this Meeting? [/]
[Phoebe] I should not like to leave you alone. [/]
[Phoebe] It is so much nicer for me to be here. [/]
[Narrator] ""You _ are _ a good girl, you are,"said Mrs. Tozer admiringly,"and me as was frightened for a fine lady from London! [/]
[MrsTozer] But Tozer would say as it was my doing. [/]
[MrsTozer] He would say as it was n't natural for a young creature; and, bless you, they'll all be there in their best -- that Pigeon and the others, and Mrs. Tom. [/]
[MrsTozer] I just wish I could go too, to see you outshine'em all, which you'll do if you take pains. [/]
[MrsTozer] Take a little more pains with your hair, Phoebe, mount it up a bit higher, and if you want anything like a bit of lace or a brooch or that, just you come to me. [/]
[MrsTozer] I should like Mrs. Tom to see you with that brooch as she's always wanting for Minnie. [/]
[MrsTozer] Now why should I give my brooch to Minnie? [/]
[MrsTozer] I do n't see no reason for it, for my part. [/]
[Narrator] ""Certainly not, grandmamma,"said Phoebe,"you must wear your brooches yourself, that is what I like a great deal better than giving them either to Minnie or me." [/]
[TheOldWoman] "Ah, but there ai n't a many like you, my sweet,"cried the old woman, wiping her eyes. [/]
[TheOldWoman] "You're my Phoebe's own daughter, but you're a touch above her, my darling, and us too, that's what you are. [/]
[TheOldWoman] Run now and dress, or I do n't know what Tozer will say to me. [/]
[TheOldWoman] He's set his heart on showing you off to - night. [/]
[TheOldWoman] "Thus adjured, Phoebe went away reluctantly. [/]
[Narrator] It is unnecessary to say that her disinterestedness about her grandmother's brooch was not perhaps so noble as it appeared on the outside. [/]
[Narrator] The article in question was a kind of small warming - pan in a very fine solid gold mount, set with large pink topazes, and enclosing little wavy curls of hair, one from the head of each young Tozer of the last generation. [/]
[Narrator] It was a piece of jewelry very well known in Carlingford, and the panic which rose in Phoebe's bosom when it was offered for her own personal adornment is more easily imagined than described. [/]
[Narrator] She went upstairs feeling that she had escaped, and took out a black silk dress at which she looked lovingly. [/]
[Phoebe] "But grandmamma would think it was no better than this,"she said to herself, and after much searchings of heart she chose a costume of Venetian blue, one soft tint dying into another like the lustre on a piece of old glass, which in her own opinion was a great deal too good for the occasion. [/]
[Phoebe] "Some one will tread on it to a certainty, and the colours do n't show in candle - light; but I must try to please grandmamma,"she said heroically. [/]
[Narrator] When it was put on with puffings of lace such as Mrs. Tozer had never seen, and was entirely ignorant of the value of, at the throat and sleeves, Phoebe wrapt a shawl round her in something of the same dim gorgeous hue, covered with embroidery, an Indian rarity which somebody had bestowed upon Mrs. Beecham, and which no one had used or thought of till Phoebe's artistic eye fell upon it. [/]
[Narrator] It was a great deal too fine for Carlingford. [/]
[Narrator] An opera - cloak bought in Oxford Street for a pound or two would have much more impressed the assembly to which Phoebe was bound. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Tozer inspected her when she went downstairs, with awe, yet dissatisfaction. [/]
[TheOldWoman] "I dare say as it's all very fine, and it ai n't like other folks, anybody can see; but I'd dress you different, my dear, if you was in my hands,"said the old woman, walking round and round her. [/]
[Narrator] As for Tozer, he too showed less admiration than if he had known better. [/]
[He] "I got a fly, thinking as you'd have some fallal or other on you; but, bless my heart, you could have walked in that gown,"he said. [/]
[Narrator] So that Phoebe's toilette, which would have been mightily admired in a London drawing - room, could not be said to be a success. [/]
[Narrator] She was somewhat discouraged by this, notwithstanding that she knew so very much better; and accordingly set out in the fly with her grandfather in his best coat, feeling, generally, in a depressed condition. [/]
[Phoebe] "It is clear that I must take to the pinks and blues to please them,"she said to herself with a sigh. [/]
[Narrator] She could triumph over the slight that might be shown to herself in consequence of her relations; but those sneers at her dress went to Phoebe's heart. [/]
[Narrator] The Music Hall was full of a miscellaneous crowd when Phoebe, following her grandfather, went in; and the seats allotted to these important people were on the platform, where, at least, Tozer's unacknowledged object of showing her off could be amply gratified. [/]
[Narrator] This arrangement did not, on the whole, displease Phoebe. [/]
[Narrator] Since she must be exhibited, it seemed better, on the whole, to be exhibited there, than in a less distinguished place; and all the speakers knew her, which was something. [/]
[Narrator] She sat down with some complaisance, and let her Indian scarf droop from her shoulders, and her pretty dress show itself. [/]
[MrsTozer] "I declare if that is n't Phoebe, junior,"said Mrs. Tom audibly, in the middle of the hall,"making a show of herself; but, Lord bless us, for all their grandeur, how she do dress, to be sure. [/]
[MrsTozer] A bit of a rag of an old shawl, and a hat on! [/]
[MrsTozer] the same as she wears every day. [/]
[MrsTozer] I've got more respect for them as comes to instruct us than that. [/]
[MrsTozer] "And, indeed, Mrs. Tom was resplendent in a red _ sortie de bal _, with a brooch almost as big as that envied one of Mrs. Tozer's stuck into her gown, and a cap covered with flowers upon her head. [/]
[Narrator] This was the usual fashion of the Salem ladies on such rare occasions. [/]
[Narrator] The meeting of the Disestablishment Society was to them what a ball is to worldly - minded persons who frequent such vanities. [/]
[Narrator] The leading families came out _ en masse _ to see and to be seen. [/]
[Narrator] It would be wrong to say that they did not enter into all the arguments and recognise the intellectual feast set before them; no doubt they did this just as well as if they had come in their commonest attire; but still the seriousness of the occasion was, no doubt, modified by being thus made into a dissipation. [/]
[Narrator] The men were not so fine, perhaps, because it is more difficult for men to be fine -- but they were all in their Sunday clothes; and the younger ones were in full bloom of coloured satin cravats and fine waistcoats. [/]
[Narrator] Some of them were almost as fine a sight as the ladies in their ribbons and flowers. [/]
[AnObeseElderlyMinisterWhoHadSeatedHimselfByPhoebeAndWhoseEyesWereDazzledByTheDisplay] "I suppose by the look of them this must be an influential community -- people of some pretensions,"said an obese elderly minister, who had seated himself by Phoebe, and whose eyes were dazzled by the display. [/]
[AnObeseElderlyMinisterWhoHadSeatedHimselfByPhoebeAndWhoseEyesWereDazzledByTheDisplay] "I never expected all this dress in a quiet country place." [/]
[Phoebe] "Oh, yes! [/]
[Phoebe] they are people of much pretension,"said Phoebe gravely. [/]
[Narrator] And then the proceedings began. [/]
[OldMrGreen] Old Mr. Green, the grocer, whose son had married Maria Pigeon, and who had long been retired from business, occupying a house in the country and"driving his carriage,"was in the chair; and the proceedings went on according to the routine of such assemblies, with differing degrees of earnestness on the part of the speakers. [/]
[Narrator] To most of these gentlemen it was the ordinary occupation of their lives; and they made their hearers laugh at well - known stories, and enjoyed their own wit, and elicited familiar cheers, and made hits such as they had made for years on the same subject, which was a comfortable _ cheval de bataille _, not at all exciting to themselves, though they were quite willing to excite their audience, if that audience would allow itself to be excited. [/]
[Narrator] Things jogged on thus for the first hour very pleasantly! [/]
[Narrator] the Meeting was not excited, but it was amused and enjoyed itself. [/]
[Narrator] It was an intellectual treat, as Pigeon said to Brown, and if the younger people did not like it so well as they would have liked a ball, the elder people liked it a great deal better, and the hall rang with applause and with laughter as one speaker succeeded another. [/]
[Her] It was pleasant to know how unstable"the Church"was on her foundation; that aristocratical Church which looked down upon Dissent, and of which the poorest adherent gave himself airs much above Chapel folks; and how much loftier a position the Nonconformist held, who would have nothing to say to State support. [/]
[OneOfTheSpeakers] "For my part,"said one of the speakers,"I would rather abandon my sacred calling to - morrow, or make tents as St. Paul did in its exercise, than put on the gilded fetters of the State, and pray or preach as an Archbishop told me; nay, as a Cabinet Council of godless worldlings directed. [/]
[OneOfTheSpeakers] There are many good men among the clergy of the Church of England; but they are slaves, my friends, nothing but slaves, dragged at the chariot wheels of the State; ruled by a caste of hard - headed lawyers; or binding themselves in the rotten robes of tradition. [/]
[OneOfTheSpeakers] It is we only who can dare to say that we are free!" [/]
[Narrator] At this sentiment, the Meeting fairly shouted with applause and delight and self - complacency; and the speaker, delighted too, and tasting all the sweetness of success, gave place to the next, and came and sat down by Phoebe, to whose society the younger men were all very glad to escape. [/]
[TheOrator] "Miss Beecham, you are fashionably calm,"whispered the orator,"you do n't throw yourself, like the rest of us, into this great agitation. [/]
[Narrator] ""Have you a leading member?"whispered Phoebe back again;"and does he never drag you at his chariot wheels? [/]
[Phoebe] Have you deacons that keep you up to the mark? [/]
[Phoebe] Have you people you must drink tea with when they ask you, or else they throw up their sittings? [/]
[Phoebe] I am thinking, of course, of papa. [/]
[Narrator] ""Have I deacons? [/]
[TheMinister] Have I leading members? [/]
[Narrator] Miss Beecham, you are cruel--""Hush!"said Phoebe, settling herself in her chair. [/]
[Phoebe] "Here is somebody who is in dreadful earnest. [/]
[Phoebe] Do n't talk, Mr. Northcote is going to speak. [/]
[Phoebe] "Thus it will be seen that the Minister's daughter played her _ rôle _ of fine lady and _ bel esprit _ very fairly in an atmosphere so unlike the air that fine ladies breathe. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe paid no more attention to the discomfited man at her elbow. [/]
[Narrator] She gathered up her shawl in her hand with a seeming careless movement, and let it drop lightly across her knee, where the gold threads in the embroidery caught the light; and she took off her hat, which she had thought proper to wear to show her sense that the Meeting was not an evening party; and prepared herself to listen. [/]
[Narrator] Her complexion and her hair, and the gold threads in the rich Indian work, thus blazed out together upon the startled audience. [/]
[Narrator] Many of them were as much struck by this as by the beginning of Mr. Northcote's speech, though it was very different from the other speeches. [/]
[Narrator] The others had been routine agitation, this was fiery conviction, crude, and jumping at conclusions, but still an enthusiasm in its way. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Northcote approached his subject gradually, and his hearers, at first disappointed by the absence of their familiar watch - words, were dull, and bestowed their attention on Phoebe; but before he had been speaking ten minutes Phoebe was forgotten even by her uncle and aunt, the two people most interested in her. [/]
[Narrator] It would be dangerous to repeat to a reader, probably quite uninterested in the controversy, Mr. Northcote's speech, in which he laid hold of some of those weak points which the Church, of course, has in common with every other institution in the world. [/]
[Narrator] Eloquence has a way of evaporating in print, even when the report is immediate. [/]
[Narrator] But his peroration was one which startled his hearers out of a calm abstract interest to all that keen personal feeling which accompanies the narrative of facts known to an audience, and affecting people within their own locality. [/]
[TheSpeaker] "I have only been in this place three days,"said the speaker,"but in that short time I have heard of one of the most flagrant abuses which I have been indicating to you. [/]
[TheSpeaker] There is in this town, as you all know, an institution called the College; what was its original object I do not know. [/]
[TheSpeaker] Nests of idle pauperism, genteelly veiled under such a name, do exist, I know, over all the country; but it is at least probable that some educational purpose was in the mind of the pious founder who established it. [/]
[TheSpeaker] The pious founder! [/]
[TheSpeaker] how immense are the revenues, how incalculable the means of doing good, which have been locked up in uselessness, or worse than uselessness, by men who have purchased a pass into the kingdom of heaven at the last moment by such gifts, and become pious founders just before they ceased to be miserable sinners! [/]
[TheSpeaker] Whatever may have been the original intention of the College, however, it is clear that it was meant for something more than the pitiful use it is put to now. [/]
[TheSpeaker] This old foundation, ladies and gentlemen, which might provide half the poor children in Carlingford with a wholesome education, is devoted to the maintenance of six old men, need I say Churchmen?" [/]
[TheSpeaker] (here the speaker was interrupted by mingled hisses and ironical"hear, hears")--"and a chaplain to say their prayers for them. [/]
[TheSpeaker] Six old men: and one able - bodied parson to say their prayers for them. [/]
[TheSpeaker] What do you think of this, my friends? [/]
[TheSpeaker] I understand that this heavy and onerous duty has been offered -- not to some other mouldy old gentleman, some decayed clergyman who might have ministered in peace to the decayed old burghers without any interference on my part: for a refuge for the aged and destitute has something natural in it, even when it is a wrong appropriation of public money. [/]
[TheSpeaker] No, this would have been some faint approach perhaps to justice, some right in wrong that would have closed our mouths. [/]
[TheSpeaker] But no! [/]
[TheSpeaker] it is given to a young gentleman, able - bodied, as I have said, who has appeared more than once in the cricket - field with your victorious Eleven, who is fresh from Oxford, and would no more condescend to consider himself on a footing of equality with the humble person who addresses you, than I would, having the use of my hands, accept a disgraceful sinecure! [/]
[TheSpeaker] Yes, my friends, this is what the State Church does. [/]
[TheSpeaker] She so cows the spirit and weakens the hearts of her followers that a young man at the very beginning of his career, able to teach, able to work, able to dig, educated and trained and cultured, can stoop to accept a good income in such a position as this. [/]
[TheSpeaker] Think of it! [/]
[TheSpeaker] Six old men, able surely, if they are good for anything, to mumble their prayers for themselves somehow; yet provided with an Oxford scholar, an able - bodied young man, to read the service for them daily! [/]
[TheSpeaker] He thinks it very fine, no doubt, a good income and a good house for life, and nothing to do but to canter over morning and evening prayer at a swinging pace, as we have all heard it done: morning prayer, let us see, half an hour -- or you may throw in ten minutes, in case the six should mumble their Amens slowly -- and twenty minutes for the evening, one hour a day. [/]
[TheSpeaker] Here it is under your very eyes, people of Carlingford, a charming provision for the son of one of your most respected clergymen. [/]
[TheSpeaker] Why, it is in your newspaper, where I read it! [/]
[TheSpeaker] Can I give a more forcible instance of the way in which a State Church cuts honesty and honour out of men's hearts. [/]
[TheSpeaker] "A great many people noticed that when Mr. Northcote ended this with a thundering voice, some one who had been listening near the door in an Inverness cape, and hat over his brows, gave himself a sudden impetuous shake which shook the crowd, and turning round made his way out, not caring whom he stumbled against. [/]
[Narrator] The whole assembly was in a hubbub when the orator ceased, and whispers ran freely round among all the groups in the front. [/]
[SomeOneWhoHadBeenListeningNearTheDoorInAnInvernessCapeAndHatOverHisBrows] "That's young May he means." [/]
[SomeOneWhoHadBeenListeningNearTheDoorInAnInvernessCapeAndHatOverHisBrows] "In course it's young May. [/]
[SomeOneWhoHadBeenListeningNearTheDoorInAnInvernessCapeAndHatOverHisBrows] Infernal job, as I've always said." [/]
[TheOrator] "Oh hush, Pigeon, do n't swear! [/]
[TheOrator] but it do seem a black burning shame, do n't it?" [/]
[MrsTozer] "Bravo, Mr. Nor'cote!"called out old Tozer, on the platform,"that's what I call giving forth no uncertain sound. [/]
[MrsTozer] That's laying it into them'ot and'ot." [/]
[Narrator] This was the climax of the Meeting. [/]
[Narrator] Everything else was flat after such a decided appeal to personal knowledge. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe alone gave a frigid reception to the hero of the evening. [/]
[Phoebe] "I dislike personalities,"she said, pointedly. [/]
[Phoebe] "They never do a cause any good; and it is n't gentlemanly; do n't you think so, Mr. Sloely;"and she turned away from Northcote, who had come to speak to her, and devoted herself to the man at her elbow, whom she had snubbed a little while before. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Northcote said to himself that this was untrue, and brought up a hundred very good reasons why he should have employed such an example, but the reproof stung him to the quick, for to be ungentlemanly was the reproach of all others most calculated to go to his heart. [/]
[Narrator] But nobody knew how Mr. May went home in his Inverness cape, breathing fire and flame, nor of the execution he did thereupon. CHAPTER XVIII. [/]
[Narrator] MR. [/]
[Narrator] MAY'S AFFAIRS. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May went into his study and closed the door. [/]
[Narrator] He poked the fire -- he put himself into his easy - chair -- he drew his writing - book towards him, and opened it at where a half - written sheet lay waiting. [/]
[Narrator] And then he paused, rubbed his hands softly together, and falling back again, laughed quietly to himself. [/]
[Narrator] Yes; he who had stormed out of the drawing - room like a whirlwind, having discomfited everybody, leaving the girls in tears, and the boys in a white heat of passion, when he reached the profoundest depths of his own retirement, laughed. [/]
[Narrator] What did it mean? [/]
[Narrator] Of all the people in the world, his children would have been most entirely thunderstruck by this self - betrayal. [/]
[Narrator] They could not have understood it. [/]
[Narrator] They were acquainted with his passions, and with his moments of good temper. [/]
[Narrator] They knew when he was amiable, and when he was angry, by instinct, by the gleam of his eye, by the way in which he shut the door; but this was something totally unknown to them. [/]
[Narrator] The truth was that Mr. May, like many other people, having a naturally bad temper, which he indulged freely when he pleased, had attained the power of using it when it suited him to use it, without being suspected by anybody. [/]
[Narrator] A bad temper is a possession like another, and may be made skilful use of like other things which, perhaps, in themselves, are not desirable. [/]
[Narrator] He could work himself up into fury, and launch the doom he felt disposed to launch, like a burning and fizzing thunder - bolt from a hand which was, in reality, not at all excited; and like most other people who possess such an unrevealed power, it pleased him very much when he persuaded his surroundings that it was an impulse of rage which moved him. [/]
[Reginald] He had been at the Meeting at the Music Hall,"to hear what those fellows had to say for themselves. [/]
[Reginald] "Contempt, unbounded but wrathful, was the feeling in his mind towards"those fellows;"but he felt that young Northcote's eloquence, reported in next day's papers, was quite enough to quash for ever all hopes of his son's acceptance of the chaplaincy. [/]
[Narrator] So he walked home as fast as his legs would carry him, and burst into his house, as we have seen, with a semblance of passion so perfect as to deceive his entire family and fill the place with anger and tears. [/]
[Narrator] Upon which, withdrawing from the scene of conflict, he threw himself down in his easy - chair and chuckled, recovering his composure by slow degrees. [/]
[Narrator] When, however, this private indulgence was over, Mr. May's face grew dark enough. [/]
[Narrator] He pushed his writing away from him, and pulling out a drawer in his writing - table, which was full of papers of a very unliterary aspect, betook himself to the consideration of them, with anything but laughter in his looks, or in his mind. [/]
[Narrator] Letters upon blue paper in straight up and down handwriting -- other papers, also blue, with ruled lines and numerals, for which Mr. May was more frightened than he would have been for a charge of cavalry. [/]
[Narrator] These were the very unattractive contents of this drawer. [/]
[Narrator] He brought two or three of them out in a bundle and read them over, one after another, with contracted brows. [/]
[Narrator] Debt is an idiosyncrasy like other things. [/]
[Narrator] Some people keep clear of it miraculously, some seem to drop into it without cause or meaning, and to spend all their lives afterwards in vain attempts to get out. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May was one of these unfortunate men. [/]
[Narrator] He could not tell himself where his money went to. [/]
[Narrator] Poor man! [/]
[Narrator] it was not so much he had, and there was a large family to be fed and clothed, and schooled after a sort. [/]
[Narrator] But still other people on incomes as small as his had managed to maintain their families without dropping into this hopeless condition. [/]
[Narrator] He had been in debt since ever he could remember; and to be sure it was not the pain and trouble to him that it is to many people. [/]
[Narrator] So long as, by hook or by crook, he could manage to stave off the evil day, so long was he happy enough, and he had managed this by all sorts of semi - miraculous windfalls up to the present time. [/]
[Narrator] James's remittances had been like heavenly dew to him. [/]
[Narrator] It is true that these remittances had been intended to keep Reginald at Oxford, and perhaps something of the special hardness with which he regarded Reginald arose from the fact that he had done him wrong in this respect, and had appropriated what was intended for him. [/]
[Narrator] But after all, he had said to himself, the maintenance of the house in comfort, the keeping clean of the family name, and the staving off disagreeable revelations of the family's poverty, were more, for even Reginald's comfort, than a little more money in his pocket, which everybody knew was very dangerous for a young man. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May had always a bill coming due, which James's remittances arrived just in time to meet. [/]
[Narrator] Indeed, this was the normal condition of his life. [/]
[Narrator] He had always a bill coming due -- a bill which some good - humoured banker had to be coaxed into renewing, or which was paid at the last moment by some skilful legerdemain in the way of pouring out of one vessel into another, transferring the debt from one quarter to another, so that there may have been said to be always a certain amount of quite fictitious and visionary money floating about Mr. May, money which existed only in the shape of symbol, and which, indeed, belonged to nobody -- which was borrowed here to - day, and paid there to - morrow, to be re - borrowed and repaid in the same way, never really reaching anybody's pocket, or representing anything but that one thing which money is supposed to be able to extinguish -- debt. [/]
[Narrator] When human affairs reach this very delicate point, and there is nothing at any moment, except a semi - miraculous windfall, to keep a man going, the crisis is very serious. [/]
[Narrator] And it was no wonder that Mr. May was anxious to drive his son into accepting any possible appointment, and that he occasionally railed unreasonably at his family. [/]
[Narrator] Unless a hundred pounds or so fell down from the skies within the next ten days, he saw nothing before him but ruin. [/]
[Narrator] This, it is needless to say, is very far from being a comfortable position. [/]
[Narrator] The _ sourde _ agitation, excitement, feverish hope and fear of the sufferer might well affect his temper. [/]
[Narrator] If he could not get a hundred pounds within ten days, he did not know what he was to do. [/]
[Narrator] And nobody could say (he thought to himself) that he was an expensive man; he had no expensive habits. [/]
[Narrator] He liked good living, it is true, and a glass of good wine, but this amount of regard for the table does not ruin men. [/]
[Narrator] He liked books also, but he did not buy them, contenting himself with such as the library could afford, and those which he could obtain by the reviews he wrote for the Church Magazines. [/]
[Narrator] How then was it that he never could get rid of that rapidly maturing bill? [/]
[Narrator] He could not tell. [/]
[Narrator] Keeping out of debt is one thing, and getting rid of it when you have once taken its yoke upon your neck is another. [/]
[People] His money, when he had any,"slipped through his fingers,"as people say. [/]
[Narrator] When James's remittance or any other piece of good fortune gave him enough to pay that hundred pounds without borrowing elsewhere, he borrowed elsewhere all the same. [/]
[Narrator] It was a mysterious fatality, from which he seemed unable to escape. [/]
[Narrator] In such circumstances a crisis must come sooner or later, and it appeared to him that now at least, after many hairbreadth escapes, the crisis had come. [/]
[Narrator] What was he to do? [/]
[Narrator] There was no chance, alas! [/]
[Narrator] of money from James, and even if Reginald accepted the chaplaincy, and was willing at once to come to his father's aid, there was no hope that he would have anything for some time -- for chaplains incomes are not, any more than other people's, generally paid in advance. [/]
[Reginald] He leaned back in his chair and went over again, for the hundredth time, the list of all the people he could borrow from, or who would"back"a bill for him, and he was still employed in this melancholy and hopeless enumeration, when a low knock came to the door, and a maid - of - all - work, pushing it open, thrust in a homely little man in a dusty - brown coat, who put up a hand to his forehead as he came in with a salutation which was half charity school - boy, half awkward recruit. [/]
[Narrator] Beyond this there was no ceremony about his entrance, no leave asked or question made. [/]
[Narrator] Betsy knew very well that he was to come in when he pleased, and that her master did not deny himself to Cotsdean. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May received him with a familiar nod, and pointed hastily to a chair. [/]
[Narrator] He did not even take the trouble to put away those blue papers, which he would have done if any other individual, even if one of his children had come into the room. [/]

[Narrator] "Well, what news?""Nothing as is pleasant, sir,"said the man, sitting down on a corner of his chair. [/]
[TheMan] "I've been to the bank, and it's no use my explaining, or begging ever so hard. [/]
[TheMan] They wo n't hear of it. [/]

[TheMan] That's flat,'and so indeed it is flat, sir, as you may say downright Dunstable; but that ai n't no advantage to you and me." [/]

[Cotsdean] It ai n't equal, sir, I swear it ai n't. [/]

[/]

[Cotsdean] It's enough to put a man beside himself, that's what it is. [/]

[Narrator] He was a corn - factor in a tolerable business, which, as most of the bankers of Carlingford knew, he had some difficulty in carrying along, being generally in want of money; but this was not so rare a circumstance that any special notice should be taken of it. [/]

[Narrator] There were still two days; but if before that he could not make some provision, what was to be done? [/]
[Narrator] He was not a cruel or bad man, and would have suffered keenly had anything happened to poor Cotsdean and his family on his account. [/]
[Narrator] But they must be sacrificed if it came to that, and the thought was very appalling. [/]
[Narrator] What was he to do? [/]
[Narrator] His friends were exhausted, and so were his expedients. [/]
[Narrator] There was no longer any one he could borrow from, or who would take even a share of his burden on their shoulders. [/]
[Narrator] What was he to do? CHAPTER XIX. [/]
[Narrator] THE NEW CHAPLAIN. [/]
[Narrator] It can not be denied that, reluctant as Reginald May had been to accept the chaplaincy of which so much had been said, he had no sooner fairly done so, and committed himself beyond remedy, than a certain sense of relief began to steal over the young man's mind. [/]
[Narrator] He had made the leap. [/]
[Narrator] Moved, at last, by arguments which, perhaps, were not worth very much logically, and which even while he yielded to them he saw the weakness of, he felt sure that when he woke in the morning, and realized what he had done, fearful feelings of remorse would seize him. [/]
[Narrator] But, curiously enough, this was not so; and his first sensation was relief that the conflict was over, and that he had no more angry remonstrances to meet with, or soft pleadings from Ursula, or assaults of rude abruptness from Janey. [/]
[Narrator] All that was over; and then a warm glow of independence and competency came over the young man. [/]
[Narrator] You may be sure he had no fire in his rooms to make him warm, and it was a chill January morning, with snow in the heavy sky, and fog in the yellow air; but, notwithstanding, there came a glow of comfort over him. [/]
[Reginald] Independent! --free to go where he pleased, buy what he liked, spend his time as best seemed to him, with a"position"of his own; even a house of his own. [/]
[Narrator] He laughed softly to himself at this new idea. [/]
[Narrator] It did not somehow hurt him as he thought it would, this sinecure he had accepted. [/]
[Ursula] Could he not make it up, as Ursula said,"work for the town in other ways without pay, since the town had given him pay without work?" [/]
[Narrator] A genial feeling of toleration came over Reginald's mind. [/]
[Narrator] Why should he have made such a fuss about it? [/]
[Narrator] It was natural that his father should insist, and, now that it was done, he himself did not wish it undone, as he had expected to do. [/]
[Narrator] After all, if you judged matters with such rigidity, who was there without guilt? [/]
[Narrator] what public appointment was given and held according to abstract right, as, formally speaking, it ought to be? [/]
[Narrator] Those in the highest offices were appointed, not because of their personal excellence, but because of being some other man's son or brother; and yet, on the whole, public duty was well done, and the unjust ruler and hireling priest were exceptions. [/]
[Narrator] Even men whose entry into the fold was very precipitate, over the wall, violently, or by some rat - hole of private interest, made very good shepherds, once they were inside. [/]
[Narrator] Nothing was perfect in this world, and yet things were more good than evil; and if he himself made it his study to create for himself an ideal position, to become a doer of all kinds of volunteer work, what would it matter that his appointment was not an ideal appointment? [/]
[Narrator] It seemed very strange to him, and almost like an interposition of Providence in his favour, that he should feel in this way, for Reginald was not aware that such revulsions of feeling were very natural phenomena, and that the sensation, after any great decision, is almost invariably one of relief. [/]
[HisFather] To be sure it upset this manly state of mind a little when, coming down to breakfast, his father gave him a nod, and said briefly,"I am glad you have seen your duty at last. [/]
[HisFather] "This made him almost resolve to throw it up again; but the feeling was momentary. [/]
[Narrator] Why should he give it up? [/]
[Narrator] It had made him independent (already he thought of his independence as a thing accomplished), and he would make full amends to the Church and to Carlingford for taking two hundred and fifty pounds a year without working for it. [/]
[Narrator] Surely he could do that. [/]
[Narrator] He did not grudge work, but rather liked it, and would be ready to do anything, he did not care what, to make his sinecure into a volunteer's outpost for every good work. [/]
[Narrator] Yes, that was the way to look at it. [/]
[Narrator] And it was a glorious independence. [/]
[Narrator] Two hundred and fifty pounds a year! [/]
[Ursula] "And the house,"cried Ursula, when Mr. May had left the breakfast - table, and left them free to chatter. [/]
[Reginald] "The house -- I do n't think you are likely to find a tenant for it. [/]
[Reginald] The houses in Grange Lane are so cheap now; and some people object to the poor old men. [/]
[Reginald] I think you must keep the house. [/]
[Narrator] Furnishing will be an expense; but, of course, when you have a certain income, that makes such a difference; and you can come and see us every day.""Why ca n't he live at home?"said Janey;"we are so poor; he ought to come and pay us something for his board, and help us to get on." [/]
[Ursula] "What can you know about it, at your age?"said Ursula. [/]

[Narrator] The College was a picturesque old building at the other side of Carlingford, standing in pretty grounds with some fine trees, under which the old men sat and amused themselves in the summer mornings. [/]

[Joe] It's all furnished, is College. [/]
[Joe] You'll think as the things look a bit queer; they was n't made not this year, nor yet last year, I can tell you; and they ai n't in the fashion. [/]
[Joe] But if so be as you do n't stand by fashion, there they is,"said Joe, throwing open the door. [/]
[Narrator] The young people went in softly, their excitement subdued into a kind of awe. [/]
[Narrator] An empty house, furnished, is more desolate, more overwhelming to the imagination, than a house which is bare. [/]
[Narrator] For whom was it waiting, all ready there, swept and garnished? [/]
[Narrator] Or were there already unseen inhabitants about, writing ghostly letters on the tables, seated on the chairs? [/]
[Narrator] Even Janey was hushed. [/]
[Janey] "I'd rather stay at home, after all,"she whispered in Ursula's ear under her breath. [/]
[Narrator] But after awhile they became familiar with the silent place, and awoke the echoes in it with their voices and new life. [/]
[Narrator] Nothing so young had been in the College for years. [/]
[Narrator] The last chaplain had been an old man and an old bachelor; and the pensioners were all solitary, living a sort of monastic life, each in his room, like workers in their cells. [/]
[Narrator] When Janey, surprised by some unexpected joke, burst into one of her peals of laughter, the old building echoed all through it, and more than one window was put up and head projected to know the cause of this profanation. [/]
[Workers] "Joe!"cried one portentous voice;"what's happened? [/]

[His] "They ai n't used to it, that's the truth; but laugh away, Miss, it'll do you good,"he added benignly. [/]
[Narrator] Joe was of a cheerful spirit, notwithstanding his infirmities, and he foresaw lightsome days. [/]
[Narrator] Somewhat taken aback, however, by the commotion produced by Janey's laugh, the young party left the College, Ursula carrying with her sundry memoranda and measurements for curtains and carpets. [/]

[Narrator] Ursula was transformed by the instinct of business and management into the leader of the party. [/]

[/]
[Narrator] It is doubtful whether Holden would have given them so good a reception had the Miss Mays gone to hint to order curtains for the Parsonage -- for the Carlingford tradesmen were very well aware of the difficulties, in point of payment, which attended Mr. May's purchases. [/]

[Narrator] The air was heavy and yellow, and prophesied snow; but a very red wintry sun had managed to make an opening temporarily in the clouds, and threw a ruddy ray down Grange Lane, bringing out the few passengers who were coming and going under the old garden walls. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula clasped her hands together, and came to a stop suddenly, when she turned her eyes that way. [/]

[Narrator] CHAPTER XX. THAT TOZER GIRL! [/]

[Narrator] A NEW FRIEND. [/]

[/]

[Narrator] CHAPTER XXII. [/]
[Narrator] A DESPERATE EXPEDIENT. [/]
[Phoebe] It was something of a comfort to Phoebe to find that the"tea"to which Ursula asked her was a family meal, such as Mr. and Mrs. Tozer indulged in, in Grange Lane, with no idea of dinner to follow, as in more refined circles. [/]
