[Narrator] After all, where was the harm? CHAPTER XXIII. [/]
[Narrator] TIDED OVER. [/]
[Narrator] Next morning, Cotsdean was mournfully turning over his ledger in the High Street, wondering whether he should go back to Mr. May on another forlorn expedition, or whether he should betray his overwhelming anxiety to his wife, who knew nothing about the state of affairs. [/]
[Narrator] The shop was what is called a corn - factor's shop, full of sacks of grain, with knots of wheat - ears done up ornamentally in the window, a stock not very valuable, but sufficient, and showing a good, if not a very important, business. [/]
[Narrator] A young man behind, attended to what little business was going on; for the master himself was too much pre - occupied to think of bushels of seed. [/]
[Narrator] He was as uneasy as Mr. May had been on the previous night, and in some respects even more unhappy; for he had no resource except a sort of dumb faith in his principle, a feeling that he must be able to find out some way of escape -- chequered by clouds of despondency, sometimes approaching despair. [/]
[Narrator] For Cotsdean, too, felt vaguely that things were approaching a crisis -- that a great many resources had been exhausted -- that the pitcher which had gone so often to the well must, at last, be broken, and that it was as likely the catastrophe was coming now as at any other time. [/]
[Narrator] He said to himself that never in his previous experience had things seemed so blank as at present; never had the moment of fate approached so nearly without any appearance of deliverance. [/]
[Narrator] He had not even the round of possibilities before him which were in Mr. May's mind, however hopeless, at this particular moment, he might find them. [/]
[Narrator] Cotsdean, for his part, had nothing to think of but Mr. May. [/]
[Cotsdean] Would he find some way out of it still, he who was always so clever, and must, in his position, have always"good friends?" [/]
[Narrator] How the poor man wished that he had never been led into this fatal course -- that he had insisted, long ago, on the settlement which must come some time, and which did not get any easier by putting it off; but then, who was he to stand against his clergyman? [/]
[Narrator] He did not feel able now to make any stand against him. [/]
[Narrator] If he had to be ruined -- he must be ruined: what could he do? [/]
[Narrator] The man who had brought him to this, held him in such subjection that he could not denounce or accuse him even now. [/]
[Narrator] He was so much better, higher, abler, stronger than himself, that Cotsdean's harshest sentiment was a dumb feeling of injury; a feeling much more likely to lead him to miserable tears than to resistance. [/]
[Narrator] His clergyman -- how was he to stand against his clergyman? [/]
[Narrator] This was the burden of his thoughts. [/]
[Narrator] And still, perhaps, there might be salvation and safety in the resources, the power, and cleverness, and superior strength of the man for whom, in his humility, he had risked everything. [/]
[Narrator] Poor Cotsdean's eyes were red with sleeplessness and thinking, and the constant rubbings he administered with the sleeve of his rough coat. [/]
[Narrator] He hung helpless, in suspense, waiting to see what his chief would say to him; if he would send for him -- if he would come. [/]
[Narrator] And in the intervals of these anxious thoughts, he asked himself should he tell poor Sally -- should he prepare her for her fate? [/]
[Narrator] She and her children might be turned out of house and home, very probably would be, he said to himself, leaping to the extreme point, as men in his condition are apt to do. [/]
[Narrator] They might take everything from him; they might bring all his creditors on him in a heap; they might sell him up; his shop by which he made his daily bread, and everything he had, and turn his children out into the streets. [/]
[Narrator] Once more he rubbed his sleeve over his eyes, which were smarting with sleeplessness and easily - coming tears. [/]
[Narrator] He turned over the pages of the ledger mechanically. [/]
[Narrator] There was no help in it -- no large debts owing to him that could be called in; no means of getting any money; and nothing could he do but contemplate the miseries that might be coming, and wait, wait, wondering dully whether Mr. May was doing anything to avert this ruin, and whether, at any moment, he might walk in, bringing safety in the very look of his bold eyes. [/]
[Narrator] Cotsdean was not bold; he was small and weakly, and nervous, and trembled at a sharp voice. [/]
[Narrator] He was not a man adapted for vigorous struggling with the world. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May could do it, in whose hands was the final issue. [/]
[Narrator] He was a man who was afraid of no one; and whose powers nobody could deny. [/]
[Narrator] Surely now, even at the last moment, he would find help somehow. [/]
[Narrator] It seemed profane to entertain a doubt that he would be able to do it even at the very last. [/]
[Narrator] But Cotsdean had a miserable morning; he could do nothing. [/]
[Narrator] Minute by minute, hour by hour, he waited to be called to the Parsonage; now and then he went out to the door of his shop and looked out wistfully down the street where it ended in the distance of Grange Lane. [/]
[Narrator] Was that the maid from the Parsonage coming up across the road? [/]
[Narrator] Were these the young ladies, who, though they knew nothing about the matter at issue, very frequently brought a note, or message, from their father to Cotsdean? [/]
[Narrator] But he was deceived in these guesses as well as in so many others. [/]
[Narrator] All the world seemed out of doors that morning, but nobody came. [/]
[Narrator] The ruddy sunshine shone full down the street, glorifying it with rays of warm gold, and tinting the mists and clouds which lurked in the corners. [/]
[Narrator] It had been heavy and overcast in the morning, but at noon the clouds had cleared away, and that big red globe of fire had risen majestically out of the mists, and everybody was out. [/]
[Narrator] But no one, except humble people in the ordinary way of business, came to Cotsdean. [/]
[Narrator] Bushels of grain for chickens, pennyworths of canary seed -- oh! did any one think he could pay a hundred pounds out of these? --a hundred pounds, the spending of which had not been his, poor man; which was indeed spent long ago, and represented luxuries past and over, luxuries which were not Cotsdean's. [/]
[Narrator] Strange that a mere lump of money should live like this, long after it was, to all intents and purposes, dead, and spent and gone! [/]
[Narrator] Then came the hour of dinner, when his Sally called him to the room behind the shop, from which an odour of bacon and fine big beans -- beans which were represented in his shop in many a sackful. [/]
[Narrator] He went in unwillingly in obedience to her command, but feeling unable to eat, soon left the table, sending the young man to fill his place, with whose appetite no obstacle of care or thought interfered. [/]
[Narrator] Poor Cotsdean felt that the smell of the dinner made him sick -- though he would have liked to eat had he been able -- the smell of the bacon which he loved, and the sight of the small children whom he loved still better, and poor Sally, his wife, still red in the face from dishing it up. [/]
[Narrator] Sally was anxious about her husband's want of appetite. [/]
[Sally] "What ails you, John?" [/]
[Sally] she said, pathetically;"it was n't as if you were out last night, nor nothing o'that sort. [/]
[Sally] A man as is sober like you do n't ought to turn at his dinner. [/]
[Sally] "She was half sorry, and half aggrieved, poor woman, feeling as if some blame of her cookery must be involved. [/]
[PoorCotsdean] "It's the bile,"said poor Cotsdean, with that simplicity of statement which is common in his class. [/]
[Cotsdean] "Do n't you take on, Sally, I'll be a deal better by supper - time----or worse,"he added to himself. [/]
[Narrator] Yes, he would make an effort to eat at supper - time; perhaps it might be the last meal he should eat in his own comfortable home. [/]
[Narrator] He had been out at the shop door, gazing despairingly down the road; he had come in and sold some birdseed, wondering -- oh, what good would that penny do him? --he who wanted a hundred pounds? [/]
[Narrator] and was standing listening with a sad heart to the sound of the knives and forks and chatter of the children, when suddenly all at once Mr. May walked into the shop, changing dismay into hope. [/]
[Narrator] What a thing it was to be a gentleman and a clergyman. [/]
[Narrator] Cotsdean could not but think! [/]
[Narrator] The very sight of Mr. May inspired him with courage; even though probably he had no money in his pocket, it was a supporting thing only to see him, and hear the sound of his free unrestrained step. [/]
[Cotsdean] He came in with a friendly nod to his humble helper; then he glanced round the shop, to see that no one was present, and then he said,"All right, Cotsdean,"in a voice that was as music to the little corn - factor's ears. [/]
[Cotsdean] His heart, which had been beating so low, jumped up in his bosom; his appetite came back with a leap; he asked himself would the bacon be cold? and cried,"God be praised, sir,"in a breath. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May winced slightly; but why should it be wrong to be grateful to God in any circumstances? [/]
[Narrator] he asked himself, having become already somewhat composed in his ideas on this particular point. [/]
[Reginald] "Are we quite alone?"he said. [/]
[Reginald] "Nobody within hearing? [/]
[Reginald] I have not brought you the money, but a piece of paper that is as good as the money. [/]
[Reginald] Take it: you will have no difficulty in discounting this; the man is as well known as the Carlingford Bank, and as safe, though I dare say you will be surprised at the name. [/]
[Reginald] "Cotsdean opened out the new bill with trembling hands. [/]
[Cotsdean] "Tozer!" [/]
[Narrator] he said faintly, between relief and dismay. [/]
[Reginald] "Yes. [/]
[Reginald] You must know that I am taking a pupil -- one who belongs to a very rich Dissenting family in London. [/]
[Reginald] Tozer knows something about him, from his connection with the body, and through this young man I have got to know something of _ him _. [/]
[Reginald] He does it upon the admirable security of the fees I am to receive with this youth; so you see, after all, there is no mystery about it. [/]
[Reginald] Better not wait for to - morrow, Cotsdean. [/]
[Reginald] Go at once, and get it settled. [/]
[Narrator] You see,"said Mr. May, ingratiatingly,"it is a little larger than the other -- one hundred and fifty, indeed -- but that does not matter with such an excellent name.""Tozer!"said Cotsdean, once more bewildered. [/]
[Narrator] He handled the piece of paper nervously, and turned it upside down, and round about, with a sense that it might melt in his hold. [/]
[Narrator] He did not like the additional fifty added. [/]
[Narrator] Why should another fifty be added? [/]
[Narrator] but so it was, and there seemed nothing for him but to take the immediate relief and be thankful. [/]
[Cotsdean] "I'd rather, sir, as Tozer had n't known nothing about it; and why should he back a bill for me as ai n't one of my friends, nor do n't know nothing about me? [/]
[Cotsdean] and fifty more added on,"said Cotsdean. [/]
[Narrator] It was the nearest he had gone to standing up against his clergyman; he did not like it. [/]
[Narrator] To be Mr. May's sole stand - by and agent, even at periodical risk of ruin, was possible to him; but a pang of jealousy, alarm, and pain came into his mind when he saw the new name. [/]
[Narrator] This even obliterated the immediate sense of relief that was in his mind. [/]
[Cotsdean] "Come three months it'll have to be paid,"said Cotsdean,"and Tozer ai n't a man to stand it if he's left to pay; he'd sell us up, Mr. May. [/]
[Cotsdean] He ai n't one of the patient ones, like -- some other folks; and there's fifty pounds put on. [/]
[Cotsdean] I do n't see my way to it. [/]
[Narrator] I'd rather it was just the clear hundred, if it was the same to you.""It is not the same to me,"said Mr. May, calmly. [/]
[Reginald] "Come, there is no cause to make any fuss. [/]
[Reginald] There it is, and if you do n't like to make use of it, you must find some better way. [/]
[Reginald] Bring the fifty pounds, less the expenses, to me to - night. [/]
[Reginald] It is a good bit of paper, and it delivers us out of a mess which I hope we shall not fall into again." [/]
[TheCornFactor] "So you said before, sir,"said the corn - factor sullenly. [/]
[Reginald] "Cotsdean, you forget yourself; but I can make allowance for your anxiety. [/]
[Reginald] Take it, and get it settled before the bank closes; pay in the money to meet the other bill, and bring me the balance. [/]
[Reginald] You will find no difficulty with Tozer's name; and what so likely as that one respectable tradesman should help another? [/]
[Narrator] By the way, the affair is a private one between us, and it is unnecessary to say anything to him about it; the arrangement, you understand, is between him and me.""Beg your pardon, sir,"said Cotsdean, with a deprecatory movement of his hand to his forehead;"but it is me as will be come upon first if anything happens, and that fifty pounds--""Have you ever found me to fail you, Cotsdean? [/]
[Cotsdean] If you knew the anxiety I have gone through, that you might be kept from harm, the sleepless nights, the schemes, the exertions! [/]
[Cotsdean] You may suppose it was no ordinary effort to ask a man like Tozer. [/]
[Cotsdean] "Cotsdean was moved by the touching tone in which his partner in trouble spoke; but terror gave him a certain power. [/]
[Narrator] He grumbled still, not altogether vanquished. [/]
[Cotsdean] "I do n't say nothing against that, sir,"he said, not meeting Mr. May's eye;"but when it comes to be paid, sir, I'm the first in it, and where is that other fifty to come from? [/]
[Narrator] That's what I'm a thinking for -- for I'm the first as they'd haul up after all.""You!"said Mr. May,"what could they get from you? [/]
[Reginald] You are not worth powder and shot. [/]
[Reginald] Do n't be ridiculous, my good fellow. [/]
[Reginald] I never avoid my responsibilities, as you know. [/]
[Reginald] I am as good, I hope, for that fifty as for all that went before. [/]
[Narrator] Have you ever known me leave you or any one in the lurch?""No, sir, I ca n't say as -- I do n't suppose I have. [/]
[I] I've always put my trust in you like in Providence itself,"he cried, hastily, holding his breath. [/]
[Reginald] "Then do as I tell you,"said Mr. May, waving his hand with careless superiority; and though his heart was aching with a hundred anxious fears, he left the shop with just that mixture of partial offence and indifference which overawed completely his humble retainer. [/]
[Narrator] Cotsdean trembled at his own guilty folly and temerity. [/]
[Narrator] He did not dare to call his patron back again, to ask his pardon. [/]
[Narrator] He did not venture to go back to the table and snatch a bit of cold bacon. [/]
[Narrator] He was afraid he had offended his clergyman, what matter that he was hungry for his dinner? [/]
[Narrator] He called the young man from the bacon, which was now cold and all but eaten up, and snatched at his hat and went out to the bank. [/]
[Narrator] It was all he could do. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER XXIV. [/]
[Narrator] A VISIT. [/]
[Cotsdean] "DEAR MAY,"Young Copperhead, the young fellow whom you have undertaken to coach, is coming to the Hall for a few days before he enters upon his studies, and Anne wishes me to ask you to come over on Tuesday to dine and sleep, and to make acquaintance with him. [/]
[Cotsdean] You can carry him back with you if it suits you. [/]
[Cotsdean] In my private opinion, he is a cub of the most disagreeable kind; but the girls like his mother, who is a kind of cousin, as you know. [/]
[Cotsdean] It is not only because he has failed to take his degree (you know how I hate the hideous slang in which this fact is generally stated), but that his father, who is one of the rich persons who abound in the lower circles of society, is ambitious, and would like to see him in Parliament, and that sort of thing -- a position which can not be held creditably without some sort of education: at least, so I am myself disposed to think. [/]
[Cotsdean] Therefore, your pleasing duty will be to get him up in a little history and geography, so that he may not get quite hopelessly wrong in any of the modern modifications of territory, for instance; and in so much Horace as may furnish him with a few stock quotations, in case he should be called upon, in the absence of any more hopeful neophyte, to move the Address. [/]
[Cotsdean] He is a great hulking fellow, not very brilliant, you may suppose, but not so badly mannered as he might be, considering his parentage. [/]
[Cotsdean] I do n't think he'll give you much trouble in the house; but he will most probably bore you to death, and in that case your family ought to have a claim, I should think, for compensation. [/]
[Cotsdean] Anyhow, come and see him, and us, before you begin your hard task. [/]
[Cotsdean] "Very truly yours,"R. DORSET." [/]
[Cotsdean] "Anne makes me open my letter to say that Ursula must come too. [/]
[Cotsdean] We will send a carriage to meet you at the station. [/]
[Cotsdean] "This letter caused considerable excitement in the Parsonage. [/]
[Narrator] It was the first invitation to dinner which Ursula had ever received. [/]
[Narrator] The dinner - parties in Carlingford were little frequented by young ladies. [/]
[Narrator] The male population was not large enough to afford a balance for the young women of the place, who came together in the evening, and took all the trouble of putting on their pretty white frocks, only to sit in rows in the drawing - room, waiting till the old gentlemen came in from the dining - room, after which everybody went away. [/]
[Narrator] There were no young gentlemen to speak of in Carlingford, so that when any one was bold enough to attempt a dancing - party, or anything of an equally amusing description, friends were sent out in all directions, as the beaters are sent into the woods to bring together the unfortunate birds for a _ battue _, to find men. [/]
[Narrator] These circumstances will explain the flutter in Ursula's innocent bosom when her father read her that postscript. [/]
[Reginald] Mr. May was singularly amiable that day, a thing which happened at periodical intervals, usually after he had been specially"cross." [/]
[Narrator] On this occasion there was no black mark against him in the family reckoning, and yet he was more kind than any one had ever known him. [/]
[Narrator] Instead of making any objections, he decided at once that Ursula must go, and told her to put on her prettiest frock, and make herself look very nice. [/]
[Reginald] "You must let Anne Dorset see that you care to please her,"he said. [/]
[Narrator] "Anne is a very good woman, and her approval is worth having.""Oh, papa!"cried Janey,"when you are always calling her an old maid!""L'un n'empêche pas l'autre,"he said, which puzzled Janey, whose French was very deficient. [/]
[Narrator] Even Ursula, supposed to be the best French scholar in the family, was not quite sure what it meant; but it was evidently something in favour of Cousin Anne, which was sweet to the grateful girl. [/]
[Narrator] Janey, though suffering bitterly under the miserable consciousness of being only fourteen, and not asked anywhere, helped with disinterested zeal to get her sister ready, and consoled herself by orders for unlimited muffins and cake for tea. [/]
[Ursula] "There will only be the children,"she said, resignedly, and felt herself _ incomprise _; but indeed, the attractions of a good romp afterwards, no one being in the house to restrain the spirits of the youthful party, made even Janey amends. [/]
[Narrator] As for Reginald, who was not asked, he was, it must be allowed, rather sulky too, and he could not solace himself either with muffins or romps. [/]
[Narrator] His rooms at the College were very pleasant rooms, but he was used to home; and though the home at the Parsonage was but faded, and not in such perfect order as it might have been, the young man felt even his wainscoted study dull without the familiar voices, the laughter and foolish family jokes, and even the little quarrels which kept life always astir. [/]
[Narrator] He walked with Ursula to the station, whither her little box with her evening dress had gone before her, in a half - affronted state of mind. [/]
[Reginald] "What does he want with a pupil?" [/]
[Narrator] Reginald was saying, as he had said before. [/]
[Reginald] "A fellow no one knows, coming and taking possession of the house as if it belonged to him. [/]
[Reginald] There is plenty to do in the parish without pupils, and if I were not on the spot he would get into trouble, I can tell you. [/]
[Reginald] A man that has been ploughed,'a big hulking fellow'(Sir Robert says so, not I). [/]
[Reginald] Mind, I'll have no flirting, Ursula; that is what always happens with a pupil in the house.""Reginald, how dare you--""Oh, yes, I dare; my courage is quite equal to facing you, even if you do shoot thunderbolts out of your eyes. [/]
[Reginald] Mind you, I wo n't have it. [/]
[Reginald] There is a set of fellows who try it regularly, and if you were above them, would go in for Janey; and it would be great fun and great promotion for Janey; she would feel herself a woman directly; so you must mind her as well as yourself. [/]
[Reginald] I do n't like it at all,"Reginald went on. [/]
[Reginald] "Probably he will complain of the dinners you give him, as if he were in an inn. [/]
[Reginald] Confound him! [/]
[Reginald] What my father means by it, I ca n't tell. [/]
[Narrator] ""Reginald, you ought not to swear,"said Ursula. [/]

[Ursula] Poor papa meant making a little more money. [/]

[Ursula] I thought he was very----". [/]

[Reginald] I begin to see. [/]

[Narrator] ""The sort of girl to encourage that sort of thing! [/]

[Narrator] After this little out - break, she came down at once to her usual calm. [/]

[/]
[Narrator] It was still early, for the trains which stopped at the little country station next to the Hall were very few and inconvenient, and [/]
[Narrator] the sun, though setting, was still shining red from over St. Roque's upon Grange Lane. [/]
[Narrator] The old red walls grew redder still in the frosty night, and the sky began to bloom into great blazing patches of colour upon the wintry clearness of the blue. [/]
[Narrator] There was going to be a beautiful sunset, and such a thing was always to be seen from Grange Lane better than anywhere else in Carlingford. [/]
[Narrator] Reginald went down the road slowly, looking at it, and already almost forgetting his idea of calling on Miss Beecham. [/]
[Narrator] To call on Miss Beecham would be to call on old Tozer, the butterman, to whom alone the visit would be naturally paid; and this made him laugh within himself. [/]
[Narrator] So he would have passed, no doubt, without the least attempt at intruding on the privacy of the Tozers, had not the garden - door opened before he got so far, and Phoebe herself came out, with her hands in her muff, to take a little walk up and down as she did daily. [/]
[Narrator] She did not take her hand out of its warm enclosure to give it to him; but nodded with friendly ease in return to his salutation. [/]
[Phoebe] "I have come out to see the sunset,"said Phoebe;"I like a little air before the day is over, and grandmamma, when she is poorly, likes her room to be very warm.""I hope Mrs. Tozer is better. [/]
[Phoebe] I hope you have not been anxious. [/]
[Phoebe] ""Oh, no! it is chronic; there is no danger. [/]
[Phoebe] But she requires a great deal of attendance; and I like to come out when I can. [/]
[Phoebe] Oh, how fine it is! [/]
[Phoebe] what colour! [/]
[Phoebe] I think, Mr. May, you must have a _ spécialité _ for sunsets at Carlingford. [/]
[Narrator] I never saw them so beautiful anywhere else.""I am glad there is something you like in Carlingford.""Something! [/]
[I] there is a very great deal; and that I do n't like too,"she said with a smile. [/]
[I] "I do n't care for the people I am living among, which is dreadful. [/]
[I] I do n't suppose you have ever had such an experience, though you must know a great deal more in other ways than I. All the people that come to inquire about grandmamma are very kind; they are as good as possible; I respect them, and all that, but----Well, it must be my own fault, or education. [/]
[I] It is education, no doubt, that gives us those absurd ideas. [/]
[Narrator] ""Do n't call them absurd,"said Reginald,"indeed I can enter into them perfectly well. [/]
[Narrator] I do n't _ know _ them, perhaps, in my own person; but I can perfectly understand the repugnance, the distress--""The words are too strong,"said Phoebe,"not so much as that; the -- annoyance, perhaps, the nasty disagreeable struggle with one's self and one's pride; as if one were better than other people. [/]
[Phoebe] I dislike myself, and despise myself for it; but I ca n't help it. [/]
[Phoebe] We have so little power over ourselves. [/]
[Narrator] ""I hope you will let my sister do what she can to deliver you,"said Reginald;"Ursula is not like you; but she is a good little thing, and she is able to appreciate you. [/]

[Reginald] "Oh, Clarence Copperhead; he is come then? [/]

[Reginald] It would be better if we were all poor. [/]
[Reginald] "Reginald said nothing; he thought so too. [/]
[Narrator] In that case there would be a few disagreeable things out of a poor clergyman's way, and assaults like that of Northcote upon himself would be impossible; but he could scarcely utter these virtuous sentiments. [/]
[Reginald] "Poverty is the desire of ascetics, and this is not an ascetic age,"he said at length, with a half - laugh at himself for his stiff speech. [/]

[Reginald] That is why I like the church; for other things of course I like our own body best. [/]
[Reginald] ""I do n't suppose such things can be argued about, Miss Beecham. [/]

[Reginald] I do n't like having a stranger in the house; my father is fond of having his own way. [/]
[Narrator] ""It is astonishing how often parents are so,"said Phoebe, demurely;"and the way they talk of their experience! [/]
[Phoebe] as if each new generation did not know more than the one that preceded it. [/]
[Narrator] ""You are pleased to laugh, but I am quite in earnest. [/]

[Reginald] For instance, no man who has a family should ever take one. [/]
[Reginald] I know what things are said. [/]

[Phoebe] Mr. Trollope is good for that too, but not so good. [/]

[Phoebe] "CHAPTER XXVI. [/]
[Narrator] THE HALL. [/]
[Narrator] It is unnecessary to say that the dinner party in the Hall bore very little resemblance to those simple amusements in No. 6, Grange Lane. [/]
[Narrator] There were three or four people to meet Mr. May, who, as an orator and literary man, had greater reputation even such a little way from home than he had in his own town. [/]
[Reginald] He was a very good preacher, and those articles of his were much admired as"thoughtful"papers, searching into many mental depths, and fathoming the religious soul with wonderful insight. [/]
[Narrator] Ladies especially admired them; the ladies who were intellectual, and found pleasure in the feeling of being more advanced than their neighbours. [/]
[Narrator] The Rector's wife of the parish in which the Dorsets lived applied herself with great vigour to the art of drawing him out. [/]
[Narrator] She asked him questions with that air of delightful submission to an intellectual authority which some ladies love to assume, and which it pleases many men to accept. [/]
[Narrator] His daughters were not at all reverential of Mr. May, and it soothed him to get marks of devotion and literary submission out of doors. [/]

[Narrator] Little Ursula was one of her prime favourites -- a motherless girl, who was the eldest, and who had to work for the family, was of all others the thing which moved her sympathies most. [/]

[Narrator] CHAPTER XXVII. [/]
[Narrator] A PAIR OF NATURAL ENEMIES. [/]
[Narrator] After the evening at Grange Lane which has been described, Reginald May met Northcote in the street several times, as was unavoidable, considering the size of the place, and the concentration of all business in Carlingford within the restricted length of the High Street. [/]
[Narrator] The two young men bowed stiffly to each other at first; then by dint of seeing each other frequently, got to inclinations a little more friendly, until at length one day when Northcote was passing by the College, as Reginald stood in the old doorway, the young chaplain feeling magnanimous on his own ground, and somewhat amused by the idea which suddenly presented itself to him, asked his Dissenting assailant if he would not come in and see the place. [/]
[Narrator] Reginald had the best of it in every way. [/]
[Narrator] It was he who was the superior, holding out a hand of favour and kindness to one who here at least, was beneath him in social consideration; and it was he who was the assailed, and, so to speak, injured party, and who nevertheless extended to his assailant a polite recognition, which, perhaps, no one else occupying the same position would have given. [/]
[Narrator] He was amused by his own magnanimity, and enjoyed it, and the pleasure of heaping coals of fire upon his adversary's head was entirely delightful to him. [/]
[Reginald] "I know you do not approve of the place or me,"he said, forgetting in that moment of triumph all his own objections to it, and the ground upon which these objections were founded. [/]
[Reginald] "Come in and see it, will you? [/]
[Reginald] The chapel and the rooms are worth seeing. [/]
[Reginald] They are fair memorials of the past, however little the foundation may be to your mind. [/]
[Reginald] "He laughed as he spoke, but without ill - humour; for it is easy to be good - humoured when one feels one's self on the gaining, not the losing side. [/]
[Narrator] As for Northcote, pride kept him from any demonstration of unwillingness to look at what the other had to show. [/]
[Narrator] He would not for worlds have betrayed himself. [/]
[Narrator] It was expedient for him, if he did not mean to acknowledge himself worsted, to put on a good face and accept the politeness cheerfully. [/]
[Narrator] So that it was on the very strength of the conflict which made them first aware of each other's existence, that they thus came together. [/]
[Narrator] The Dissenter declared his entire delight in being taken to see the place, and with secret satisfaction, not easily put into words, the Churchman led the way. [/]
[Narrator] They went to all the rooms where the old men sat, some dozing by the fire, some reading, some busy about small businesses; one had a turning - lathe, another was illuminating texts, a third had a collection of curiosities of a heterogeneous kind, which he was cleaning and arranging, writing neat little labels in the neatest little hand for each article. [/]
[Reginald] "The charity of our ancestors might have been worse employed,"said Reginald. [/]
[Narrator] "A home for the old and poor is surely as fine a kind of benevolence as one could think of -- if benevolence is to be tolerated at all.""Ye - es,"said Northcote. [/]
[Northcote] "I do n't pretend to disapprove of benevolence. [/]
[Northcote] Perhaps the young who have a future before them, who can be of use to their country, are better objects still. [/]
[Narrator] ""Because they will pay,"said Reginald;"because we can get something out of them in return; while we have already got all that is to be had out of the old people? [/]
[Reginald] A very modern doctrine, but not so lovely as the old - fashioned way. [/]
[Narrator] ""I did not mean that,"said the other, colouring. [/]
[TheOther] "Certainly it ought to pay; everything, I suppose, is meant to pay one way or other. [/]
[TheOther] The life and progress of the young, or the gratified sentiment of the benefactor, who feels that he has provided for the old -- which is the noblest kind of payment? [/]
[TheOther] I think the first, for my part." [/]
[Northcote] "For that matter, there is a large and most flourishing school, which you will come across without fail if you work among the poor. [/]
[Northcote] Do you work among the poor? [/]
[Northcote] Pardon my curiosity; I do n't know. [/]
[Narrator] ""It depends upon what you call the poor,"said the other, who did not like to acknowledge the absence of this element in Salem Chapel;"if you mean the destitute classes, the lowest level, no; but if you mean the respectable, comfortable--""Persons of small income?"said Reginald. [/]
[Reginald] "I mean people with no incomes at all; people without trades, or anything to earn a comfortable living by; labouring people, here to - day and away to - morrow; women who take in washing, and men who go about hunting for a day's work. [/]
[Reginald] These are the kind of people the Church is weighted with. [/]
[Narrator] ""I do n't see any trace of them,"said the Nonconformist. [/]
[TheNonconformist] "Smooth lawns, fine trees, rooms that countesses might live in. [/]
[Narrator] I ca n't see any trace of them here.""There is no harm in a bit of grass and a few trees, and the rooms are cheaper in their long continuance than any flimsy new rubbish that could be built.""I know I am making an unfortunate quotation,"said Northcote;"but there is reason in it. [/]
[Northcote] It might be sold for so much, and given to the poor." [/]
[Narrator] "Cheating the poor, in the first place,"said Reginald, warmly concerned for what he felt to be his own;"just as the paddock an old horse dies in might bear a crop instead, and pay the owner; but what would become of the old horse?""Half - quarter of this space would do quite as well for your pensioners, and they might do without--""A chaplain!"said Reginald, laughing in spite of himself. [/]
[Reginald] "I know you think so. [/]
[Reginald] It is a sinecure." [/]

[Northcote] There are so many things more worthy of you that you might do. [/]

[Reginald] _ [/]
[Reginald] I _ think, too, almost anything would be better than to encourage the ignorant to make themselves judges of public institutions, which the wisest even find too delicate to meddle with. [/]
[Narrator] The digging and the ploughing might be a good thing for more than me.""I do n't say otherwise,"said the young Dissenter, following into the old fifteenth - century chapel, small but perfect, the young priest of the place. [/]
[Narrator] They stood together for a moment under the vaulted roof, both young, in the glory of their days, both with vague noble meanings in them, which they knew so poorly how to carry out. [/]
[Narrator] They meant everything that was fine and great, these two young men, standing upon the threshold of their life, knowing little more than that they were fiercely opposed to each other, and meant to reform the world each in his own way; one by careful services and visitings of the poor, the other by the Liberation Society and overthrow of the State Church; both foolish, wrong and right, to the utmost bounds of human possibility. [/]
[Narrator] How different they felt themselves standing there, and yet how much at one they were without knowing it! [/]
[Narrator] Northcote had sufficient knowledge to admire the perfect old building. [/]
[Narrator] He followed his guide with a certain humility through the details, which Reginald had already learned by heart. [/]
[TheYoungChurchman] "There is nothing so perfect, so beautiful, so real now - a - days,"said the young Churchman, with a natural expansion of mind over the beauty to which he had fallen heir. [/]
[Narrator] It seemed to him, as he looked up at the tall windows with their graceful tracery, that he was the representative of all who had worked out their belief in God within these beautiful walls, and of all the perpetual worshippers who had knelt among the old brasses of the early founders upon the worn floor. [/]
[Narrator] The other stood beside him with a half envy in his mind. [/]
[Narrator] The Dissenter did not feel himself the heir of those centuries in the same unhesitating way. [/]
[Narrator] He tried to feel that he was the heir of something better and more spiritual, yet felt a not ungenerous grudge that he could not share the other kinship too. [/]
[TheDissenter] "It is very beautiful and noble,"he said. [/]
[TheDissenter] "I should like to feel for it as you do; but what I should like still better would be to have the same clear certainty of faith, the same conviction that what they were doing was the only right thing to do which made both building and prayer so unfaltering in those days. [/]
[Narrator] We ca n't be so sure even of the span of an arch now.""No -- nor can you be content with the old span, even though it is clearly the best by all rules,"said Reginald. [/]
[Narrator] The other smiled; he was the most speculative of the two, being perhaps the most thoughtful; and he had no fifteenth - century chapel to charm, nor old foundation to give him an anchor. [/]
[Narrator] He smiled, but there was a little envy in his mind. [/]
[Narrator] Even to have one's life set out before one within clear lines like this, would not that be something? [/]
[Narrator] If it had but been possible, no doubt saying prayers for the world, even with no better than the old men of the College to say amen, had something more beautiful in it than tours of agitation for the Liberation Society; but Northcote knew that for him it was not possible, any more than was the tonsure of Reginald's predecessor, who had said mass when first those pinnacles were reared towards heaven. [/]
[Narrator] After he had smiled he sighed, for the old faith was more lovely than all the new agitations; he felt a little ashamed of the Liberation Society, so long as he stood under that groined and glorious roof. [/]
[SomeOne] "May!"said some one, coming in suddenly. [/]
[SomeOne] "I want you to go to the hospital for me. [/]
[SomeOne] I am obliged to go off to town on urgent business -- convocation work; and I must get a lawyer's opinion about the reredos question; there is not a moment to lose. [/]
[SomeOne] Go and see the people in the pulmonary ward, there's a good fellow; and there are two or three bad accidents; and that old woman who is ill in Brown's cottage, you saw her the other day; and the Simmonds in Back Grove Street. [/]

[Narrator] There is nothing more effectual in showing us the weakness of any habitual fallacy or assumption than to hear it sympathetically, through the ears, as it were, of a sceptic. [/]
[Narrator] Reginald, seeing Northcote's keen eyes gleam at the sound of the Rector's voice, instinctively fell into sympathy with him, and heard the speech through him; and though he himself felt the importance of the reredos, yet he saw in a moment how such a question would take shape in the opinion of the young Dissenter, in whom he clearly saw certain resemblances to himself. [/]
[Narrator] Therefore he assented very briefly, taking out his note - book to put down the special cases of which the Rector told him. [/]

[Northcote] "Who is your friend?" [/]
[Narrator] he said abruptly, for he was a rapid man, losing no time about anything. [/]

[Narrator] He was young, and liked a bit of grandiloquence as well as another. [/]

[Narrator] Had he known what was about to be said to him, he might have stirred up his faculties to say something; but he had not an idea that Reginald would answer him like this, and it took him aback. [/]
[Narrator] He was too honest himself not to be worsted by such a speech. [/]
[Narrator] He bowed his head with genuine respect. [/]
[Narrator] The apology of the Churchman whom he had assaulted, filled him with a kind of reverential confusion; he could make no reply in words. [/]
[Narrator] And need it be said that Reginald's heart too melted altogether when he saw how he had confounded his adversary? [/]
[Narrator] That silent assent more than made up for the noisy onslaught. [/]

[Narrator] He was pleased and satisfied beyond the reach of words. [/]
[Northcote] "Will you come to the hospital with me?"he said; and they walked out together, the young Dissenter saying very little, doing what he could to arrange those new lights which had suddenly flashed upon his favourite subject, and feeling that he had lost his landmarks, and was confused in his path. [/]
[Narrator] When the logic is taken out of all that a man is doing, what is to become of him? [/]
[Narrator] This was what he felt; an ideal person in Reginald's place could not have made a better answer. [/]
[Narrator] Suddenly somehow, by a strange law of association, there came into his mind the innocent talk he had overheard between the two girls who were, he was aware, May's sisters. [/]
[Narrator] A certain romantic curiosity about the family came into his mind. [/]
[Narrator] Certainly they could not be an ordinary family like others. [/]
[Narrator] There must be something in their constitution to account for this sudden downfall, which he had encountered in the midst of all his theories. [/]
[Narrator] The Mays must be people of a different strain from others; a peculiar race, to whom great thoughts were familiar; he could not believe that there was anything common or ordinary in their blood. [/]
[Narrator] He went out in silence, with the holder of the sinecure which he had so denounced, but which now seemed to him to be held after a divine fashion, in a way which common men had no idea of. [/]
[Narrator] Very little could he say, and that of the most commonplace kind. [/]
[Narrator] He walked quite respectfully by the young clergyman's side along the crowded High Street, though without any intention of going to the hospital, or of actually witnessing the kind of work undertaken by his new friend. [/]
[Narrator] Northcote himself had no turn that way. [/]
[Narrator] To go and minister at a sick - bed had never been his custom; he did not understand how to do it; and though he had a kind of sense that it was the right thing to do, and that if any one demanded such a service of him he would be obliged to render it, he was all in the dark as to how he could get through so painful an office; whereas May went to it without fear, thinking of it only as the most natural thing in the world. [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps, it is possible, Northcote's ministrations, had he been fully roused, would have been, in mere consequence of the reluctance of his mind, to undertake them, more real and impressive than those which Reginald went to discharge as a daily though serious duty; but in any case it was the Churchman whose mode was the more practical, the more useful. [/]
[Narrator] They had not gone far together, when they met the Rector hurrying to the railway; he cast a frowning, dissatisfied look at Northcote, and caught Reginald by the arm, drawing him aside. [/]

[Narrator] He left Reginald hot and angry, doubly disposed, as was natural, to march Northcote over all the town, and show his intimacy with him. [/]
[Narrator] Get rid of an acquaintance whom he chose to extend his countenance to, to please the Rector! [/]
[Narrator] For a man so young as Reginald May, and so lately made independent, such an act of subserviency was impossible indeed. [/]
[Narrator] Before they entered the hospital, however, another encounter happened of a very different character. [/]

[MrCopperhead] They shall have a party; leave it all to me."CHAPTER XXVIII. [/]
[Narrator] THE NEW PUPIL. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May, since the bargain was fairly concluded with the Copperheads, had thought a great deal about the three hundred a - year he was to get for his pupil. [/]
[Narrator] It almost doubled his income in a moment, and that has a great effect upon the imagination. [/]
[Narrator] It was true he would have another person to maintain on this additional income, but still that additional person would simply fill Reginald's place, and it did not at first occur to him that what was good enough for himself, Mr. May, of St. Roque's, was not good enough for any _ parvenu _ on the face of the earth. [/]
[Narrator] Therefore the additional income represented a great deal of additional comfort, and that general expansion of expenditure, not going into any special extravagances, but representing a universal ease and enlargement which was congenial to him, and which was one of the great charms of money in his eyes. [/]
[Narrator] To be sure, when he reflected on the matter, he felt that the first half - year of Clarence's payment ought to be appropriated to that bill, which for the present had brought him so much relief; but this would be so entirely to lose the benefit of the money so far as he was himself concerned, that it was only in moments of reflection that this appeared urgent. [/]
[Narrator] The bill to which Tozer's signature had been appended did not oppress his conscience. [/]
[Narrator] After all, what was it? [/]
[Narrator] Not a very large sum, a sum which when put to it, and with time before him, he could so easily supply; and as for any other consideration, it was really, when you came to think of it, a quite justifiable expedient, not to be condemned except by squeamish persons, and which being never known, could do no harm in the world. [/]
[Narrator] He had not harmed anybody by what he had done. [/]
[AManOfStraw] Tozer, who was quite able to pay it over and over again, would never know of it; and in what respect, he asked himself, was it worse to have done this than to have a bill really signed by a man of straw, whose"value received"meant nothing in the world but a simple fiction? [/]
[Narrator] Cotsdean was no more than a man of straw; if left to himself, he could not pay anything, nor had he anything really to do with the business for which his name stood sponsor; and Tozer's name was merely placed there in the same fictitious way, without any trouble to Tozer, or burden of responsibility. [/]
[Narrator] What was the difference, except that it saved trouble and anxiety to everybody except the principal in the affair -- he who ought to bear the brunt? [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May recognised this without doubt. [/]
[Narrator] It was he who had reaped the advantage; and whether Cotsdean was the instrument who knew all about it, or Tozer, who did not know anything about it, it was he, Mr. May, whose natural duty it was to meet the claim and pay the money. [/]
[Narrator] He was an honest man; if he was occasionally a little slow in his payments, no one could throw any doubt upon his character. [/]
[Narrator] But, of course, should any unforeseen emergency arise, the pupil at once made that straight. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May felt that he had only to go to the bank, which generally did not encourage his visits, and tell them of his pupil, to have the money at once. [/]
[Narrator] Nobody could reject such unmistakeable security. [/]
[Narrator] So that really there was no further occasion for so much as thinking of Tozer; that was provided for; with the freest conscience in the world he might put it out of his mind. [/]
[Narrator] But how he could feel this so strongly, and at the same time revel in the consciousness of a fuller purse, more to enjoy, and more to spend, is a mystery which it would be difficult to solve. [/]
[Narrator] He did so, and many others have done so besides him, eating their cake, yet believing that they had their cake with the fullest confidence. [/]
[Narrator] He was a sensible man, rather priding himself on his knowledge of business, with much experience in human nature, and a thoughtful sense (fully evidenced in his writings) of all the strange inconsistencies and self - deceits of mankind; but he dropped into this strain of self - delusion with the calmest satisfaction of mind, and was as sure of his own good sense and kindness as if he had never in all his life taken a step out of the rigidest of the narrow ways of uprightness. [/]
[Narrator] Some part of this illusion, however, was sharply dispelled at a very early date. [/]
[Narrator] Clarence Copperhead, who was not likely to err by means of too much consideration for the feelings of others, grumbled frankly at the mid - day meal. [/]
[Reginald] "I do n't understand a two o'clock dinner,"he said;"it's lunch, that's what I call it; and I wo n't be disagreeable about the kids, but I must have my dinner. [/]
[Reginald] Bless you! [/]
[Reginald] a man ca n't live without his dinner. [/]
[Reginald] What is he to do? [/]
[Reginald] It is the sort of thing you can look forward to, whatever happens. [/]
[Reginald] If it's a wet day, or anything of that sort, there's always dinner; and after it's over, if there's music or a rubber, why that's all very well; or if a man feels a bit sleepy, it does n't matter. [/]
[Reginald] Why, dinner's your stand - by, wherever you are. [/]
[Reginald] I'd as soon do without my head, for my part."Ursula hastened to tell her father this with dismay in her looks. [/]
[Ursula] "I've always heard that late dinners were so expensive; you require twice as many dishes. [/]
[Ursula] At two, one has only what is necessary; but at seven, you require to have fish, and soup, and _ entrées _, and all sorts of things, besides the joint. [/]
[Ursula] It was disgraceful of him to say it!"cried Ursula;"and I think he ought to be made to follow our plan, whatever it is, and not do everything he likes here. [/]
[Narrator] ""That is all very true,"said Mr. May;"but he is right about the dinner; it is a great deal more agreeable. [/]
[Narrator] ""And expensive, papa.""Well, perhaps it is a great deal to expect at your age; but if you read your cookery - book, as I have often said, when you were reading those novels, and learned how to toss up little dishes out of nothing, and make _ entrées _, and so forth, at next to no expense--"The tears came into Ursula's eyes at this unjust assault. [/]

[Ursula] It is all very well to talk of dishes made out of nothing. [/]

[Narrator] There was an old convex mirror opposite which reflected the girls in miniature, making a pretty picture of them as they sat together, Ursula with her dark locks, and Phoebe in her golden hair, and the tall sharp school - girl, Janey, all elbows and angles, short petticoats and grey stockings. [Narrator] Janey was the only one in whom there could have been suspected any inferiority of race; but her awkwardness was that of youth, and her disordered hair and dress belonged also to her age, for she was at that troublesome period when frocks are constantly getting too short, and sleeves too scanty. [Narrator] The furred jacket had not been seen in Grange Lane before. [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps it was because the cold had become more severe, an ordinary and simple reason -- or because Clarence Copperhead, who knew her, and in whose eyes it was important to bate no jot of her social pretensions, was here; and the furred jacket was beyond comparison with anything that had been seen for ages in Carlingford. [/]
[Narrator] The deep border of fur round the velvet, the warm waddings and paddings, the close fit up to the throat, were excellencies which warranted Janey's tour of inspection. [/]

[MrCopperhead] "CHAPTER XXIX. [/]
[Narrator] URSULA'S ENTRÉES. [/]
[Ursula] It would be difficult to describe the anxiety with which that first"late dinner"was regarded by Ursula. [/]
[Janey] Janey, too, had thrown herself into it heart and soul, until she received the crushing intimation from her father, that her company was not expected at this stately meal; a discovery which altogether extinguished poor Janey, accustomed to be always in the front whatever occurred, and to whom suggestions of things that could not be done by a girl who was not"out,"had never presented themselves. [/]
[Narrator] She retired to her own room dissolved in tears when this fearful mandate went forth, and for the rest of the morning was good for nothing, her eyes being converted into a sort of red pulp, her rough hair doubly dishevelled, her whole being run into tears. [/]
[Narrator] She was of no more use now to go errands between the kitchen and the drawing - room, or to read the cookery - book out loud, which was a process upon which Ursula depended very much, to fix in her mind the exact ingredients and painful method of preparation of the _ entrées _ at which she was toiling. [/]
[Betsy] Betsy, the former maid - of - all - work, now promoted under the title of cook, could be trusted to roast the saddle of mutton, which, on consideration that it was"a party,"had been thought preferable to a leg, and she could boil the fish, after a sort, and make good honest family soup, and the rice - pudding or apple - tart, which was the nearest approach to luxury indulged in at the Parsonage; but as for _ entrées _, Betsy did not know what they were. [/]
[Narrator] She had heard of made dishes indeed, and respectfully afar off had seen them when she was kitchen - maid at Lady Weston's -- the golden age of her youthful inexperience. [/]
[Narrator] But this was so long ago, that her recollections were rather confusing than useful to Ursula, when she went downstairs to make her first heroic effort. [/]
[Betsy] "La, Miss, that ai n't how cook used to do'em at Lady Weston's,"Betsy said, looking on with unbelieving eyes. [/]
[Narrator] She was sure of this negative, but she was not sure of anything else, and utterly failed to give any active assistance, after driving the girl desperate with her criticisms. [/]
[Narrator] Altogether it was a confused and unpleasant day. [/]
[Narrator] When Reginald came in in the morning, his sister had no time to speak to him, so anxious was she and pre - occupied, and the drawing - room was being turned upside down, to make it look more modern, more elegant, more like the Dorsets'drawing - room, which was the only one Ursula knew. [/]
[Narrator] The comfortable round table in the middle, round which the family had grouped themselves for so long, had been pushed aside into a corner, leaving one fresh patch of carpet, quite inappropriate, and unconnected with anything else; and instead of the work and the school - books which so often intruded there, all that was gaudy and uninteresting in the May library had been produced to decorate the table; and even a case of wax flowers, a production of thirty years since, which had been respectfully transferred to a china closet by Ursula's better taste, but which in the dearth of ornament she had brought back again. [/]
[Narrator] Reginald carried off the wax flowers and replaced the table with his own hands, while Ursula scorched her cheeks over the _ entrées _ downstairs. [/]
[Narrator] "All this for Northcote,"he said, when she ran up for a moment, done up in a big white apron, her face crimson with the fire and anxiety combined:"for Miss Beecham has been here before, and you made no fuss about her then.""She came to tea,"said Ursula. [/]
[Ursula] "And I got a cake, which was all any one could do; but a dinner is a very different thing." [/]
[Narrator] Indeed she had by this time come to share her father's opinion, that dinner was the right and dignified thing in all cases, and that they had been hitherto living in a very higgledy - piggledy way. [/]
[Narrator] The dinner had gone to her head. [/]

[Narrator] He laughed, but the name moved him. [/]
[Reginald] "It is a pretty fresh sort of country name, not quite like such an accomplished person." [/]

[Narrator] He had gone up and down Grange Lane many times at the hour of sunset, hoping to meet Phoebe again, but that sensible young woman had no mind to be talked of, and never appeared except when she was certain the road was clear. [/]
[Narrator] This had tantalized Reginald more than he chose to avow, even to himself. [/]
[Narrator] Pride prevented him from knocking at the closed door. [/]
[Narrator] The old Tozers were fearful people to encounter, people whom to visit would be to damn himself in Carlingford; but then the Miss Griffiths were very insipid by the side of Phoebe, and the variety of her talk, though he had seen so little of her, seemed to have created a new want in his life. [/]
[Narrator] He thought of a hundred things which he should like to discuss with her -- things which did not interest Ursula, and which the people about him did not understand much. [/]
[Narrator] Society at that time, as may be presumed, was in a poor way in Carlingford. [/]
[Narrator] The Wentworths and Wodehouses were gone, and many other nice people; the houses in Grange Lane were getting deserted, or falling into inferior hands, as was apparent by the fact that the Tozers -- old Tozer, the butterman -- had got one of them. [/]
[TheGriffithsAboveMentionedPutUpWithInConsiderationOfTolerableGoodLooksAndFun] The other people were mostly relics of a bygone state of things: retired old couples, old ladies, spinsters, and widows -- excellent people, but not lively to talk to -- and the Griffiths, above mentioned, put up with in consideration of tolerable good looks and"fun,"became tiresome when anything better was to be had. [/]
[Narrator] The mere apparition of Phoebe upon the horizon had been enough to show Reginald that there were other kinds of human beings in the world. [/]
[Narrator] It had not occurred to him that he was in love with her, and the idea of the social suicide implied in marrying old Tozer's granddaughter, had not so much as once entered his imagination. [/]
[Narrator] Had he thought of it, he would have pulled that imagination up tight, like an unruly horse, the thing being too impossible to bear thinking of. [/]
[Narrator] But this had never entered his mind. [/]

[Narrator] No one else within his sphere could talk so well. [/]
[Narrator] The Rector was very great indeed on the reredos question, and the necessity of reviving the disused"Church"customs; but Reginald could not go so far as he did as to the importance of the reredos, and was quite in doubt whether it was not as well for most people to"direct"themselves by their own consciences as to be directed by the spiritual head of the parish, who was not over wise in his own concerns. [/]
[Narrator] His father, Reginald knew, could be very agreeable among strangers, but he seldom chose to be so in his own house. [/]

[Narrator] He was an Oxford man, with the best of education, but he was a simpleton all the same. [/]

[Narrator] It was not the world of fashion he was aware, but he thought in his simplicity that it was the still higher world of culture and knowledge, in which genius, and wit, and intellect stood instead of rank or riches. [/]

[Narrator] He called young Northcote to him, who was standing beside Ursula, eager to talk, but not knowing how to begin. [/]
[Narrator] It was bad enough to be thus withdrawn from his chance of making himself agreeable; but the reader may imagine what was the Dissenter's feelings when Mr. May, with a smile, turned upon him. [/]

[Narrator] Mr. May was too clear - sighted not to see it. [/]
[Narrator] He thought, quite impartially, that perhaps it was an excusable weakness, even though it was his own society that was the counter attraction. [/]
[Narrator] They were two nice - looking girls. [/]
[Narrator] This was how he put it, being no longer young, and father to one of them; naturally, the two young men would have described the attraction of Phoebe and Ursula more warmly. [/]
[Narrator] Clarence Copperhead, who had come in with an armful of music and his fiddle, was not thinking of the girls, nor of anything but the sweet sounds he was about to make -- and himself. [/]

[Reginald] "This is more than mortal can stand,"he said, making as though he would have gone away. [/]

[/]
[Narrator] Reginald felt very strongly, though for the first time, that she was over doing the Scriptural maxim of being all things to all men. [/]
[Narrator] CHAPTER XXX. [/]
[Narrator] SOCIETY AT THE PARSONAGE. [/]
[Narrator] After this dinner - party, such as it was, the Parsonage became gradually the centre of a little society, such as sometimes forms in the most accidental way in a house where there are young men and young women, and of which no one can say what momentous results may arise. [/]
[Narrator] They came together fortuitously, blown to one centre by the merest winds of circumstance, out of circles totally different and unlike. [/]
[Narrator] Why it was that Mr. May, so good a Churchman, permitted two people so entirely out of his sphere to become his habitual guests and the companions of his children was very perplexing to the outside world, who half in mere surprise, and a little in despite, wondered and commented till they were tired, or till they had become so familiar with the strange spectacle that it ceased to strike them. [/]
[Narrator] A rich pupil might be forgiven for being a Dissenter, indeed in Carlingford as elsewhere money made up for most deficiencies; but even natural complacency towards the rich pupil scarcely accounted for the reception of the others. [/]
[Narrator] The neighbours could never be quite sure whether the family at the Parsonage knew or did not know that their new friend Northcote was not only acting as Minister of Salem Chapel, but was the assailant of Reginald May at the Anti - Establishment Meeting, and various persons in Grange Lane held themselves for a long time on the tip - toe of preparation, ready to breathe to Mr. May the painful intelligence, in case he was unaware of it. [/]
[Narrator] But he never gave them the opportunity. [/]
[Narrator] Honestly, he had forgotten the speaker's name at first, and only recognized him when he was introduced by young Copperhead; and then the situation was piquant and amused him, especially the evident confusion and consternation of the culprit when found out. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "I do n't know what he thinks he has done to you,"said Clarence,"I could scarcely make him come in. [/]
[MrCopperhead] He says he is sure you ca n't wish to see him. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "This was two days after the dinner, when Horace Northcote came to leave a respectful card, hoping that he might see Ursula at a door or window. [/]
[Narrator] Clarence had seized upon him and dragged him in, in spite of himself. [/]
[Reginald] "On the contrary, I am very glad to see him,"said Mr. May, with a smile. [/]
[Narrator] He looked at the young Dissenter with a jeer in his eyes. [/]
[Narrator] He liked to punish him, having suddenly perceived that this jeer was much more potent than any serious penalty. [/]
[Reginald] "If he will promise not to slay me, I sha n't quarrel with him." [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May was in such good spirits at this moment that he could afford to joke; his own magnanimity, and the other's confused looks of guilt, overcame his gravity. [/]
[Reginald] "Come back again,"he said, holding out his hand; and though Horace retired for the moment utterly confounded, yet the attractions of the cheerful house overcame, after a while, his sense of humiliation and inappropriateness. [/]
[Narrator] If the injured family had condoned his offence, why should he mind? [/]
[Narrator] and the pleasant girlish friendliness, without any _ arrière pensée _, of Ursula, was enough to have set any man at his ease; the facts of the case being that Mrs. Hurst was away upon a long visit, and that, having no other gossip within the range of her acquaintance, Ursula did not know. [/]
[Narrator] Reginald, who did, had the same sense of magnanimity as his father had, and began to like the society of the congenial yet different spirit which it was so strange to him to find under a guise so unlike his own. [/]
[Reginald] And Northcote, on his side, finding no house to which he could betake himself among those whom Phoebe called"our own people;"found a refuge, which gradually became dearer and dearer to him, at the Parsonage, and in his profound sense of the generosity of the people who had thus received him, felt his own partizanship wax feebler and feebler every day. [/]
[Narrator] He seemed to see the ground cut from under his feet, as he watched the young chaplain at his work. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May, to be sure, was no example of pastoral diligence, but he was a pleasant companion, and had put himself from the first in that position of moral superiority which naturally belongs to an injured person who can forgive heartily and without prejudice. [/]
[Narrator] And Ursula! [/]
[Narrator] He did not venture to call her Ursula, even in the secret depths of his heart. [/]
[Narrator] There a pronoun was enough, as, indeed, incipient Love generally finds it. [/]
[Narrator] She spoke to him, smiled at him in the street; and immediately life became a _ Vita Nuova _ to him. [/]
[Narrator] The young Dissenter was as Dante, and simple Ursula, with her housekeeping books in her hand, became another Beatrice. [/]
[Narrator] It is not every one who has the capacity for this perfect and absorbing sentiment; but Horace Northcote had, and for a long time Ursula was as unconscious of it as heart could desire. [/]
[Narrator] Phoebe's admission to the house had been more simple still. [/]
[Narrator] A girlish fancy on Ursula's part, a fit of good - nature on her father's, and then that secret thread of connection with Tozer which no one knew of, and the coming of Clarence Copperhead, to please whom Mr. May permitted himself to be persuaded to do much; and in addition to all this, her good looks, her pretty manners, her cleverness and the deference she had always shown in the proper quarter. [/]
[Reginald] Mr. May did not enter into the lists with his son, or think of offering himself as a suitor to Phoebe; but he liked to talk to her, and to watch what he called"her little ways,"and to hear her play when Clarence and his violin were otherwise disposed of. [/]
[Phoebe] He was an experienced man, priding himself on a knowledge of human nature, and Phoebe's"little ways"amused him greatly. [/]
[Phoebe] What did she mean? --to"catch"Clarence Copperhead, who would be a great match, or to fascinate Northcote? [/]
[Narrator] Oddly enough Mr. May never thought of Reginald, though that young man showed an eagerness to talk to Phoebe which was more than equal with his own, and had always subjects laid up ready to discuss with her, when he could find the opportunity. [/]
[Narrator] Sometimes he would go up to her in the midst of the little party and broach one of these topics straight on end, without preface or introduction, as which was her favourite play of Shakespeare, and what did she think of the character of King Lear? [/]
[Narrator] It was not very wise, not any wiser than his neighbour was, who made pretty little Ursula into the ideal lady, the most gentle and stately figure in poetry; and yet no doubt there was something in both follies that was a great deal better than wisdom. [/]
[Narrator] The society formed by these two young pairs, with Clarence Copperhead as a heavy floating balance, and Mr. May and Janey -- one philosophical, wise and mistaken; the other sharp - sighted and seeing everything -- as spectators, was very pleasant to the close little coterie themselves, and nobody else got within the charmed circle. [/]
[Narrator] They grew more and more intimate daily, and had a whole vocabulary of domestic jokes and allusions which no one else could understand. [/]
[Narrator] It must be allowed, however, that the outside world was not pleased with this arrangement on either side of the question. [/]
[HoraceNorthcote] The Church people were shocked with the Mays for harbouring Dissenters under any circumstances whatever, and there had not been a Minister at Salem Chapel for a long time so unpopular as Horace Northcote, who was always"engaged"when any of the connection asked him to tea, and preached sermons which went over their heads, and did not remember them when he met them in the street. [/]
[Narrator] Tozer was about the only one of the congregation who stood up for the young man. [/]
[TheOthers] The others thanked Heaven that"he was but tempory,"and on the whole they were right, for certainly he was out of place in his present post. [/]
[Narrator] As for Clarence Copperhead, he led an agreeable life enough among all these undercurrents of feeling, which he did not recognise with any distinctness. [/]
[MrCopperhead] He was comfortable enough, pleased with his own importance, and too obtuse to perceive that he bored his companions; and then he considered himself to be slightly"sweet upon"both the girls. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula was his favourite in the morning, when he embarrassed her much by persistently seeking her company whenever liberated by her father; but Phoebe was the queen of the evening, when he would get his fiddle with an unfailing complacency which drove Reginald frantic. [/]
[Narrator] Whether it was mere good - nature or any warmer impulse, Phoebe was strangely tolerant of these fiddlings, and would go on playing for hours with serene composure, never tired and never impatient. [/]
[Narrator] Yet poor Clarence was not an accompanyist to be coveted. [/]
[Narrator] He was weak in the ear and defective in science, but full of a cheerful confidence which was as good as genius. [/]
[MrCopperhead] "Never mind, Miss Phoebe,"he would say cheerfully, when he had broken down for the twentieth time,"play on and I'll catch you up." [/]
[Narrator] He had thus a series of trysting places in every page or two, which might have been very laughable to an indifferent spectator, but which aggravated the Mays, father and son, to an intolerable extent. [/]
[Narrator] They were the two who suffered. [/]
[Narrator] As for Horace Northcote, who was not a great talker, it was a not disagreeable shield for his silent contemplation of Ursula, and the little things which from time to time he ventured to say to her. [/]
[Narrator] For conversation he had not the thirst which animated Reginald, and Ursula's talk, though lively and natural, was not like Phoebe's; but while the music went on he could sit by her in a state of silent beatitude, now and then saying something to which Ursula replied if she was disposed, or if she was not disposed put aside by a little shake of her head, and smiling glance at the piano. [/]
[Narrator] Sometimes it was simple wilfulness that made her silent; but Northcote set it down to an angelical sweetness which would not wound even the worst of performances by inattention. [/]
[Narrator] They were happy enough sitting there under the shelter of the piano, the young man absorbed in the dreams of a young love, the girl just beginning to realize the adoration which she was receiving, with a timid perception of it -- half - frightened, half - grateful. [/]
[Narrator] She was in spite of herself amused by the idea only half understood, and which she could scarcely believe, that this big grown man, so much more important than herself in everybody's eyes, should show so much respect to a little girl whom her father scolded, whom Reginald sent trotting about on all sorts of errands, and whom Cousin Anne and Cousin Sophy considered a child. [/]
[Narrator] It was very strange, a thing to call forth inextinguishable laughter, and yet with a strange touch of sweetness in it, which almost made her cry in wondering gratitude. [/]
[Narrator] What she thought of him, Ursula did not ask herself; that he should think _ like this _ of her was the bewildering, extraordinary, ridiculous fact that at present filled her girlish head. [/]
[Narrator] But if they were sweet to Northcote, these evenings were the crown of Clarence Copperhead's content and conscious success; he was supremely happy, caressing his fiddle between his cheek and his shoulder, and raising his pale eyes to the ceiling in an ecstasy. [/]
[Narrator] The music, and the audience, and the accompanyist all together were delightful to him. [/]
[Narrator] He could have gone on he felt not only till midnight, but till morning, and so on to midnight again, with short intervals for refreshment. [/]
[Narrator] Every ten minutes or so there occurred a break in the continuity of the strain, and a little dialogue between the performers. [/]
[TheYoungMan] "Ah, yes, I have missed a line; never mind; go on, Miss Phoebe, I will make up to you,"he said. [/]
[Phoebe] "It is those accidentals that have been your ruin,"said Phoebe laughing;"it is a very hard passage, let us turn back and begin again,"and then the audience would laugh, not very sweetly, and (some of them) make acrid observations; but the pianist was good - nature itself, and went back and counted and kept time with her head, and with her hand when she could take it from the piano, until she had triumphantly tided him over the bad passage, or they had come to the point of shipwreck again. [/]
[Narrator] During these labours, Phoebe, who was really a good musician, ought to have suffered horribly; but either she did not, or her good - nature was stronger than her good taste, for she went on serenely, sometimes for hours together, while her old and her young admirers sat secretly cursing (in such ways as are becoming to a clergyman) each in his corner. [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps she had a slight degree of pleasure in the evident power she had over father and son; but it was difficult fully to understand her views at this somewhat bewildering period of her life, in which she was left entirely to her own resources. [/]
[Narrator] She was herself groping a little through paths of uncertain footing, enjoying herself a great deal, but not seeing clearly where it led to, and having no definite purpose, or chart of those unknown countries in her mind. [/]
[Reginald] "How you can go on,"said Reginald, on one of these occasions, having at length managed to seize upon and get her into a corner,"for hours, having your ears sacrificed and your patience tried by these fearful discords, and smile through it, is a mystery which I can not fathom! [/]
[Narrator] If it was only consideration for your audience, that might be enough to move any one -- but yourself--""I do n't seem to feel it so very much myself." [/]
[Narrator] "And yet you are a musician!""Do n't be too hard upon me, Mr May. [/]
[TheYoungMan] I only play -- a little. [/]
[Narrator] I am not like my cousins in the High Street, who are supposed to be very clever at music; and then poor Mr. Copperhead is a very old friend.""Poor Mr. Copperhead! [/]
[Reginald] poor us, you mean, who have to listen -- and you, who choose to play. [/]
[Narrator] ""You are very vindictive,"she said, with a piteous look. [/]
[I] "Why should you be so vindictive? [/]
[Narrator] I do what I can to please my friends, and -- there is no doubt about what poor Clarence likes best; if you were to show me as plainly what you would like--_quite _ plainly, as he does----""Do n't you know?"said Reginald, with glowing eyes. [/]
[TheYoungMan] "Ah, well! [/]
[TheYoungMan] if I may show you plainly -- quite plainly, with the same results, you may be sure not to be left long in doubt. [/]
[TheYoungMan] Talk to me! [/]
[TheYoungMan] it is easier, and not so fatiguing. [/]
[TheYoungMan] Here,"said the young man, placing a chair for her;"he has had your patient services for two hours. [/]
[Narrator] Do only half as much for me.""Ah! [/]

[Phoebe] Well!"said Phoebe, with a laugh and a blush, taking the chair,"I will try, but you must begin; and I can not promise, you know, for a whole hour. [/]
[Narrator] ""After you have given that fellow two! [/]

[Narrator] ""Ah!"cried Reginald, startled; for of course it is needless to say that the idea of any special devotion to his little sister had never entered his mind. [/]
[Narrator] He felt disposed to laugh at first when the idea was suggested to him, but he gave a second look, and fellow - feeling threw a certain enlightenment upon the subject. [/]

[Reginald] ""Why would it not do? [/]

[/]
[Narrator] "Well?"she said at length, softly,"it was talk you said you wanted, Mr. May; but you are not so ready to tune up your violin as Mr. Copperhead, though I wait with my fingers on the piano, so to speak.""I beg your pardon!" [/]

[Phoebe] though, perhaps, she does not want me any more than you do. [/]
[Phoebe] ""Do n't give me up for one moment's distraction; and it was your fault, not mine, for suggesting such a startling idea."Phoebe shook her head, and waved her hand as a parting salutation, and then went across the room to where Ursula was sitting, where Horace Northcote at least found her very much in his way. [/]
[Narrator] She began at once to talk low and earnestly on some subject so interesting that it absorbed both the girls in a way which was very surprising and unpleasant to the young men, neither of whom had been able to interest the one whose attention he was specially anxious to secure half so effectually. [/]
[Narrator] Northcote, from the other side of the table, and Reginald from the other end of the room, gazed and gloomed with discomfited curiosity, wondering what it could be; while Clarence strutted uneasily about the piano, taking up his fiddle now and then, striking a note, and screwing up his strings into concord, with many impatient glances. [/]
[Narrator] But still the girls talked. [/]
[Narrator] Was it about their dresses or some nonsense, or was it a more serious subject, which could thus be discussed without masculine help? [/]
[Narrator] but this matter they never fathomed, nor have they found out till this hour. CHAPTER XXXI. [/]
[Narrator] SOCIETY. [/]
[Narrator] Notwithstanding such little social crosses, however, the society at the Parsonage, as thus constituted, was very agreeable. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. May, though he had his faults, was careful of his daughter. [/]
[Narrator] He sat in the drawing - room every evening till she retired, on the nights their visitors came, and even when it was Clarence only who remained, an inmate of the house, and free to go and come as he pleased. [/]
[Narrator] Ursula, he felt, must not be left alone, and though it is uncertain whether she fully appreciated the care he took of her, this point in his character is worth noting. [/]
[Narrator] When the young party went out together, to skate, for instance, as they did, for several merry days, Reginald and Janey were, he considered, sufficient guardians for their sister. [/]
[Ursula] Phoebe had no chaperon--"Unless you will take that serious office upon you, Ursula,"she said, shrugging her shoulders prettily; but she only went once or twice, so well was she able, even when the temptation was strongest, to exercise self - denial, and show her perfect power of self - guidance. [/]
[Ursula] As for old Tozer and his wife, the idea of a chaperon never entered their homely head. [/]
[Ursula] Such articles are unnecessary in the lower levels of society. [/]
[Ursula] They were anxious that their child should enjoy herself, and could not understand the reason of her staying at home on a bright frosty day, when the Mays came to the door in a body to fetch her. [/]
[TheOldLady] "No, if they'd have gone down on their knees, nor if I had gone down on mine, would that girl have left me,"cried the old lady, with tears in her eyes. [/]
[TheOldLady] "She do behave beautiful to her old granny. [/]
